# Indian Dance - Live Learning Session

> **Learner-first lock:** This session teaches you to hear a claim about an Indian dance as five separable things - a **grammar** (the codified vocabulary of movement, gesture and emotion that a form uses), a **repertoire** (the named sequence of items a performance actually moves through), a **carrier** (the community, lineage, monastery or institution that holds and transmits the skill), an **occasion** (the temple, court, festival, harvest, funeral, initiation or proscenium stage at which it happens), and a **status** (what a named body has formally said about it, and when). It runs from the treatise that supplied the vocabulary to the recognition decisions that are still being taken, and it keeps four habits throughout: name the discriminating feature rather than the shared one, keep a form's social history separate from the date of its recognition, refuse every "unbroken since antiquity" sentence, and never rank a living folk tradition below a recognised classical one. Music as a system belongs to the music topic; dramaturgy, puppetry and the Sanskrit theatre belong to the theatre topic; sculpture belongs to the sculpture topic; the social history of hereditary performing communities belongs to the society papers; changing award and heritage policy belongs to the heritage topic. What is owned here is the **dance grammar, the codified forms, the living regional and ritual forms, their carriers and occasions, and the recognition vocabulary that describes them**.

## How to read this session

Work down the lessons in order. Each begins with the question a learner actually has at that point, draws or tabulates before it names, and hands over the technical word only after the idea it names has been established. Four habits are built deliberately, because they are the four places this topic is lost.

1. **Answer an identification demand with the feature no other form shares.** Devotional Vaishnava theme fits Manipuri, Sattriya and Odissi equally. The *pung* drum, the *sattra* and the mahari-gotipua history do not. Almost every objective failure in this topic is a candidate reaching for a shared feature when a discriminating one was available.
2. **Keep three clocks apart.** The clock of a text, the clock of a social practice, and the clock of an institutional decision run at different speeds. A form can be old as practice, first named in a treatise at one date, disrupted at another, reconstructed at a third and recognised at a fourth. Collapsing those four into "ancient tradition" costs marks in every directive that contains the word *discuss*.
3. **Do not let "classical" become a compliment.** In this topic *classical* is an administrative category applied by a named body in the twentieth century. It is not a verdict on beauty, antiquity, complexity or worth. A session that treats folk dance as the lower half of a ladder has already made the error the examiner is testing for.
4. **Do not decorate.** Costume colours, elemental symbolism and proponent names carry marks only when the source records them. Where this session does not state a costume detail, an award or a guru's attribution, that silence is deliberate and should be reproduced in your answer.

Everything is drawn in text. No photograph, video or notation plate is needed to follow any lesson here.

|---:|---|---|
| 1 | What does this topic own, and what five questions should I ask of any claim about an Indian dance? | Foundation |
| 2 | Where did the vocabulary come from, and what does it mean to call dance a "complete art"? | Foundation |
| 3 | Lasya and Tandava: two qualities that are constantly mistaken for two genders | Foundation |
| 4 | Nritta, Natya and Nritya: the three-way distinction that decides most dance questions | Foundation |
| 5 | Abhinaya, bhava and rasa: how an emotion is made transmissible | Foundation |
| 6 | The body as vocabulary: hasta, mudra, karana, adavu - and the disputed number 108 | Core |
| 7 | Time, stage and recital: how a performance is organised once the vocabulary exists | Core |
| 8 | Who says a dance is "classical"? Three separate recognition systems, and what each one measures | Core |
| 9 | Transmission: guru-shishya parampara, sampradaya, gharana - and what the sources do not record | Core |
| 10 | Bharatanatyam: the form whose history is also a disruption | Core |
| 11 | Kuchipudi: the itinerant dance-drama that became a solo concert form | Core |
| 12 | Kathakali: what happens when gesture has to carry an entire drama | Core |
| 13 | Mohiniyattam: the form defined by what it does not do | Core |
| 14 | Kathak: one name, four gharanas, and three social settings | Core |
| 15 | Odissi: torso, sculpture and a reconstructed lineage | Core |
| 16 | Manipuri: devotion, restraint and the drum at the centre | Core |
| 17 | Sattriya: a dance form whose institution is a monastery | Core |
| 18 | Chhau and Gaudiya Nritya: the frontier where classification is actually decided | Core |
| 19 | Reading a folk dance by function - Part A: ritual embodiment, martial occasion, agrarian calendar | Core |
| 20 | Reading a folk dance by function - Part B: property, formation, impersonation and community carriers | Core |
| 21 | Shared vocabulary, firm boundaries: dance with sculpture, music, theatre and puppetry | Advanced |
| 22 | Reform, revival and institutions: who was displaced, who was named, and what was rebuilt | Advanced |
| 23 | Attribution, chronology, region and answer construction | Advanced |


**Boundary map.** The music topic owns raga, tala theory, the two classical systems, the musical lineage-school and instruments as sound-producing objects; here an instrument appears only as the accompaniment that identifies a dance, and a musical genre appears only where a dance is defined by it. The theatre and puppetry topic owns Sanskrit dramaturgy, Koodiyattam as theatre, folk theatre and every puppet tradition; here a dance-drama is owned when its dominant grammar is danced movement, and it is routed out when its dominant grammar is spoken or manipulated text. The sculpture topic owns iconography, material and workshop history; here sculpture appears only as evidence about movement and only with its evidential limit attached. The religion and philosophy topics own Vaishnava and Bhakti doctrine and the full aesthetic theory of rasa; here rasa is performance vocabulary and Vaishnavism is a patronage and repertoire fact. The society papers own the devadasi system as a social-reform question; here the art form and its technique are owned, the community is named and its displacement is stated, and the reform debate itself is not adjudicated. The heritage and cultural-policy topic owns award administration and every changeable status total; this session states exactly one current status record, in Lesson 8, and asserts nothing beyond it.

### Visual: five questions, and the exact error each one prevents

Read the diagram left to right and notice its shape. The first five questions are answerable from evidence. The sixth is not, and it is placed last and marked as answering nothing, because an answer that opens "Indian dance is a divine and eternal art" has spent its first sentence in the only column that carries no marks.

### 1. The exact scope, stated once

This topic owns the **codified vocabulary of Indian dance, the recognised classical forms, the living regional, tribal and ritual forms, the carriers and occasions that sustain them, and the recognition vocabulary used to describe them**. Stated as a checklist of what must be answerable by the end of this session:

- the treatise foundation: the *Natyashastra*, the Natya Veda conception, and the claim that dance is a composite art;
- the two aspects, *Lasya* and *Tandava*, as qualities rather than as gender rules;
- the three elements, *Nritta*, *Natya* and *Nritya*, and the treatise that separates them;
- the emotional apparatus: *bhava*, *abhinaya*, the nine *rasas*, and *Nayaka-Nayika bhava*;
- the body vocabulary: *hasta* and *mudra*, *karana*, *adavu*, and the named signature postures;
- the organisation of a performance: tempo, rhythmic recitation, stage setting and named recital order;
- the three recognition systems and exactly what each measures;
- transmission: *guru-shishya parampara*, *sampradaya*, the Kathak *gharanas*, and the monastic *sattra*;
- all eight forms recognised by the national academy, each with origin, repertoire, costume, music, revival and its nearest confusion;
- Chhau in full, together with the ninth-form question and a claimed-but-unrecognised Bengal form;
- the folk, tribal, ritual, martial, agrarian and life-cycle field, read by function rather than by state label;
- the evidential relationship between dance and sculpture, and the limit of that evidence;
- reform, decline, reconstruction, patronage change and modern institutions;
- the attribution and chronology discipline that keeps an answer defensible.

### 2. The five questions, each with its technical vocabulary

**Question one - grammar.** What codified vocabulary does the form use? Every classical form in this topic answers with the same building blocks - *nritta*, *nritya*, *natya*, *abhinaya*, *rasa*, *hasta* - because they all descend from a shared treatise tradition. The differences appear in emphasis: one form foregrounds facial expression, another veils the face; one thumps the floor, another is defined by the absence of thumping. Grammar is therefore where identification questions are actually won.

**Question two - repertoire.** What named items does a recital move through, in what order? *Alarippu, Jatiswaram, Shabdam, Varnam, Padam, Jawali, Thillana* is not a list of techniques; it is an order of events. *Mangalacharan, Batu nritya, Pallavi, Tharijham, Moksha* is a different order of events. A candidate who knows grammar but not repertoire can describe a form and cannot identify it.

**Question three - carrier.** Who holds the skill and passes it on? The answer varies sharply across this topic, and that variation is itself examinable: a hereditary temple-attached performer, a travelling troupe, an all-male troupe, a household lineage, a celibate monk in a monastery, a named community such as the Chakyar or the Nalike, or a twentieth-century institution. The rule that unites them is *guru-shishya parampara*: transfer of knowledge through a guru who passes on a *sampradaya*.

**Question four - occasion.** Where, and on what occasion, does it happen? A temple, a court, an open-air theatre lit by a brass lamp, a monastery's daily ritual, the first day of sowing, the monsoon, a wedding, a harvest, a festival of lights, a proscenium stage. Occasion is the single most neglected question in this topic and the one that makes a folk-dance answer analytical instead of a list.

**Question five - status.** Who has formally said what about it, and when? Three bodies matter and they are not interchangeable. This is developed fully in Lesson 8; fix now only that recognition is dated, institutional and revisable, and that it is never evidence about antiquity.

### 3. The classification vocabulary, fixed here

These seven terms recur constantly. Each is developed properly in its own lesson; fix the working sense now so that later lessons can use them without pausing.

| Term | Working definition, fixed here |
|---|---|
| **Natya Veda** | The conception of drama-dance as a "fifth Veda" formed by combining four elements drawn from the four Vedas: *Paathya* (words), *Abhinaya* (gestures), *Geet* (music) and *Rasa* (emotions) |
| **Lasya** | The aspect of dance denoting grace, and associated with *bhava*, *rasa* and *abhinaya*; described as symbolic of the feminine features of the art form |
| **Tandava** | The aspect symbolic of the male features, with greater emphasis on rhythm and movement |
| **Nritta** | Basic dance steps performed rhythmically and devoid of expression or mood |
| **Natya** | Dramatic representation - the story elaborated through the recital |
| **Nritya** | Sentiment and emotion evoked through dance, including mime and the methods of expression, of which *mudras* are the principal one |
| **Rasa** | The emotion a performance is designed to produce; nine are named, and the same nine-fold scheme recurs in the music and sculpture topics |

Two of these are already enough to prevent a standard failure. If *Nritta* is expressionless rhythmic movement and *Nritya* is expression, then "the dancer performed nritta with great emotional depth" is a confused sentence: the moment expression enters, the element has changed.

### 4. The three clocks this topic keeps apart

An examiner can test a date here in three different senses, and merging them is expensive.

1. **The clock of a text.** The *Natyashastra* and the *Abhinaya Darpana* are datable works, each of which fixed a concept. This clock is the safest in the topic, and even here the safe formulation is a broad range rather than a single year.
2. **The clock of a practice.** When was a form practised, by whom, under whose patronage, and when did that patronage change? The Jain king Kharavela's patronage of the *maharis*, the Vijayanagara and Golconda patronage of Kuchipudi, the Travancore patronage of Mohiniyattam, the breakdown of the feudal set-up that hurt Kathakali - this clock is social and regional, and it runs independently of the text clock.
3. **The clock of an institution.** When was a form reconstructed, taught in a school, recognised by a national academy or inscribed on an international list? This clock begins in the twentieth century. A form's history does not begin when this clock starts, and this is the single most frequently tested confusion in the topic.

### 5. The boundaries, stated so they never have to be re-argued

- **Music** owns raga, tala theory, the Hindustani and Carnatic systems, the musical lineage-school and instruments as sound-producing objects. Here, an instrument is named when it identifies a dance - the *pung* for Manipuri, *chenda* and *maddala* for Kathakali, *khol* for Sattriya, *dholki* for Lavani, *Been* for Kalbelia - and a musical system is named when it identifies a form, as Hindustani accompaniment does for Odissi and Carnatic accompaniment for Kuchipudi.
- **Theatre and puppetry** own dramaturgy, the Sanskrit theatre and every puppet form. A dance-drama is owned here when danced movement is its dominant grammar, which is why Kathakali, Kuchipudi and the Sattriya *Ankia Naat* are taught here while the Sanskrit theatre tradition to which Kathakali is recorded as closely related is routed out.
- **Sculpture** owns iconography and workshop history. Here, sculpture appears twice: as evidence that an idiom of movement existed, and as a cautionary limit on what that evidence can prove.
- **Religion and philosophy** own Vaishnava and Bhakti doctrine and the full theory of *rasa* as aesthetics. Here, Vaishnavism appears as the historical force that changed who was allowed to perform - a repertoire and patronage fact - and *rasa* appears as performance vocabulary.
- **Society** owns the devadasi system as a question of gender, caste and reform. Here the resulting art form and its technique are owned; the community is named, its displacement is stated, and the reform debate itself is not adjudicated.
- **Heritage and cultural policy** own award administration, conservation machinery and every changing total. This session cites exactly one dated current record, in Lesson 8, and asserts nothing beyond it.

### Examination use

Four things in this lesson are directly usable. The **five-question frame** converts an unstructured "write about Indian dance" demand into five evidenced paragraphs, and converts a comparison demand into the same frame run twice. The **three clocks** supply the qualification sentence that separates a first-class answer from a competent one. The **seven-term table** is the minimum vocabulary below which no dance answer can be written. The **boundary map** prevents the commonest structural error in this topic - an answer on dance that spends a third of its length on music theory or temple architecture.

### Traps introduced here

- Opening a dance answer with praise rather than with a definition or a frame.
- Using "mudra" as a general word for any dance movement, when it denotes a specific class of codified gesture.
- Treating *nritta*, *nritya* and *natya* as three words for dance.
- Assuming that the state a form is associated with is the most discriminating thing about it.
- Allowing the institutional clock to become the history of the practice.

### Revision notes

1. This topic owns **grammar, repertoire, carrier, occasion and status**; value judgements are not owned by anyone.
2. **Natya Veda** = a fifth Veda formed from *Paathya* (words), *Abhinaya* (gestures), *Geet* (music) and *Rasa* (emotions).
3. **Lasya** = grace, linked to *bhava*, *rasa* and *abhinaya*, described as symbolic of feminine features; **Tandava** = symbolic of male features, emphasising rhythm and movement.
4. **Nritta** = rhythmic steps devoid of expression; **Natya** = dramatic representation; **Nritya** = sentiment and emotion, including mime and *mudras*.
5. Nine **rasas** are shared with the music and sculpture topics.
6. Three clocks: **text**, **practice**, **institution**. They are independent, and the third begins only in the twentieth century.
7. The carrier varies by form: hereditary temple performer, travelling troupe, all-male troupe, household lineage, monastic *Bhokot*, named community, modern institution.
8. Occasion varies by form: temple, court, open-air theatre, monastery ritual, sowing, monsoon, harvest, wedding, festival, proscenium.
9. Boundaries: music owns the system; theatre owns dramaturgy; sculpture owns iconography; society owns the reform debate; heritage policy owns changing status totals.
10. A dance-drama is classified here by its **dominant grammar**, not by the mere presence of drama.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Identify what a dance answer owns and what it must route elsewhere, and explain why stating that boundary improves the answer. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

A dance answer owns five things: the codified vocabulary a form uses, the named items of its repertoire, the community or institution that transmits it, the occasion on which it is performed, and what a named body has formally said about it.

The rest routes out. Music owns raga and tala theory; an instrument appears here only when it identifies a form - the *pung*, the *khol*, *chenda* and *maddala*. Theatre owns dramaturgy, so a dance-drama is placed by its dominant grammar. Sculpture owns iconography; carving evidences a movement idiom, with a stated limit. Society owns the devadasi reform debate; the community is named, the debate is not settled here. Heritage policy owns totals and awards.

Stating the boundary matters because the commonest structural failure in this topic is a dance answer that spends a third of its length on architecture or raga theory.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the five owned questions named 3 + four routings each with a concrete instance 5 + the structural reason for stating the boundary 2.**

### Concept check

**Question:** A statement reads: "Kathakali is an ancient Kerala dance recognised by the Sangeet Natak Akademi as one of India's eight classical dance forms, and its elaborate make-up proves its temple origin." Using the five-question frame and the three clocks, identify everything in this sentence that a careful answer would have to separate.

**Model answer:** The sentence fuses four independent claims. **Grammar and occasion are collapsed:** elaborate make-up belongs to the grammar of the form and says nothing by itself about where it was first performed, so "make-up proves temple origin" is an inference the evidence does not carry. **The practice clock and the institutional clock are merged:** the recorded history of Kathakali runs from two temple dance-dramas, Ramanattam and Krishnattam, through patronage by feudal lords, a decline when that set-up broke down, and a twentieth-century revival - none of which is dated by a national academy's classification decision, which belongs to a different clock entirely. **"Ancient" is doing unearned work:** the recorded chain is specific and traceable, and naming that chain is worth more than the adjective. Finally, **the carrier is missing altogether** - that the form is generally an all-male troupe performance is a stronger identification fact than any of the four claims actually made. A careful answer would say: a Kerala dance-drama derived from earlier temple dance-dramas, carried by all-male troupes, sustained by feudal patronage, disrupted when that patronage collapsed, revived in the twentieth century, and subsequently classified by a national academy - five statements, each resting on its own evidence.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that a formal recognition is a historical claim. Recognition records an administrative decision at a date; it is silent about how old the practice is, how continuous it has been, and who used to perform it. A sentence that runs "recognised as classical, and therefore ancient" has invented a bridge between two clocks that do not touch.

### Visual: how the Natya Veda is assembled, and what each contribution does

```text

       Rig             Yajur             Sama            Atharva
        |                 |                |                |
        v                 v                v                v
    PAATHYA           ABHINAYA           GEET             RASA
    (words)          (gestures)         (music)        (emotions)
   the text a        the body's       the sound the    what the
   performance       carrying of      performance      audience is
   speaks or sings   meaning          moves in         meant to feel
        |                 |                |                |
        +--------+--------+--------+-------+
                          |
                     NATYA VEDA
              created as a "fifth Veda"
                          |
        +-----------------+------------------+
        |                                    |
   NATYA = an amalgamation of          DANCE described as a
   dance + drama + music               COMPLETE ART uniting
                                       music, sculpture,
                                       poetry and drama
                          |
                          v
   CONSEQUENCE FOR THE EXAM: Indian dance is analysed through CATEGORIES
   (nritta, nritya, natya, abhinaya, rasa) and not through STEPS
```

The diagram is not decoration. Read it as an argument about method. If the four contributions are word, gesture, music and emotion, then a dance tradition built on this conception cannot be described adequately by describing movement alone - and that is exactly why every classical form in this session has a *text* (Attakkatha, Borgeet, Gita Govinda), a *gesture system*, an *accompaniment* and a *named emotional target*. The frame tells you what to look for in every form that follows.

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Most candidates meet the *Natyashastra* as a name to be dropped: "the ancient treatise on dance by Bharata". That is worth almost nothing, because every other candidate writes it. The examinable content is not the name but four specific things: what the text is said to combine, what it is said to describe, what it claims dance *is*, and how carefully its date must be handled.

### 2. The Natya Veda derivation, stated exactly

According to Bharata's *Natya Shastra*, Lord Brahma created the **Natya Veda** as the fifth Veda by combining elements of the four Vedas:

| Element | Meaning | Drawn from |
|---|---|---|
| **Paathya** | words | Rig |
| **Abhinaya** | gestures | Yajur |
| **Geet** | music | Sama |
| **Rasa** | emotions | Atharva |

The result is that **Natya** is an amalgamation of dance, drama and music. Two consequences follow immediately and both are examinable.

First, the four-element derivation is the **standard opening frame** for any question that asks about the contribution of the *Natyashastra*. A candidate who writes the four elements with their Vedic attributions has given the examiner something specific in the first three lines.

Second, the derivation explains why the later vocabulary looks the way it does. *Abhinaya* is not an optional embellishment in this scheme; it is one of the four constituent contributions. *Rasa* is not a critic's word applied from outside; it is built into the conception of what the performance is for.

### 3. What the treatise is recorded as containing

The first formal mention of dance appears in Bharata's *Natya Shastra*, described as a detailed treatise on classical dance. It describes:

- **techniques** - how movement is executed;
- **postures** - how the body is held;
- **emotions** - what is to be conveyed;
- **stagecraft** - how the performance space is organised;
- **audiences** - who is watching and how they receive it.

That last item is more interesting than it looks. A treatise that discusses audiences is a treatise that treats performance as a transaction, not as a private devotion - which is precisely why the tradition needed a theory of *rasa* at all. If nobody is receiving, there is nothing for the emotion to be transmitted to.

### 4. The composite-art claim, and what it licenses

The text calls dance a **"complete art" that unites music, sculpture, poetry and drama**. This single sentence does more analytical work than any other sentence in the topic, and it should be quoted in substance in any Mains answer on the *Natyashastra*.

It licenses three moves:

1. **It authorises the dance-sculpture reading method.** Because the root text itself unites dance and sculpture, the posture and gesture vocabulary defined for dance is directly applicable to reading a dynamic sculptural figure. This is not an analogy imposed by a modern art critic; it is a connection the source conception makes. Lesson 21 develops this fully, including its limit.
2. **It authorises the dance-music and dance-poetry linkages.** A dance form's accompaniment and its lyric text are not background detail; on this conception they are constituents. That is why the *Gita Govinda* matters for Odissi and the *Borgeet* for Sattriya.
3. **It explains the analytical categories.** Because performance is conceived as one composite art, the tradition analyses it by *aspects* and *elements* - *lasya* and *tandava*, *nritta*, *nritya* and *natya*, *abhinaya* and *rasa* - rather than by choreographic steps. A candidate who lists steps and a candidate who uses categories are answering two different questions, and only the second one is the question asked.

### 5. Sacred and social - a single sentence that prevents a large error

In the Indian tradition, dance is recorded as **both sacred and social**. Keep both halves. A candidate who retains only the sacred half produces the standard temple-to-stage narrative that Lesson 22 dismantles; a candidate who retains only the social half cannot explain why a Sattriya performance is part of a monk's daily ritual or why Theyyam performers embody deities. The whole field of this session - courts, sowing seasons, weddings, monasteries, temples, harvest fairs, proscenium stages - is only intelligible if both halves are held together.

### 6. The Mohenjo-daro bronze: what it does and does not establish

The famous bronze figure from Mohenjo-daro, conventionally called the "Indian Dancing Girl" and dated around 2500 BCE, is recorded as **indicating early familiarity with dance gestures and body movements**.

Read that formulation carefully, because it is a model of evidential discipline and the examiner has used exactly this kind of distinction before.

- What it supports: that an idiom of bodily movement was known, valued and worth representing at that date.
- What it does not support: that any named form, repertoire, treatise category or choreography existed then. The object is silent about grammar, repertoire, carrier and occasion.

The correct exam sentence is therefore "an early indication of familiarity with dance gesture and movement", not "proof that Indian classical dance is five thousand years old".

### 7. Dating discipline for the two treatises

Two dating brackets circulate for the foundational texts, and both are printed in standard preparation material:

| Text | Attribution | Bracket as commonly printed | How to write it |
|---|---|---|---|
| *Natya Shastra* | Bharata | 200 BC - 200 AD | Write the range, or write "a layered text conventionally attributed to Bharata, broadly dated across the late centuries BCE and early centuries CE" |
| *Abhinaya Darpana* | Nandikeshvara | 5th - 4th century BC | Write the range as printed and do not convert it into a single year |

⚠️ **Handle this with care rather than with confidence.** The *Natyashastra* is best treated as a **layered** work - a text that accumulated material over time - which is why a single composition year cannot be defended and why a range is the safe formulation. The printed bracket for the *Abhinaya Darpana* would place it earlier than the *Natyashastra*'s bracket, which is itself a reason to quote the range as given rather than to build a chronological argument on it. In an answer, the safest and most creditable sentence names the attribution, gives the range, and says that the text is layered. It is not a weakness to write that; it is the difference between a candidate who has read and a candidate who has memorised.

❌ Do not invent a precise composition date for either text. ❌ Do not write that Bharata "wrote" the *Natyashastra* as a single authored book if the question invites a chronological claim; "conventionally attributed to" is both safer and more accurate.

### Examination use

For Prelims, four things here are directly testable: the four constituent elements with their Vedic attributions; the identification of the *Natyashastra* as the first formal mention of dance; the "complete art" formulation and the four arts it unites; and the conventional identification and dating range of the *Abhinaya Darpana* with Nandikeshvara.

For Mains, this lesson supplies the opening paragraph of almost every dance answer. A demand of the form *discuss the contribution of the Natya Shastra to Indian dance* is failed by "it is an ancient text on dance" and is passed by: the four-element derivation, the five recorded contents, the composite-art claim, and the consequence that Indian dance is analysed by category rather than by step. A demand that links dance to sculpture is opened by the composite-art sentence, because that sentence is the licence for the whole comparison.

### Traps introduced here

- Supplying a single precise year for the *Natyashastra* or the *Abhinaya Darpana*.
- Attributing *Paathya*, *Abhinaya*, *Geet* and *Rasa* to the wrong Vedas, which is a standard four-pair matching item waiting to be set.
- Treating the composite-art claim as poetic praise rather than as a method statement with consequences.
- Reading the Mohenjo-daro bronze as proof of a continuous classical tradition.
- Dropping the "social" half of "sacred and social" and producing a purely temple-centred narrative.

### Revision notes

1. **Natya Veda** = fifth Veda created by combining **Paathya (words, Rig) + Abhinaya (gestures, Yajur) + Geet (music, Sama) + Rasa (emotions, Atharva)**.
2. **Natya** is therefore an **amalgamation of dance, drama and music**.
3. The **first formal mention of dance** is in Bharata's *Natya Shastra*.
4. The treatise describes **techniques, postures, emotions, stagecraft and audiences**.
5. It calls dance a **"complete art" uniting music, sculpture, poetry and drama** - the licence for the dance-sculpture reading method.
6. Dance in the Indian tradition is **both sacred and social**; keep both halves.
7. The **Mohenjo-daro bronze figure (c. 2500 BCE)** indicates **early familiarity with dance gestures and body movements** - an idiom, not a form.
8. *Natya Shastra* bracket as printed: **200 BC - 200 AD**; treat as **layered**, never as a single year.
9. *Abhinaya Darpana*, **Nandikeshvara**, bracket as printed: **5th - 4th century BC**.
10. Consequence for method: analyse Indian dance by **categories**, not by steps.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**The Natya Shastra is described as a treatise on techniques, postures, emotions, stagecraft and audiences. Examine the analytical significance of the last of these. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The inclusion of audiences is the most consequential item on that list, because it converts performance from private devotion into a transaction that can succeed or fail.

Three consequences follow. First, it explains why the tradition needs a theory of **rasa** at all. If the object of a performance is an emotion produced in someone, there must be a vocabulary for naming what is to be produced, and nine such targets are named. Emotion in the performer would require no such apparatus; emotion in a receiver does.

Second, it explains the scale of codification. A specified effect must be reachable reliably, across performers and generations, and several of the named targets are close neighbours - sorrow with compassion, horror beside wonder. Reliability at that resolution demands a fine-grained inventory, which is what the gesture, movement-unit and whole-body scales supply.

Third, it explains the shape of a recital. If an audience must be brought to the emotional target, the sequence will teach before it loads meaning - which is what the recorded orders do, opening with simple steps, establishing pure movement, then admitting words, then fusing story with rhythm and melody.

A qualification. The same treatise describes techniques, postures and stagecraft, so the audience clause is one constituent among several and should not carry the whole account.

Verdict: a treatise that discusses audiences is a treatise that treats performance as communication, and every technical feature of Indian dance taught afterwards is intelligible as a solution to that problem.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the transactional reading stated at the outset 2 + the rasa consequence argued rather than asserted 4 + the codification consequence with the close-neighbour evidence 4 + the recital-shape consequence with its order 3 + a qualification restoring the other constituents 1 + verdict 1.**

### Concept check

**Question:** Why does the sentence "dance is a complete art uniting music, sculpture, poetry and drama" matter more to an examiner than the sentence "the Natya Shastra is the oldest treatise on Indian dance"?

**Model answer:** Because the first sentence is a **method claim** and the second is only a **label**. The composite-art formulation tells you how the tradition itself proposes that performance should be analysed - as one art with several constituents - and three examinable consequences follow from it. It explains why the analytical vocabulary is built from aspects and elements rather than from steps, so a candidate who lists movements is answering a question nobody asked. It licenses the cross-reading of temple sculpture through dance categories, because the union of the two arts is asserted by the source conception rather than imposed by a later critic. And it predicts the internal structure of every form taught later in this session: each has a text, a gesture system, an accompaniment and an emotional target, because the conception says a performance is made of exactly those things. The second sentence, by contrast, generates no further reasoning; it can only be asserted, and once asserted the answer must start again.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating "complete art" as an ornamental compliment to Indian dance. It is a structural statement with testable consequences. The proof that it is structural rather than decorative is that you can use it to predict what you will find in a form you have not yet studied - and, in Lesson 21, to justify and then to limit the use of sculpture as evidence about movement.

## Lesson 3 - Lasya and Tandava: two qualities, routinely mistaken for two genders

Progress: 3/23 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: the two basic aspects of Indian classical dance, what each is associated with, why the gender reading fails, and how the distinction is actually used to describe a form

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the Aspects of Dance section, and every per-form statement in which the balance of the two aspects is recorded
CA search: "Not applicable - conceptual distinction lesson with no genuine current linkage"
CA found: None found; none claimed
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the two aspects as a balance, not as a wall

```text
   THE TWO BASIC ASPECTS OF INDIAN CLASSICAL DANCE (per the Natya Shastra)

   LASYA  <--------------------- balance --------------------->  TANDAVA
   grace                                                    rhythm, movement
   bhava, rasa, abhinaya                                    emphasis on the
   symbolic of the feminine                                 male features
   features of the art form                                 of the art form

   WHERE THE EIGHT FORMS ARE RECORDED TO SIT
   |------------------------------------------------------------------|
   Mohiniyattam      Manipuri        Bharatanatyam      Kuchipudi
   Lasya dominant    both present,   equal emphasis     both important
                     Lasya           on Tandava
                     emphasised      and Lasya
                                     Sattriya: combines both Lasya and
                                     Tandava elements
   |------------------------------------------------------------------|
   READ THE AXIS, NOT THE ENDS. No form is recorded as pure Tandava, and
   no form is recorded as pure Lasya. The examinable fact is the BALANCE.
```

### 1. The learner's actual problem

The pair *Lasya* and *Tandava* is usually memorised as "feminine dance and masculine dance", and that memorisation produces two errors that are both directly examinable. The first is that a performer's sex determines the aspect. The second is that a form is either one or the other. Both fail against the sources, and the sources fail them in a very specific way: they describe the two aspects, and then they describe individual forms by the *proportion* in which the two are present.

### 2. The two aspects, stated exactly

As per the *Natya Shastra*, there are **two basic aspects of Indian classical dance**.

| Aspect | What it denotes | What it is symbolic of |
|---|---|---|
| **Lasya** | grace; associated with **bhava**, **rasa** and **abhinaya** | the feminine features of dance as an art form |
| **Tandava** | greater emphasis on **rhythm** and **movement** | the male aspects of dance |

Notice the exact wording of the second column. *Lasya* is bound to the **expressive apparatus** - emotion, the emotional target, and the art of conveying it. *Tandava* is bound to the **kinetic apparatus** - the beat and the movement. That is the analytically useful distinction, and it survives even if the gender vocabulary is set aside entirely.

### 3. Why the gender reading fails, in the sources' own terms

The vocabulary is gendered - the sources say "symbolic of the feminine features" and "symbolic of the male aspects". But *symbolic of* is not *performed by*, and the per-form record proves the point four times over:

- **Bharatanatyam** gives **equal emphasis to both the Tandava and the Lasya aspects**, and its history includes a period limited to solo female dancers followed by its later popularity among men and groups. Same form, same two aspects, changing performer demography.
- **Mohiniyattam** has the **Lasya aspect dominant** and is mainly performed by female dancers - and it is also recorded as combining the grace of Bharatanatyam with **the vigour of Kathakali**, which is the form of the topic most associated with the kinetic.
- **Manipuri** incorporates **both Tandava and Lasya**, with emphasis on the latter, and is performed generally by female dancers - while its central deity-theme and its pung-driven rhythmic apparatus are anything but static.
- **Sattriya** combines **both Lasya and Tandava elements** and is generally performed **by male monks**. A form performed by men that explicitly carries the aspect labelled feminine is by itself sufficient to destroy the gender-exclusive reading.

⚠️ The analytically honest statement is therefore: *Lasya* and *Tandava* are **qualities present in varying emphasis within dance generally**, carrying a traditional gendered symbolism, and they are not a rule about who may perform what.

### 4. How the distinction is actually used

Once the aspects are understood as a balance, they become a **description tool** rather than a label, and this is what an examiner rewards:

1. **As a discriminator between neighbouring forms.** The single sharpest use in this topic is the Kerala pair: Mohiniyattam is dominated by Lasya and is marked by the **absence of thumping of footsteps**, while Kathakali is the vigorous dance-drama whose vigour Mohiniyattam is recorded as borrowing. Two forms, one state, opposite positions on the axis.
2. **As a way of describing what a recital does over time.** A Bharatanatyam recital moves from an opening item of simple steps, through a pure-movement item, into expressive items, and closes with pure dance of exuberant movement and intricate rhythmic variation. The balance of the two aspects is not fixed across a performance; it shifts item by item.
3. **As the bridge to the sculpture topic.** *Tandava* and *Lasya* are among the small set of terms that can be applied directly to reading a dynamic sculptural figure, because they describe quality of movement rather than a particular choreography.

### 5. The Tandava that is not the aspect

One caution before leaving this lesson. The word *Tandava* also names specific **dance items and traditions** elsewhere in this session - the Telangana warrior dance **Perini**, recorded as performed in honour of Lord Shiva and depicted in sculpture at a named temple, is commonly encountered under a Tandava-bearing title. The *aspect* and a *named dance* that shares the word are not the same thing, and an examiner who names a specific item is not asking about the aspect. Read the question for which of the two is being tested; if the stem names a region, a temple, a dynasty or a performer, the item is being tested, not the aspect.

### Examination use

For Prelims, the association list is the testable core: Lasya with grace, bhava, rasa and abhinaya; Tandava with rhythm and movement; and the recorded balance for Bharatanatyam (equal), Mohiniyattam (Lasya dominant), Manipuri (both, Lasya emphasised) and Sattriya (both combined). Matching items in this topic are frequently built from exactly these pairs.

For Mains, this lesson supplies the qualification sentence in any answer that touches gender and performance: the aspects carry a gendered symbolism but do not distribute performance by sex, and the record of a male monastic tradition carrying the Lasya element is the cleanest available evidence for that claim. It also supplies a precise, non-decorative way to describe a form in one line, which is exactly what a ten-mark identification demand rewards.

### Traps introduced here

- Writing that Tandava is danced by men and Lasya by women.
- Assigning a form wholly to one aspect when the record gives a balance.
- Forgetting that Mohiniyattam is recorded as combining Bharatanatyam's grace **with Kathakali's vigour**, which makes a pure-Lasya description incomplete.
- Confusing the *aspect* Tandava with a **named dance** that carries the word in its title.
- Treating the aspects as aesthetic praise rather than as a description of where a form sits on an axis.

### Revision notes

1. Two basic aspects per the *Natya Shastra*: **Lasya** and **Tandava**.
2. **Lasya** = grace; denotes **bhava, rasa and abhinaya**; symbolic of the **feminine** features of the art form.
3. **Tandava** = symbolic of the **male** aspects; greater emphasis on **rhythm and movement**.
4. **Bharatanatyam**: **equal emphasis** on both, with major emphasis on *mudras*.
5. **Mohiniyattam**: **Lasya dominant**; combines Bharatanatyam's grace with **Kathakali's vigour**; marked **absence of thumping**.
6. **Manipuri**: incorporates both, **emphasis on Lasya**.
7. **Sattriya**: **combines both** Lasya and Tandava elements; generally performed by **male monks (Bhokots)**.
8. **Kuchipudi**: both Lasya and Tandava elements are **important**.
9. The aspects are **qualities in varying emphasis**, not a gender-exclusive performance rule.
10. A named dance carrying "Tandava" in its title is an **item**, not the aspect.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Lasya and Tandava describe qualities, not performers." Substantiate with evidence from at least three classical forms. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

The sources gender the vocabulary and do not gender the performance, and the per-form record proves it three times.

**Sattriya** combines both Lasya and Tandava elements and is generally performed in a group by male monks - a tradition of men carrying the aspect described as symbolic of the feminine features of the art.

**Bharatanatyam** gives equal emphasis to both aspects, and its recorded history runs from a period limited to solo female dancers to later popularity among men and groups. Same aspects, changing performers.

**Mohiniyattam** has Lasya dominant and is mainly performed by women, yet is recorded as combining Bharatanatyam's grace with Kathakali's vigour - the kinetic quality borrowed from an all-male troupe form.

Qualification: the gendered symbolism is real and should be reported; what fails is the inference from symbolism to a rule about who performs.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the symbolism-versus-performance distinction stated first 2 + three forms each with its recorded balance and performer demography 6 + a qualification that preserves the symbolism 2.**

### Concept check

**Question:** Sattriya is generally performed by male monks and is recorded as combining both Lasya and Tandava elements. What exactly does this single fact settle, and what does it not settle?

**Model answer:** It settles the **gender-exclusivity question** decisively. If the aspect described as symbolic of the feminine features of the art form is carried by a tradition performed by celibate male monks as part of their daily ritual, then the aspects cannot be a rule about which sex performs which kind of movement. They are qualities of movement and expression that any performer may carry, and the gendered vocabulary is a traditional symbolism attached to those qualities rather than a distribution of performers. What the fact does **not** settle is anything about the *history* of gender in Indian dance, which is a separate and much harder question. The record of this topic contains temple-attached female performers whose tradition lapsed, boys dressed as females who continued an art form when that happened, all-male troupes in one Kerala form and a mainly female tradition in the other, and a twentieth-century sequence in which a solo female form later became popular among men and groups. None of that is explained by the two aspects; it is explained by patronage, community and reform. Use the Sattriya fact to kill the gender-exclusivity claim, and then go to the social history for everything else.

**Misconception to avoid:** Over-reading the correction. Saying "the aspects are not gendered at all" is as inaccurate as the error it replaces, because the sources do attach a gendered symbolism to them. The defensible sentence keeps both parts: a traditional gendered symbolism, with no gender-exclusive performance rule.

## Lesson 4 - Nritta, Natya and Nritya: the three-way distinction that decides most dance questions

Progress: 4/23 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: the Abhinaya Darpana's three elements, the exact content of each, Nayaka-Nayika bhava, how the three appear inside real repertoires, and the precision test the examiner sets

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the Abhinaya Darpana section with the three elements and the Nayaka-Nayika formulation, and every per-form statement that maps a recital item onto one of the three
CA search: "Not applicable - terminological precision lesson"
CA found: None found; none claimed
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: what enters and what is excluded at each of the three doors

```text
   AN ACT OF DANCE IS BROKEN INTO THREE ELEMENTS (Abhinaya Darpana,
   Nandikeshvara)

   +----------------------+----------------------+----------------------+
   |        NRITTA        |        NATYA         |        NRITYA        |
   +----------------------+----------------------+----------------------+
   | basic dance steps    | dramatic             | sentiment and        |
   | performed            | representation       | emotion evoked       |
   | rhythmically         | the story elaborated | through dance        |
   |                      | through the recital  |                      |
   +----------------------+----------------------+----------------------+
   | DEVOID OF any        | carries a NARRATIVE  | includes MIME and    |
   | expression or mood   |                      | methods of           |
   |                      |                      | expression, incl.    |
   |                      |                      | MUDRAS               |
   +----------------------+----------------------+----------------------+
   | TEST: remove the     | TEST: remove the     | TEST: remove the     |
   | expression - it is   | story - it is no     | emotion - it is no   |
   | still Nritta         | longer Natya         | longer Nritya        |
   +----------------------+----------------------+----------------------+
            |                      |                       |
            v                      v                       v
   Jatiswaram is the      Shabdam is the         Varnam is the main
   Nritta component,      dramatic element       part, where dance and
   devoid of              with expressed         emotion combine with
   expressions            words                  tala and raag to tell
   Thillana is pure                              a story
   dance with exuberant   Sollakath/Patakshara   Kavutvams is the
   movement               is the Nritta part     Nritya part
```

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Three words, similar in sound, easily treated as synonyms for "dance". They are not, and the distinction is the single most reliable precision test an examiner can set in this topic, because it can be tested at three levels: as a definition, as an identification of a named recital item, and as a distinction between two whole forms.

### 2. The three elements, stated exactly

As per the *Abhinaya Darpana*, Nandikeshvara's treatise on dance, an act has been broken into three basic elements:

1. **Nritta** - the basic dance steps, performed rhythmically but **devoid of any expression or mood**.
2. **Natya** - **dramatic representation**; it refers to the story that is elaborated through the dance recital.
3. **Nritya** - the **sentiment and the emotions** evoked through dance. It includes the **mime** and the different **methods of expression**, including *mudras*, in the dance.

Three exclusions make the definitions usable:

- *Nritta* excludes expression. Rhythmic virtuosity is not disqualified; feeling is.
- *Natya* requires a narrative. Emotion without a story is not *Natya*.
- *Nritya* requires an emotional target and uses the expressive apparatus. Story is not necessary; feeling is.

### 3. Nayaka-Nayika bhava: the relationship the expression usually models

Nandikeshvara also explained the **Nayaka-Nayika Bhav**, in which the **deity is the hero (Nayaka)** and the **devotee-dancer is the heroine (Nayika)**.

This is small in size and large in use. It explains, in a single formulation, why so much of the expressive repertoire of Indian classical dance is structured as a relationship of longing, service, separation and union addressed to a deity - and why *Shringara*, the rasa of love, sits at the head of the nine-rasa list in a devotional tradition without any contradiction. It is also the conceptual bridge into the Vaishnava repertoires that dominate the eastern and north-eastern forms: the Radha-Krishna material of Manipuri's *Ras Leela*, the *Gita Govinda* lyrics of Odissi, the Vishnu narratives of Sattriya. The devotee-as-heroine formulation is what allows the same emotional grammar to serve both love poetry and devotion.

### 4. The three elements inside real repertoires

The distinction is not abstract; the recorded repertoires label their own items with it. This table is worth memorising as a block, because it converts a definition into an identification skill.

| Form | Item explicitly mapped | Element |
|---|---|---|
| Bharatanatyam | **Jatiswaram** - devoid of expressions, includes different poses and movements | **Nritta** |
| Bharatanatyam | **Shabdam** - the dramatic element with expressed words, includes abhinaya in the song | **Natya** |
| Bharatanatyam | **Varnam** - the main part, where dance and emotions combine with *tala* and *raag* to tell a story | **Nritya** |
| Bharatanatyam | **Thillana** - the concluding stage, pure dance with exuberant movement and intricate rhythmic variations | **Nritta** |
| Kuchipudi | **Sollakath / Patakshara** - the part where movement of the body is made | **Nritta** |
| Kuchipudi | **Kavutvams** - involves extensive acrobatics; may also be performed as Nritta | **Nritya** |
| Odissi | **Tharijham** - pure dance before the conclusion | **Nritta** |
| Odissi | **Pallavi** - includes use of facial expressions and the representation of the song | expressive |
| Kathak | **Tarana** - comprises pure rhythmic movements before the end | **Nritta** |
| Kathak | **Gat Bhav** - dance without any music or chanting, used to outline mythological episodes | narrative |

Three observations to carry forward. First, **the same form contains all three elements**; this is why "which element does Bharatanatyam use" is a badly formed question and "which item is the Nritta component" is a well-formed one. Second, **Kuchipudi, Sattriya and Odissi are each recorded as involving all three elements** - *Nritta*, *Natya* and *Nritya* - which is a frequently tested completeness statement. Third, note the Kathak item **Gat Bhav**, dance without any music or chanting used to outline mythological episodes: narrative carried with neither text nor accompaniment is the purest demonstration in the topic that the three elements are analytically separable from the arts that usually accompany them.

### 5. Why the distinction is the precision test

Consider how an examiner can build items from these three words alone:

- **Definition level.** Which of the three is devoid of expression? Which includes mime and mudras? Which refers to the story?
- **Item level.** A recital item is described and the element must be named - the strategy is to ask whether expression is present, and if so whether a story is being told.
- **Form level.** A form is described in terms of what it emphasises. A dance-drama in which dance and drama cannot be clearly separated sits differently from a solo expressive form.
- **Negative level.** A statement is offered that treats the three as interchangeable, and must be rejected.

The single sentence that defends against all four: *Nritta* is pure rhythmic movement without expression, *Natya* is dramatic representation of a story, and *Nritya* is expression and emotion conveyed through mime and *mudras*.

### Examination use

For Prelims, memorise the three definitions verbatim in substance, the attribution to Nandikeshvara's *Abhinaya Darpana*, the Nayaka-Nayika formulation, and the item-to-element mappings in the table above. Matching items and "which of the following is the Nritta component" items are built directly from that block.

For Mains, this lesson gives you the vocabulary to describe a form precisely in one sentence instead of vaguely in five. It also gives you the standard structure for a "distinguish between two forms" demand: describe each form's balance of the three elements, then name one item from each repertoire that carries a different element, and the comparison writes itself.

### Traps introduced here

- Treating the three terms as synonyms for dance.
- Attributing the three-element scheme to the *Natyashastra* instead of to Nandikeshvara's *Abhinaya Darpana*.
- Calling an expressive item *Nritta* because it is rhythmically complex.
- Inverting *Natya* and *Nritya*, which is the commonest single error in this topic.
- Forgetting that in the Nayaka-Nayika formulation the **deity** is the hero and the **devotee-dancer** the heroine, not the reverse.

### Revision notes

1. Source of the three-element scheme: **Nandikeshvara's *Abhinaya Darpana***.
2. **Nritta** = basic dance steps performed rhythmically, **devoid of any expression or mood**.
3. **Natya** = **dramatic representation**; the story elaborated through the recital.
4. **Nritya** = **sentiment and emotions** evoked through dance; includes **mime** and methods of expression, including **mudras**.
5. **Nayaka-Nayika Bhav**: deity = **Nayaka** (hero); devotee-dancer = **Nayika** (heroine).
6. Bharatanatyam mapping: **Jatiswaram = Nritta; Shabdam = Natya; Varnam = Nritya; Thillana = Nritta**.
7. Kuchipudi mapping: **Sollakath/Patakshara = Nritta; Kavutvams = Nritya**, which may also be performed as Nritta.
8. **Kuchipudi, Sattriya and Odissi** are each recorded as involving all three components.
9. Kathak: **Tarana** = pure rhythmic movement; **Gat Bhav** = dance without music or chanting outlining mythological episodes.
10. Precision test: expression present? story present? - those two questions place any item correctly.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Explain how the three-element scheme of the Abhinaya Darpana allows a recital item to be classified even when its form is unknown. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

The scheme classifies by two questions about the danced act itself, not by the form it sits in.

Ask whether **expression** is present. If it is absent, the item is *Nritta* - basic steps performed rhythmically and devoid of any expression or mood. If it is present, ask whether a **story** is being elaborated. If yes, *Natya*, dramatic representation; if no, *Nritya*, sentiment and emotion conveyed through mime and mudras.

The test works across forms because the same labels are applied inside different repertoires: an expressionless item of poses and movements in one recital, a part where body movement alone is made in another, and a concluding item of pure dance with intricate rhythmic variation, are all *Nritta* regardless of region.

Its power is shown by a silent narrative item danced without music or chanting: narrative, therefore *Natya*, with no accompaniment to help.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the two-question test stated as a procedure 3 + the three definitions used precisely 3 + cross-form application with named items 2 + the unaccompanied-narrative case as the decisive demonstration 2.**

### Concept check

**Question:** A recital item is described as follows: the dancer performs without any accompanying music or chanting, using the body alone to outline an episode from mythology. Which of the three elements is dominant, and what does this item prove about the relationship between the three elements and the arts that usually accompany dance?

**Model answer:** The dominant element is **Natya**, dramatic representation, because a story is being elaborated through the recital. The item is Kathak's **Gat Bhav**, recorded as dance without any music or chanting, used to outline different mythological episodes. What it proves is that the three elements are **properties of the danced act itself**, not by-products of its accompaniment. It would have been easy to assume that *Natya* depends on a sung or spoken text - Bharatanatyam's *Shabdam* is described precisely as the dramatic element with expressed words, and Kathakali's narrative is carried by a named song-text - and Gat Bhav shows that the assumption is false. Narrative can be carried by movement with neither text nor music present. The practical consequence for an answer is that you classify an item by asking what the body is doing, not by asking what is being sung over it.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that the absence of music makes an item *Nritta*. *Nritta* is defined by the absence of **expression and mood**, not by the absence of accompaniment. An unaccompanied narrative mime is full of expression and is therefore the opposite of *Nritta*, however silent the stage is.

## Lesson 5 - Abhinaya, bhava and rasa: how an emotion is made transmissible

Progress: 5/23 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: the nine rasas in full, bhava against rasa, abhinaya as the four-element contribution, the Navarasas in performance, and the boundary with aesthetic theory

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the nine-rasa enumeration, the statement that moods and expressions are emoted through mudras, and the per-form records of facial expression, veiling and the nine facial expressions taught in one dance-drama
CA search: "Not applicable - the emotional apparatus is a timeless doctrinal element of this topic"
CA found: None found; none claimed
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the transmission chain, and the nine destinations

```text
   WHAT IS INSIDE                 HOW IT IS CARRIED           WHAT IS PRODUCED
   THE PERFORMER                  ACROSS THE GAP              IN THE AUDIENCE

      BHAVA          ------>        ABHINAYA        ------>       RASA
   the mood or                   the gesture-and-             the emotion
   emotional state               expression apparatus         evoked
                                 (one of the four
                                  Natya Veda elements,
                                  drawn from Yajur)
                                        |
                          +-------------+-------------+
                          |             |             |
                    hasta/mudra    facial        bodily
                    (hand          expression    posture and
                    gesture)       (bhavabhinaya) movement

                       THE NINE RASAS (Navarasas)
   +-----------------+-----------------+-----------------+
   | Shringara  love | Roudra    anger | Bibhatsa disgust|
   +-----------------+-----------------+-----------------+
   | Veera   heroism | Shanta    peace | Haasya laughter |
   |                 | and tranquility | and comedy      |
   +-----------------+-----------------+-----------------+
   | Karuna   sorrow | Bhayanaka horror| Adbhuta  wonder |
   | and compassion  |                 |                 |
   +-----------------+-----------------+-----------------+

   MNEMONIC BY PAIRS OF OPPOSITES (a learning aid, not a source claim):
   love / disgust  .  heroism / horror  .  laughter / sorrow  .  anger / peace
   with WONDER standing outside every pair
```

### 1. The learner's actual problem

"Nine rasas" is one of the most-memorised and least-used facts in this subject. Candidates can list them and then cannot say what work they do. The work is this: the nine rasas are the **named destinations** of a transmission system, and the whole technical apparatus of Indian dance - gesture, facial expression, posture, tempo - exists to reach one of them reliably. Once that is seen, the list stops being trivia and becomes the answer to "why is this art so heavily codified?"

### 2. The chain: bhava, abhinaya, rasa

Keep three words in a fixed order.

- **Bhava** is the mood or emotional state. In the Lasya description it is named alongside *rasa* and *abhinaya* as what that aspect denotes.
- **Abhinaya** is the carrying apparatus - gesture, and by extension the whole art of expression. It is one of the four constituent contributions of the Natya Veda, drawn from the Yajur Veda, which is why it is structural rather than ornamental.
- **Rasa** is the emotion evoked. It is the fourth constituent, drawn from the Atharva Veda.

The relationship to memorise is directional: *bhava* is what is being conveyed, *abhinaya* is how, and *rasa* is what results. **Dance expresses nine rasas.** A form that emphasises expression is a form that emphasises *abhinaya*; a form that veils the face has deliberately narrowed one channel of *abhinaya* and must compensate through others.

### 3. The nine rasas, in full

Dance expresses nine rasas:

| # | Rasa | For |
|---:|---|---|
| 1 | **Shringara** | love |
| 2 | **Roudra** | anger |
| 3 | **Bibhatsa** | disgust |
| 4 | **Veera** | heroism |
| 5 | **Shanta** | peace and tranquility |
| 6 | **Haasya** | laughter and comedy |
| 7 | **Karuna** | sorrow and compassion |
| 8 | **Bhayanaka** | horror |
| 9 | **Adbhuta** | wonder |

Two notes. First, several of these have a **double gloss** - *Shanta* is peace **and tranquility**, *Haasya* is laughter **and comedy**, *Karuna* is sorrow **and compassion** - and the second half of each gloss is worth writing, because it shows you have the term rather than a one-word translation. Second, the same nine-fold scheme recurs in the music and sculpture topics, which makes it one of the few pieces of vocabulary that pays for itself three times.

### 4. How the rasas are actually produced: the mudra statement

The recorded mechanism is explicit: these **moods and expressions are emoted through the use of *mudras* - a combination of hand gestures and body postures**. That single sentence contains the definition you should reproduce, because it is broader than the usual one: a *mudra* here is a **combination of hand gesture and body posture**, not a hand shape alone.

The apparatus is developed fully in the next lesson. What matters now is the logical order: the emotional target comes first, the gesture system exists to reach it, and the number and complexity of gestures is a consequence of the precision required. A tradition that wants to distinguish reliably between sorrow and compassion, or between horror and wonder, needs a fine-grained vocabulary - and that is why the vocabulary is fine-grained.

### 5. The nine rasas in a specific form: the Kathakali case

The clearest recorded instance of the rasa system as working technique is **Kathakali**, in which gestures are the crown jewel of the entire dance-drama. The form is recorded as **remarkable in the representation of the rasas through movements of the eye and eyebrows**, through which the story is conveyed, and **nine important facial expressions called *Navarasas*** are taught to convey the different emotions. Extensive hand gestures are also used, and the *mudras* are based on the *Hastalakshana Deepika*, a treatise on the language of hand gestures. This is why the form calls for strenuous training.

Three things follow, and each is examinable.

1. **The eyes and eyebrows are a named channel.** Where most descriptions of expression stop at "facial expression", this one specifies the anatomical channel. In an identification item, "representation of rasas through eye and eyebrow movement" is a discriminating detail.
2. **"Navarasas" is the working name for the nine in performance.** The doctrinal list and the performance term are the same nine.
3. **There is a second gesture treatise.** *Hastalakshana Deepika* is the named source of Kathakali's hand-gesture language, distinct from the *Natyashastra* and the *Abhinaya Darpana*. Three treatises now sit in this topic and they should not be merged.

### 6. The contrast case: a form that narrows the channel

Set beside Kathakali the recorded description of **Manipuri**: the faces are **covered with a thin veil** and **facial expression is of lesser importance**; hand gestures and gentle movement of the feet are important; and the form is unique in its **emphasis on devotion and not sensuality**.

This is the most instructive contrast in the lesson. Two classical forms, the same emotional apparatus available, and opposite choices about which channel to use. Kathakali maximises facial expression to the point of building a whole pedagogy on eye and eyebrow movement; Manipuri veils the face and shifts the burden to the hands and feet. The lesson for an answer is that **abhinaya is a set of channels, and a form is partly defined by which channels it privileges and which it closes**. That is a far better sentence than "Manipuri is graceful".

### 7. The boundary with aesthetic theory

⚠️ The full philosophical theory of *rasa* - how an emotion in a performer becomes an aesthetic experience in a spectator, what kind of thing a *rasa* is, and the commentarial debates about it - belongs to the religion, philosophy and cultural-synthesis topic and is developed there. In this session *rasa* is used as **performance vocabulary**: the named emotional targets, the channels that reach them, and the per-form evidence of which channels are used. Do not import an aesthetic-theory argument into a dance answer unless the question asks for it; and do not answer a rasa-theory question with a list of dance forms.

### Examination use

For Prelims, the nine rasas with both halves of each gloss, the *Navarasas* term, the *Hastalakshana Deepika* attribution and the Manipuri veil-and-lesser-importance record are all directly testable, and the last two are the kind of discriminating detail that decides a four-option identification.

For Mains, this lesson supplies the causal explanation that raises an answer above description: Indian dance is heavily codified **because** it commits to producing named emotional effects reliably in an audience, and codification is what makes reliability possible. That sentence can be deployed in any answer on the *Natyashastra*, on the relationship between dance and sculpture, or on why the guru-based transmission system was necessary.

### Traps introduced here

- Listing the nine rasas without their glosses, or truncating the double glosses.
- Treating *bhava* and *rasa* as synonyms; one is in the performer, the other is evoked in the audience.
- Assuming every classical form maximises facial expression; Manipuri veils the face and treats facial expression as of lesser importance.
- Merging the three treatises: *Natyashastra*, *Abhinaya Darpana* and *Hastalakshana Deepika* are distinct and are attached to different things.
- Importing aesthetic-theory debates into what is a performance-vocabulary answer.

### Revision notes

1. Chain: **bhava (mood) -> abhinaya (carrying apparatus) -> rasa (emotion evoked)**.
2. *Abhinaya* and *Rasa* are two of the **four constituent elements** of the Natya Veda, from **Yajur** and **Atharva** respectively.
3. **Nine rasas: Shringara (love), Roudra (anger), Bibhatsa (disgust), Veera (heroism), Shanta (peace and tranquility), Haasya (laughter and comedy), Karuna (sorrow and compassion), Bhayanaka (horror), Adbhuta (wonder).**
4. Moods and expressions are emoted through **mudras - a combination of hand gestures and body postures**.
5. **Kathakali**: rasas represented through **eye and eyebrow movement**; **nine facial expressions called Navarasas**; mudras based on **Hastalakshana Deepika**; strenuous training.
6. **Manipuri**: face **covered with a thin veil**, facial expression of **lesser importance**, hand gestures and gentle foot movement important; **devotion, not sensuality**.
7. **Bharatanatyam** uses **Bhavabhinaya** - facial expression - as one of its three named techniques.
8. **Nayaka-Nayika bhava** explains why *Shringara* heads the list in devotional repertoires.
9. The nine-rasa scheme is **shared** with the music and sculpture topics.
10. Full *rasa* theory as aesthetics is **routed out** to the religion-philosophy topic.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"In Indian dance, rasa is a technical target rather than a mood." Examine. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The proposition holds, and the strongest evidence is that the scheme behaves like a specification rather than like a description of feeling.

A mood is diffuse; the scheme is enumerated and closed. Nine rasas are named - love, anger, disgust, heroism, peace and tranquility, laughter and comedy, sorrow and compassion, horror, wonder - and several are deliberately fine-grained, separating sorrow from compassion within one entry and setting horror beside wonder as distinct targets.

A mood belongs to a person; a rasa is produced in a receiver. The chain is directional: **bhava** is the state conveyed, **abhinaya** the apparatus that carries it, **rasa** the emotion evoked. That is a transmission model, and it is why the moods and expressions are recorded as being emoted through mudras - combinations of hand gesture and body posture.

A mood cannot be taught; a target can. One dance-drama teaches nine named facial expressions explicitly, conveys them through movement of the eye and eyebrows, and draws its hand gestures from a dedicated treatise. That is a curriculum aimed at a specification.

The qualification is that traditions differ in which channels they use to reach the same targets. One form maximises facial expression; another veils the face and treats it as of lesser importance, shifting the load to hands and feet. The targets are shared; the routes are not.

Verdict: rasa names what a performance is designed to achieve, which is why the art surrounding it is codified rather than merely expressive.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the specification-versus-description distinction 2 + the enumerated scheme with its fine-grained entries 4 + the directional bhava-abhinaya-rasa chain with the mudra mechanism 4 + the taught-pedagogy evidence from one form 3 + the channel qualification with a contrasting form 1 + verdict 1.**

### Concept check

**Question:** Why is it more accurate to describe *abhinaya* as a set of channels than as "acting", and what single comparison in this topic demonstrates the point?

**Model answer:** Because "acting" suggests one undifferentiated skill, whereas the record treats expression as running through **separable, independently controllable channels** - the hand gesture, the facial expression, the movement of the eyes and eyebrows specifically, and the posture and movement of the whole body. A form does not simply have more or less *abhinaya*; it chooses which channels to open and which to close, and that choice is part of its identity. The demonstrating comparison is **Kathakali against Manipuri**. Kathakali makes the face and, within the face, the eyes and eyebrows its principal narrative instrument, teaches nine facial expressions as a named pedagogy, and draws its hand-gesture language from a dedicated treatise. Manipuri covers the face with a thin veil, expressly treats facial expression as of lesser importance, and shifts the load onto hand gestures and gentle foot movement while emphasising devotion rather than sensuality. Both are classical, both work within the same nine-rasa scheme, and they are at opposite ends of a single design decision. The general rule an answer can state is: **identify the channel a form privileges, and you have identified the form**.

**Misconception to avoid:** Inferring that a veiled face means weak or undeveloped expression. Closing a channel is a design choice with a stated purpose - in this case, an emphasis on devotion rather than sensuality - and it raises rather than lowers the demand on the remaining channels. Ranking forms by how much facial expression they use is exactly the kind of hierarchy this session refuses.

## Lesson 6 - The body as vocabulary: hasta, mudra, karana, adavu - and the disputed number 108

Progress: 6/23 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the gesture-and-posture apparatus, the named signature postures of the eight forms, the movement-unit vocabulary, and an explicit disagreement about what the number 108 counts

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the mudra statement and its count, the per-form posture records (Katakamukha, tribhanga, chowk, Nagabandha, araimandi-type knee position), the adavu and Atavakul records, and the second gesture treatise named for one Kerala form
CA search: "Not applicable - body-vocabulary lesson; the disagreement handled here is textual, not current"
CA found: None found; none claimed
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the body vocabulary from smallest unit to whole-body posture

```text
   SMALLEST ---------------------------------------------------> LARGEST

   HASTA / MUDRA            ADAVU / ATAVU            KARANA
   a codified gesture       a movement unit          a coordinated unit of
   - defined in the         from which a recital     WHOLE-BODY movement
     sources as a           is built                 (the unit the number
     COMBINATION of         Bharatanatyam: Adavu     108 attaches to in
     HAND GESTURE and       Mohiniyattam:            standard treatment of
     BODY POSTURE           Atavakul/Atavus = a      the Natyashastra)
   - named example:         collection of 40 basic
     Katakamukha Hasta      dance movements
     (Bharatanatyam):
     three fingers joined
     to symbolise 'Om'

                    SIGNATURE WHOLE-BODY POSTURES
   +-----------------+---------------------------------------------+
   | TRIBHANGA       | three-bend form of the body; innate to      |
   |                 | ODISSI; also used extensively by the        |
   |                 | Amaravati sculptors                         |
   +-----------------+---------------------------------------------+
   | CHOWK           | hands spread out, depicts masculinity;      |
   |                 | ODISSI                                      |
   +-----------------+---------------------------------------------+
   | NAGABANDHA      | body connected through curves in the shape  |
   |                 | of '8'; MANIPURI - and NOT Mohiniyattam     |
   +-----------------+---------------------------------------------+
   | BENT-KNEE       | in a Bharatanatyam recital the knees are    |
   | WEIGHT-EVEN     | mostly bent and the weight is equally       |
   | STANCE          | distributed across both feet                |
   +-----------------+---------------------------------------------+
```

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Everything in this topic is described using words for parts of the body in motion, and candidates use those words loosely. "Mudra" becomes a general word for anything the dancer does; "posture" becomes a synonym for "pose"; and the number 108 gets attached to whichever noun happens to be nearby. This lesson fixes the vocabulary at three scales and then confronts the one genuine disagreement in it.

### 2. Hasta and mudra: the definition that is broader than you expect

Moods and expressions are emoted through the use of **mudras - a combination of hand gestures and body postures**. Write that definition, because it contains something most candidates leave out: a *mudra* in this usage is not only a hand shape; it is a hand gesture **together with** a body posture. A combination of mudras is used to depict a particular emotion.
