Alongside it sits the term **hasta**, used in the per-form records as the codified hand gesture proper - Bharatanatyam uses **Hastamudra (hand gestures)** as one of its three named techniques, and Odissi is recorded as similar to Bharatanatyam **in the use of Mudras and postures to express emotions**.

A named example makes the class concrete. One of the principal mudras of Bharatanatyam is **Katakamukha Hasta**, in which **three fingers are joined to symbolise 'Om'**. Notice what makes it examinable: a name, a physical description and a stated meaning. That is the level of specificity to reproduce; a general statement that "hand gestures convey meaning" carries no marks.

❌ **Do not invent gesture meanings.** Where this session does not record what a named gesture symbolises, no symbolism should be supplied. Fabricated costume and gesture symbolism is one of the two or three most common ways this topic is answered badly.

### 3. Adavu and Atavu: the movement unit

Between the single gesture and the whole-body movement sits the **movement unit** from which a recital is actually built.

- **Bharatanatyam** uses **Adavu (steps)**, alongside **Hastamudra (hand gestures)** and **Bhavabhinaya (facial expressions)**. Those three together are the form's named technical apparatus and should be quoted as a set.
- **Mohiniyattam** has **'Atavakul' or 'Atavus'** - recorded as the collection of **40 basic dance movements**. The number is small, specific and frequently tested, and it belongs to Mohiniyattam.

The general point: a classical form is teachable because it decomposes into a finite inventory of units. That is what makes a *guru-shishya* transmission system workable across generations, and it is also what makes reconstruction possible when a tradition is disrupted - a theme that returns in Lesson 22.

### 4. Karana: the whole-body coordinated unit

A **karana** is a coordinated unit of whole-body movement - the largest of the three scales in this lesson. It is not a hand gesture and it is not a step; it is a complete articulated movement of the body treated as one nameable unit.

The reason it matters is entirely practical: **karana is the noun to which the number 108 is standardly attached** in treatments of the *Natyashastra*, and the number 108 is the single most frequently misplaced figure in this topic.

### 5. The disagreement about 108, stated openly

Here two formulations that a candidate will meet do not agree, and the honest thing is to set them side by side rather than to pick the convenient one.

| Formulation | What it says |
|---|---|
| As printed in standard preparation material | "There are **108 fundamental mudras**, a combination of which is used to depict a particular emotion." |
| As standardly treated in scholarship on the *Natyashastra* | The number **108 attaches to the *karanas***, the coordinated whole-body movement units codified in the treatise's chapter on the characteristics of Tandava; **hand-gesture counts are treatise-specific and are not 108**. |

⚠️ **How to handle this in an examination.** Three rules, in order of importance.

1. **Never build an answer on the number alone.** If a Mains question invites you to discuss the *Natyashastra*'s codification, write about *what* is codified - whole-body movement units, hand gestures, postures, facial expression - and the fact that the counts are treatise-specific. That is stronger than any number.
2. **If the number must be used, attach it to *karanas*.** The safe sentence is: "the *Natyashastra* tradition codifies 108 *karanas*, coordinated units of whole-body movement; the number of hand gestures varies by treatise."
3. **Record the disagreement rather than suppressing it.** An answer that says "the figure of 108 is standardly attached to the *karanas*, though it is sometimes reported of the mudras" demonstrates exactly the source-handling the examiner is looking for.

⚠️ A further reported figure, recorded here as **reported rather than settled**: the *Abhinaya Darpana*'s hand-gesture inventory is widely given as **28 single-hand (asamyuta) and 23 combined-hand (samyuta) gestures**. This session does not treat that total as verified from a primary source and does not build any claim on it; it is stated only so that a candidate who meets it elsewhere can place it. **Do not write it as a certainty.**

❌ **The trap in its pure form:** "There are 108 basic mudras in Indian classical dance." Rewritten safely: "The *Natyashastra* tradition codifies 108 *karanas*; hand-gesture counts differ from treatise to treatise."

### 6. The signature postures, and the one that is deliberately misassigned in options

Four whole-body postures are named in this topic, and three of them are single-form discriminators.

- **Tribhanga** - the **three-bended form of the body**, **innate to Odissi**. It is also the posture used extensively by the **Amaravati sculptors**, which makes it the single most important term in the dance-sculpture bridge of Lesson 21.
- **Chowk** - hands spread out, **depicts masculinity**; the counterpart posture in Odissi. Odissi's lower body remains largely static during the dance with only movement of the torso, which is why the form is described as **"mobile sculpture"**.
- **Nagabandha mudra** - the body connected through **curves in the shape of '8'**; an important posture in **Manipuri**. ❌ **Nagabandha is Manipuri, not Mohiniyattam.** This is a deliberately planted confusion in option sets, because both forms are lyrical and both are described with the vocabulary of grace.
- **The Bharatanatyam stance** - in a recital the **knees are mostly bent** and the **weight is equally distributed across both the feet**. This bent-knee, evenly weighted idiom is what distinguishes Bharatanatyam's body line from Kathak's upright, spin-and-footwork idiom.

### 7. Why codification at this scale was necessary

Step back and connect this lesson to the previous one. A tradition that undertakes to produce nine named emotional effects reliably needs a vocabulary fine enough to distinguish sorrow from compassion and horror from wonder. That requirement is met at three scales: a gesture inventory at the smallest scale, a movement-unit inventory in the middle, and a whole-body movement inventory at the largest. The result is an art that can be **taught, corrected, transmitted and - crucially - reconstructed**. Lesson 22 shows what happened when reconstruction was actually required; the possibility of reconstruction was created here, by codification.

### Examination use

For Prelims, memorise as discrete facts: the mudra definition as gesture-plus-posture; Katakamukha Hasta with its three joined fingers symbolising 'Om'; the Bharatanatyam triad of Adavu, Hastamudra and Bhavabhinaya; Mohiniyattam's 40 Atavakul/Atavus; tribhanga and chowk as Odissi and tribhanga's use by the Amaravati sculptors; Nagabandha as Manipuri; the bent-knee, evenly weighted Bharatanatyam stance; and the *Hastalakshana Deepika* as the treatise behind Kathakali's hand gestures.

For Mains, the codification argument in section 7 is the transferable asset. It answers "why is Indian classical dance so technically elaborate?" with a mechanism rather than with praise, and it sets up the revival argument later in the session.

### Traps introduced here

- Attaching 108 to mudras without qualification.
- Using "mudra" for hand shape alone when the recorded definition includes body posture.
- Assigning Nagabandha to Mohiniyattam.
- Assigning the 40 Atavakul to Bharatanatyam rather than to Mohiniyattam.
- Inventing a symbolic meaning for a named gesture or costume item.
- Forgetting that tribhanga is shared with sculpture, which is where half its exam value lies.

### Revision notes

1. **Mudra** = **a combination of hand gestures and body postures**; a combination of mudras depicts a particular emotion.
2. **Katakamukha Hasta** (Bharatanatyam) = **three fingers joined to symbolise 'Om'**.
3. Bharatanatyam's named apparatus: **Adavu (steps), Hastamudra (hand gestures), Bhavabhinaya (facial expressions)**.
4. **Mohiniyattam: 'Atavakul' or 'Atavus' = 40 basic dance movements.**
5. **Karana** = coordinated unit of **whole-body** movement.
6. **108 standardly attaches to karanas**, not to a universal mudra count; some preparation material prints "108 fundamental mudras" - record the disagreement, do not resolve it silently.
7. The *Abhinaya Darpana* hand-gesture total of **28 single + 23 combined** is **reported, not verified here**; do not assert it.
8. **Tribhanga** = three-bend posture, **innate to Odissi**, used extensively by the **Amaravati sculptors**.
9. **Chowk** = hands spread out, depicts masculinity (Odissi); lower body static, torso moves; hence **"mobile sculpture"**.
10. **Nagabandha** = figure-of-eight body connection, **Manipuri**.
11. **Bharatanatyam stance**: knees mostly bent, weight equally distributed across both feet.
12. **Hastalakshana Deepika** = the treatise on hand-gesture language behind **Kathakali**'s mudras.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Explain how the body vocabulary of Indian classical dance is organised, and why that organisation matters beyond description. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

It is organised at three scales. The smallest is the **hasta** or **mudra**, defined as a combination of hand gesture and body posture, of which a named instance joins three fingers to symbolise 'Om'. The middle scale is the **movement unit** - *adavu* in one form, a collection of forty *Atavus* in another. The largest is the **karana**, a coordinated unit of whole-body movement, to which the number 108 is standardly attached.

Above these sit named whole-body postures: the three-bend *tribhanga* and the squared *chowk*, a figure-of-eight connection, and a bent-knee stance with weight evenly distributed.

Why it matters: an art decomposed into finite inventories can be taught, corrected and transmitted through a guru - and, when a carrier community is displaced, it can be **reconstructed**. Codification is what made twentieth-century revival technically possible.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the three scales named with a definition each 4 + a named instance at two of the three scales 2 + the signature postures 2 + the reconstruction consequence as the analytical payoff 2.**

### Concept check

**Question:** You are given the sentence: "Indian classical dance uses 108 basic mudras, of which Nagabandha is the best known in Mohiniyattam." Rewrite it so that every element is defensible, and state what each correction is protecting against.

**Model answer:** A defensible rewriting: "The *Natyashastra* tradition codifies **108 *karanas***, which are coordinated units of whole-body movement; hand-gesture inventories are **treatise-specific** and differ between texts. **Nagabandha**, in which the body is connected through curves in the shape of an '8', is an important posture in **Manipuri**." Three corrections are doing the work. Moving 108 from mudras to karanas protects against the commonest numeric trap in the topic and simultaneously demonstrates that you know a karana and a mudra are units at different scales. Replacing "basic mudras" with a statement that hand-gesture counts are treatise-specific protects against asserting a count that the available evidence does not settle - and it is a stronger sentence than any number would have been, because it shows source awareness. Moving Nagabandha from Mohiniyattam to Manipuri protects against a planted regional confusion between the two most lyrical forms in the topic; the discriminating features that keep them apart are Mohiniyattam's absence of thumping and white Kasavu costume against Manipuri's veiled face and pung drum.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that a precise-sounding number is safer than a qualified statement. In this topic the opposite is true. An unqualified count that the sources disagree about is a liability; a sentence that names what is counted, attaches the number to the right noun, and notes that other counts are treatise-specific is both accurate and visibly better informed.

## Lesson 7 - Time, stage and recital: how a performance is organised once the vocabulary exists

Progress: 7/23 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: tempo and rhythmic syllable, the invocation-to-conclusion architecture shared across forms, stage setting and lighting, solo against group against troupe, and the named organising devices

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the recital sequences of four forms, the opening and closing item records, the Kathak rhythmic devices, the Kathakali stage and lighting record, the Kuchipudi character-introduction device, and the solo, group and troupe statements
CA search: "Not applicable - performance-architecture lesson"
CA found: None found; none claimed
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the shared architecture of a classical recital, with four forms mapped onto it

```text
   STAGE            OPENING           BODY OF THE        CONCLUSION
   PREPARATION      INVOCATION        RECITAL
   -----------      -----------       -----------        -----------
   attention,       respect to the    nritta, natya      pure dance,
   blessing,        divine, the       and nritya         liberation,
   tuning           guru and the      items in           or formal leave-
                    audience          sequence           taking

   BHARATANATYAM
   worship of       Alarippu          Jatiswaram ->      Thillana
   Ganapati and     (flowering bud;   Shabdam ->         (pure dance,
   Nataraja         simple steps and  Varnam ->          exuberant movement,
                    rhythms; respect  Padam -> Jawali    intricate rhythmic
                    to God, teacher,  (short love-       variations)
                    audience;         lyrics at a
                    seeking blessing) faster tempo)

   ODISSI
   -                Mangalacharan     Batu nritya ->     Moksha (joyous
                    (a flower offered Pallavi ->         movements signifying
                    to Mother Earth)  Tharijham          liberation)
                                                         OR Trikhanda majura
                                                         (leave taken from
                                                         the gods, the
                                                         audience and the
                                                         stage)

   KATHAK
   -                Ananda            Thaat -> Todas     Kramalaya
                    (introductory     and Tukdas ->      (intricate and
                    item; the dancer  Jugalbandi ->      fast footwork)
                    enters the stage) Padhant ->
                                      Gat Bhav           preceded by Tarana
                                                         (pure rhythmic
                                                         movement)

   KATHAKALI
   Kelikottu        Todayam           the dance-drama    dawn, with the
   (calling the     (invoking the     proper: gesture-   continuous sound
   attention of     blessings of      led narration      of drums, marks
   the audience)    the Gods)                            the beginning AND
                                                         the end of a recital
```

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Candidates learn recital sequences as strings of unfamiliar words and then cannot use them, because they have never been shown that the four strings are four fillings of **one architecture**. Once the architecture is visible, the sequences stop being arbitrary: each form opens by securing attention and blessing, works through items that shift between pure movement, narrative and expression, and closes with either virtuosity or a formal departure.

### 2. The opening: three things an invocation does

Every opening item in the table above is doing some combination of three jobs, and naming the job is what turns memorisation into understanding.

- **Securing the audience.** Kathakali's **Kelikottu** is explicitly *calling the attention of the audience*. A performance in an open-air setting has to gather its audience before it can begin.
- **Securing blessing.** Kathakali's **Todayam** invokes the blessings of the Gods. Bharatanatyam performances **start with the worship of Ganapati and Nataraja**. Odissi's **Mangalacharan** is the beginning, where **a flower is offered to Mother Earth**.
- **Securing the relationship.** Bharatanatyam's **Alarippu**, the *flowering bud*, is an opening piece with simple dance steps and rhythms, **showing respect to God, the teacher, the audience, and seeking blessings**. Three addressees in one item - divine, pedagogic and social - which is the whole social structure of the art in miniature.

Kathak's opening, **Ananda**, is described more plainly as the introductory item through which the dancer enters the stage. Noting that difference is itself useful: the form with the most complex court history has the least ritually loaded entry.

### 3. The body of the recital: how movement, story and expression are interleaved

The middle of a recital is where the three elements of Lesson 4 are deployed in sequence. Bharatanatyam's order is the clearest worked example and should be learned as an order, not as a list:

| Item | What it is |
|---|---|
| **Alarippu** | opening piece, simple dance steps and rhythms; respect to God, teacher and audience; seeking blessings |
| **Jatiswaram** | the **Nritta** component; devoid of expressions; different poses and movements |
| **Shabdam** | the **dramatic** element with expressed words; includes the abhinaya in the song; generally performed in praise of the glory of God |
| **Varnam** | **Nritya** is the main part; dance and emotions combine with **tala** (rhythm) and **raag** (melody) to tell a story |
| **Padam** | mastery over the **abhinaya** of the spiritual message; music becomes light, dance becomes emotional |
| **Jawali** | short **love-lyrics** performed at a **faster tempo** |
| **Thillana** | the concluding stage; **pure dance (Nritta)** with exuberant movement and intricate rhythmic variations |

Read down the second column and the design is obvious: pure movement first, then words, then the fusion of story with rhythm and melody, then pure expression, then a lighter and faster lyric, then a return to pure movement at speed. The recital moves from body to meaning and back to body. That is the sentence to write in an answer; the seven names alone are worth much less.

Odissi's middle - **Batu nritya** (comprising the dance, with Tribhanga and Chowk postures) then **Pallavi** (use of facial expressions and representation of the song) then **Tharijham** (pure dance before the conclusion) - follows the same logic with three items instead of five.

### 4. Tempo, rhythmic syllable and the contest with the drummer

Three time-related devices are recorded in this topic and each is a discriminating detail.

- **Tempo change as a named property of an item.** Bharatanatyam's **Jawali** is short love-lyrics **performed at a faster tempo**. Kathak's **Todas and Tukdas** are small pieces of **fast rhythm**, and its closing **Kramalaya** comprises **intricate and fast footwork**. Speed is compositional, not incidental.
- **Rhythmic syllable recitation.** Kathak's **Padhant** is a special feature in which the **dancer recites complicated *bols* and then demonstrates them**. Sattriya places **great emphasis on rhythmic syllables and dance postures along with footwork**. The dancer here is not only moving to a rhythm but articulating it aloud - a direct demonstration that the rhythmic structure is a shared property of dancer and drummer.
- **The competitive duet.** Kathak's **Jugalbandi** is described as the **main attraction** of a recital: a **competitive play between the dancer and the tabla player**. Nothing else in this topic frames accompaniment as a contest, and that is why it is a reliable identification cue.

### 5. Introducing a character, and playing many

Two devices solve the same problem - how a danced performance tells you who is on stage - in opposite ways.

- **Kuchipudi's *daaru***: each principal character introduces itself on the stage with a *daaru*, a **small composition of dance and song specifically choreographed for the revelation of each character**. In a Kuchipudi recital the dancer **may combine the role of a singer** into themselves, which is why it becomes a **dance-drama** performance.
- **Bharatanatyam's *Ekcharya lasyam***: the style in which **one dancer plays multiple roles**.

The contrast is instructive. Kuchipudi's origin is a troupe form and it solves the identity problem by giving each character an entrance composition; Bharatanatyam's origin is solo performance and it solves the same problem by making a single body carry every role. Origin shapes device.

### 6. Stage, light and props

The most fully recorded staging in this topic is Kathakali's, and it is worth quoting because it is a complete performance environment rather than a costume note.

- **Minimal use of props**, but **very elaborate facial make-up along with a head gear** for different characters.
- Performance **in open-air theatres covered with coarse mats, or in temple premises** with the lush green trees of Kerala as a backdrop.
- **A brass lamp is used for lighting.**
- **The arrival of dawn, accompanied by a continuous sound of drums, *chenda* and *maddala*, marks the beginning and end of a recital** - which tells you the performance is an all-night event, and that its temporal frame is the night itself.

Set against that, two other staging records: Kuchipudi's **Tarangam**, in which the dancer performs with the **feet on the edges of a brass plate**, balancing a **pot of water on the head or a set of diyas**; and **Jala Chitra Nrityam**, in which the dancer **draws pictures on the floor with the toes while dancing**. Both are *solo elements*, and both convert the floor and the props into part of the technique rather than the scenery.

### 7. Solo, group and troupe - and why the distinction is examinable

| Organisation | Recorded instances |
|---|---|
| **Solo** | Mohiniyattam is essentially a **solo dance performance by women**; Bharatanatyam originated in the **solo dance performance of the temple dancers**; Chakyar Koothu is a **solo performance** |
| **Group** | Kuchipudi involves difficult foot movements and is **generally a team performance**; Sattriya is **generally performed in a group by male monks** |
| **All-male troupe** | Kathakali is **generally an all-male troupe performance** |
| **Changed over time** | Bharatanatyam was **once limited to solo female dancers** and **later gained popularity among men and groups**; Kuchipudi's solo format and female dancers were introduced by a named twentieth-century figure |

The last row is the important one. Performance organisation is not a fixed attribute of a form; it is a historical fact about a form at a time. An answer that writes "Bharatanatyam is a solo female dance" without a time index has made an error that the record itself corrects.

### Examination use

For Prelims, the four recital sequences in order, the named devices - *daaru*, *Ekcharya lasyam*, *Padhant*, *Jugalbandi*, *Gat Bhav*, *Tarangam*, *Jala Chitra Nrityam*, *Kelikottu*, *Todayam* - and the Kathakali staging block are all directly testable, and the devices are unusually good discriminators because each belongs to exactly one form.

For Mains, this lesson supplies the structural paragraph that most dance answers lack: Indian classical recitals share one architecture - invocation, interleaved movement and narrative and expression, and a closing of virtuosity or formal departure - and the differences between forms are differences of filling, not of design. That claim, evidenced from two or three recitals, converts a descriptive answer into an analytical one.

### Traps introduced here

- Reciting a sequence without knowing which item carries which element.
- Attributing *Jugalbandi* or *Padhant* to a form other than Kathak.
- Attributing *Tarangam* to Bharatanatyam; the brass-plate item is Kuchipudi's.
- Describing Kathakali's staging as elaborate scenery; props are minimal and the elaboration is in make-up and headgear.
- Writing a form's performance organisation without a time index.
- Forgetting that Odissi has **two** recorded ways of concluding.

### Revision notes

1. Shared architecture: **stage preparation -> invocation -> interleaved nritta/natya/nritya -> conclusion**.
2. **Bharatanatyam order: Alarippu, Jatiswaram, Shabdam, Varnam, Padam, Jawali, Thillana.**
3. **Odissi order: Mangalacharan -> Batu nritya -> Pallavi -> Tharijham -> Moksha OR Trikhanda majura.**
4. **Kathak elements: Ananda, Thaat, Todas and Tukdas, Jugalbandi, Padhant, Tarana, Kramalaya, Gat Bhav.**
5. **Kathakali frame: Kelikottu (attention) -> Todayam (blessings); dawn with continuous chenda and maddala marks both beginning and end.**
6. **Jugalbandi** = competitive play between dancer and tabla player; **Padhant** = dancer recites complicated *bols* and demonstrates them.
7. **Daaru** (Kuchipudi) = small dance-and-song composition revealing each character; **Ekcharya lasyam** (Bharatanatyam) = one dancer plays multiple roles.
8. **Tarangam** = feet on the edges of a brass plate, pot of water or diyas on the head; **Jala Chitra Nrityam** = drawing pictures on the floor with the toes. Both Kuchipudi solo elements.
9. Kathakali staging: **minimal props**, elaborate make-up and headgear, **open-air theatre with coarse mats** or temple premises, **brass lamp**.
10. Organisation is historical: Bharatanatyam moved from **solo female** to also **men and groups**; Kuchipudi acquired a **solo format and female dancers** in the twentieth century.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Compare the ways in which three classical forms solve the problem of telling an audience who is on stage. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The problem is real: danced performance has no dialogue to introduce a character and, in some settings, no scenery or programme either. Three forms solve it in three different ways, and each solution follows from the form's origin.

**Kuchipudi introduces.** Each principal character enters with a *daaru*, a small composition of dance and song choreographed for the revelation of that character, and the dancer may take on the singer's role - which is why the recital becomes a dance-drama. The solution fits a form whose first carriers were a travelling troupe with several performers to distinguish.

**Bharatanatyam multiplies.** The *Ekcharya lasyam* style has one dancer play multiple roles, so identity is carried by shifts within a single body rather than by separate entrances. The solution fits a form whose origin is solo temple performance.

**Kathakali paints.** With minimal props, open-air performance and a single brass lamp, a character must be legible on sight, so the five make-up types carry a colour code - green for nobility, divinity and virtue; red patches beside the nose for royalty; black for evil; yellow for saints and women; a wholly red face for evil; a white beard for higher consciousness. Identity is fixed before a gesture is made.

Verdict: introduction, multiplication and inscription are three answers to one question, and in each case the answer is predicted by the form's original performance setting rather than by taste.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the problem stated as a constraint of the medium 2 + the introducing solution with its device and its origin fit 4 + the multiplying solution with its named convention 3 + the inscribing solution with staging conditions and at least four colour meanings 5 + a verdict that ties solution to setting 1.**

### Concept check

**Question:** Bharatanatyam's recital moves from pure movement to expression and then back to pure movement at speed. What does this shape achieve, and which single item makes the design visible?

**Model answer:** The shape achieves **graded engagement**. The recital opens with an item of simple steps and rhythms that also discharges the social obligations of the occasion - respect to God, teacher and audience, and the seeking of blessings. It then establishes the movement vocabulary in a purely rhythmic item devoid of expression, so that the audience learns the physical language before meaning is loaded onto it. Only then does narrative enter through an item with expressed words, followed by the central item in which dance and emotion combine with rhythm and melody to tell a story, and then by an item of pure expressive mastery in which, as the record puts it, the music becomes light and the dance becomes emotional. Finally the load is lifted: a short love-lyric at a faster tempo, and a concluding item of pure dance with exuberant movement and intricate rhythmic variations. The item that makes the design visible is **Varnam**, the main part, because it is the only item in which rhythm, melody, dance and emotion are recorded as combining simultaneously - everything before it builds one of those components in isolation, and everything after it releases them. A recital is therefore a teaching sequence as much as a performance.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the concluding pure-dance item as an afterthought because it carries no expression. The closing item is where technical virtuosity is displayed at the point when the audience has been taught the vocabulary well enough to appreciate it. Its position is a consequence of the design, not a decline in seriousness.

## Lesson 8 - Who says a dance is "classical"? Three recognition systems, and what each one measures

Progress: 8/23 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the academy's eight, the ministry's nine, the international safeguarding list, the dance-related inscriptions owned here, a claimed-but-unrecognised form, and one dated current record

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the eight-form statement, the statement that one national academy recognises eight while the ministry recognises nine including Chhau, the two inscription records in the folk and Chhau sections, and the statement that one Bengal form has not yet been recognised
CA search: "latest Indian element inscribed on the UNESCO Representative List of the Intangible Cultural Heritage of Humanity, and the session at which it was inscribed"
CA found: Deepavali inscribed on the UNESCO Representative List of the Intangible Cultural Heritage of Humanity on 10 December 2025, at the twentieth session of the Intergovernmental Committee, held in New Delhi
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: three systems, three questions, three answers that do not substitute for each other

```text
   +--------------------------+--------------------------+-------------------------+
   |  NATIONAL DANCE ACADEMY  |  UNION CULTURE MINISTRY  |  INTERNATIONAL          |
   |  (Sangeet Natak Akademi) |                          |  SAFEGUARDING LIST      |
   |                          |                          |  (UNESCO ICH)           |
   +--------------------------+--------------------------+-------------------------+
   | QUESTION IT ANSWERS      | QUESTION IT ANSWERS      | QUESTION IT ANSWERS     |
   | Which forms does the     | Which forms does the     | Which living practices  |
   | national academy treat   | ministry list as         | does an international   |
   | as classical dance?      | classical dance?         | instrument recognise as |
   |                          |                          | heritage to be          |
   |                          |                          | safeguarded?            |
   +--------------------------+--------------------------+-------------------------+
   | ANSWER: EIGHT            | ANSWER: NINE             | ANSWER: a growing,      |
   | Bharatanatyam            | the same eight           | DATED list of elements  |
   | Kuchipudi                | PLUS CHHAU               | - not a dance canon      |
   | Kathakali                |                          | - not a ranking          |
   | Mohiniyattam             |                          | - includes non-dance    |
   | Odissi                   |                          |   elements              |
   | Manipuri                 |                          |                         |
   | Kathak                   |                          | Dance-related records   |
   | Sattriya                 |                          | owned here:             |
   |                          |                          |   Chhau        2010     |
   |                          |                          |   Kalbelia     2010     |
   |                          |                          |   Garba        2023     |
   +--------------------------+--------------------------+-------------------------+
   | NOT RECOGNISED BY IT: Gaudiya Nritya (Bengal) - claimed roots, a named modern  |
   | reconstruction, temple sculptural evidence, but NOT recognised by the academy  |
   +-------------------------------------------------------------------------------+

   THE RULE:  A form may hold ONE of these, BOTH, or NEITHER.
              None of the three is evidence about the others.
              None of the three is evidence about antiquity.
```

### 1. The learner's actual problem

"Classical" feels like a property of the dance. It is not. It is a **statement made by a body**, at a date, using that body's own criteria. Once that is accepted, three questions open up that a good answer must keep apart: *who is speaking*, *what are they classifying*, and *when did they say it*. Get those three right and the whole recognition portion of this topic becomes straightforward; merge them and the topic becomes a swamp.

### 2. The national academy's eight

Presently, as per the **Sangeet Natak Akademi**, there exist **eight classical dance forms** in India:

**Bharatanatyam, Kuchipudi, Kathakali, Mohiniyattam, Odissi, Manipuri, Kathak and Sattriya.**

Two facts about this list carry marks.

- **It is a list of forms, not a list of states**, even though every form has a regional association. Two of the eight - Kathakali and Mohiniyattam - belong to the same state, which is by itself enough to break the one-state-one-form assumption that option sets exploit.
- **It is an institutional act of the twentieth century**, layered onto much older and socially varied regional practices. The list does not date the practices, and a form's social history should never be assumed to begin with its recognition.

⚠️ **One year, recorded as reported rather than verified here.** Sattriya's admission to this eight-form list is widely reported to have occurred in **2000**. A bounded check for this session did not reach a primary academy record confirming that year, so it is stated here as **reported**, and no answer in this session depends on it. Write it, if at all, as "reported as recognised in 2000" - and prefer, for a Mains answer, the analytically stronger statement that **the list itself has been added to within living memory**, which is what makes it visibly institutional rather than ancient.

### 3. The ministry's nine

The record is explicit and it is one of the most reliable close-option facts in the topic:

> Till date, the Sangeet Natak Akademi has recognised **eight** classical dance forms, whereas the **Ministry of Culture** has recognised **nine** classical dance forms, **including Chhau**.

❌ **Do not merge the two lists.** They are two institutional statements by two bodies. A question that asks how many classical dance forms there are in India has a defective premise, and the creditable answer names both counts and both bodies. A question that asks whether Chhau is a classical dance form is answered by: it is included in the ministry's nine and is not among the academy's eight.

### 4. The international safeguarding list

The third system is entirely different in kind. It is an **international, treaty-based safeguarding instrument**, and inscription on it is not a judgement that a practice is classical, refined, old or superior. It is a decision that a living practice is heritage worth safeguarding. Three consequences:

1. **The list is not a dance list.** It contains elements from every domain of intangible heritage - festive practices, rituals, craft knowledge, oral traditions - and dance forms are only one part of it.
2. **It reaches practices the national classical canon does not.** The dance-related Indian inscriptions that this session covers are **Chhau (2010)**, **Kalbelia folk songs and dances of Rajasthan (2010)** and **Garba of Gujarat (2023)**. Chhau is not among the academy's eight; Kalbelia and Garba are folk forms. Recognition here runs on entirely different tracks from classical status.
3. **It is dated and it grows.** Every claim about what is "latest" on this list has a shelf life, and a question that asks for the latest inclusion is asking a **time-stamped** question whose correct answer changes.

✅ **Verified status record, stated once.** *Garba of Gujarat* was inscribed on the Representative List of the Intangible Cultural Heritage of Humanity in **2023**, at the **eighteenth session** of the Intergovernmental Committee, held at **Kasane, Botswana**. Some preparation material describes 2023 as the year of nomination; the inscription decision itself belongs to that session and year.

📰 **The one current record this session states.** On **10 December 2025**, **Deepavali** was inscribed on the same Representative List at the **twentieth session** of the Intergovernmental Committee, **held in New Delhi** - the first time India hosted that session. It is recorded as India's **sixteenth** element on that list, inscribed in the domain of **social practices, rituals and festive events**, and its nomination dossier was prepared by the **Sangeet Natak Akademi** under the **Ministry of Culture**.

Three things make this a genuine linkage for a dance topic rather than a decorative one.

- **It moves a time-stamped answer.** Any statement of the form "the latest Indian inclusion on the list is X" that was correct before December 2025 is no longer correct. This is the clearest available demonstration that status claims in this topic must carry a date.
- **It shows that the list is not a dance canon.** The inscribed element is a festival, not a dance form. A candidate who has been treating the international list as a ranking of dances now has a counter-example that cannot be argued away.
- **It shows that one institution operates on both tracks.** The same national academy that identifies eight classical dance forms prepared the nomination dossier for a festive practice under an international safeguarding instrument. The institution overlaps; the two kinds of recognition do not.

⚠️ Totals and "latest" claims belong to the heritage and cultural-policy topic, with its own verification date. This session states the record above and asserts nothing beyond it - no running total of Indian elements, no prediction of future inscriptions, and no claim about any element this topic does not own.

⚠️ **One correction to a tempting shortcut.** Do not describe the Indian entries on that list as splitting cleanly into "dance" and "non-dance". Two Indian elements name performance in which dance is constitutive: ***Sankirtana*, ritual singing, drumming and dancing of Manipur (2013)** - a practice this session meets again in Lesson 16, where it is recorded as influenced by Manipuri dance, with its musical and devotional treatment belonging to the music topic - and ***Mudiyettu*, ritual theatre and dance drama of Kerala (2010)**, which this session does **not** teach and which is recorded as an open gap in the final ledger. The lesson for an answer is that the instrument reaches **composite ritual performance**, not a tidy category of dances, which is a further reason it cannot be read as a classical canon.

### 5. A form that holds none of the three: Gaudiya Nritya

The clearest way to see that recognition is a decision rather than a description is to look at a form that has everything except the decision.

**Gaudiya Nritya** is a Bengali classical dance whose name traces its origin to **Gaur**, once the capital city of Bengal. It is **believed** to have its roots in the *Natya Shastra*. During the Bhakti movement, it **is said that Chaitanya Mahaprabhu revived** it. Sculptures of this dance form have been noted on the walls of many temples of Bengal. The form was later **reconstructed by Mahua Mukherjee**. **However, this dance has not yet been recognised by the Sangeet Natak Akademi as a classical dance form.**

Notice the hedged verbs in the record - *believed*, *is said*, *noted* - and keep them in your own writing. What the case establishes is structural: claimed treatise roots, a claimed historical revival, physical sculptural evidence on temple walls, and a named modern reconstruction can all be present, and the form can still sit outside the academy's list. Recognition is therefore **not** a summary of a form's history, evidence or quality. It is a separate act.

### 6. The synthesis to carry into every answer

| If the question asks | Answer with |
|---|---|
| How many classical dance forms are there in India? | The academy recognises **eight**; the Ministry of Culture recognises **nine, including Chhau**; name both bodies |
| Is Chhau a classical dance? | It is in the **ministry's nine** and **not in the academy's eight** |
| Is Garba a classical dance? | No - it is a **folk** dance; it holds a **UNESCO inscription (2023)**, which is a different kind of recognition entirely |
| Does UNESCO inscription make a dance classical? | No - the international list is a **safeguarding instrument**, not a classical canon, and it contains non-dance elements |
| Why is Gaudiya Nritya not among the eight? | Because recognition is an **institutional decision**, and this form has claimed roots, sculptural evidence and a named reconstruction but **no academy recognition** |
| What is the latest Indian inclusion on the international list? | A **date-stamped** question; state the record with its date and say that the answer changes |

### Examination use

This is the most directly Prelims-productive lesson in the session. The eight names, the eight-versus-nine distinction with both bodies named, Chhau as the ninth, the three dance-related inscriptions with their years, the Gaudiya Nritya non-recognition and the session's one dated current record are all single-fact testable.

For Mains, the transferable argument is the **recognition thesis**: the identification of a fixed number of classical forms is a twentieth-century institutional act, and a form's social history should never be assumed to begin with its recognition. That sentence, followed by the Gaudiya Nritya counter-case and the Chhau eight-versus-nine discrepancy, is a complete analytical paragraph that fits any question on classification, safeguarding or cultural policy in this topic.

### Traps introduced here

- Merging the academy's eight and the ministry's nine into a single number.
- Treating an international inscription as a classical-status award, or the reverse.
- Assuming a dance form must hold both recognitions or neither; it may hold one, both or neither.
- Writing an undated "latest inclusion" claim.
- Asserting that Gaudiya Nritya is a classical dance because of its claimed treatise roots and temple sculptures.
- Assuming one state maps to one classical form; Kerala holds two of the eight.

### Revision notes

1. **Sangeet Natak Akademi: eight classical dance forms** - Bharatanatyam, Kuchipudi, Kathakali, Mohiniyattam, Odissi, Manipuri, Kathak, Sattriya.
2. **Ministry of Culture: nine, including Chhau.** Do not merge the two lists.
3. The eight-form list is a **twentieth-century institutional act**, not an ancient classification.
4. **Sattriya's recognition year is reported as 2000**; treat as reported, not verified here.
5. UNESCO ICH is a **treaty-based safeguarding instrument**, not a classical canon, and contains **non-dance** elements.
6. Dance-related Indian ICH records owned here: **Chhau 2010; Kalbelia folk songs and dances 2010; Garba of Gujarat 2023** (eighteenth session, Kasane, Botswana).
7. 📰 **Deepavali inscribed 10 December 2025** at the **twentieth session, held in New Delhi**; India's **sixteenth** element; domain **social practices, rituals and festive events**; dossier prepared by the **Sangeet Natak Akademi** under the **Ministry of Culture**.
8. **Gaudiya Nritya**: name from **Gaur**; believed *Natya Shastra* roots; said to have been revived by **Chaitanya Mahaprabhu**; temple sculptures in Bengal; reconstructed by **Mahua Mukherjee**; **not recognised** by the academy.
9. A form may hold **one, both or neither** recognition; neither is evidence about antiquity.
10. Every "latest", "total" or "how many so far" claim in this area is **time-stamped** and belongs to the heritage topic with its own verification date.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Evaluate international intangible-heritage inscription as an instrument for India's dance traditions. (20 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The instrument is valuable, and it is valuable precisely because it does something a national classical canon does not do.

**What it reaches.** Its Indian dance-related records are a mask-and-martial tradition inscribed in 2010, a community folk tradition of Rajasthan inscribed in the same year, and a mass participatory Navratri dance inscribed in 2023. None is among the national academy's eight. An instrument that reaches a tradition practised in three states, a hereditary community's song and dance, and a festival danced by hundreds is reaching living practice rather than a canon.

**What it is not.** It is not a classical designation and confers no rank. It also does not divide neatly into dance and non-dance: elements have been inscribed in which dance is constitutive of composite ritual performance - a Manipur practice of ritual singing, drumming and dancing in 2013, and a Kerala ritual theatre and dance drama in 2010.

**Its limits as evidence.** Every claim of the form "the latest Indian inclusion" is time-stamped and expires; the element inscribed in December 2025 is a festival, not a dance. Inscription also says nothing about antiquity, continuity or technical demand.

**Its limits as protection.** Inscription is recognition, not funding, occasion or transmission, and this topic's own history shows that traditions fail when occasion and carriers fail, not when recognition is withheld.

Verdict: a useful visibility and safeguarding instrument, wholly unsuited to any use as a ranking or a chronology.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = the contrast with a national canon stated as the evaluative frame 2 + three dated dance-related records with the observation that none is classical 6 + the dance-inclusive composite elements with two dated instances 4 + the time-stamping limit with the December 2025 case 4 + the recognition-is-not-protection limit tied to this topic's own decline mechanisms 3 + a verdict separating proper from improper use 1.**

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "Chhau and Garba are both UNESCO-recognised classical dances of India." How many distinct errors does the sentence contain, and what is the correct formulation?

**Model answer:** Three distinct errors, plus one silent assumption. **First**, it fuses two recognition systems: the international list is a safeguarding instrument and does not confer classical status on anything, so "UNESCO-recognised classical" is a category error rather than a factual slip. **Second**, it misclassifies Garba, which is a **folk** dance performed during Navratri and is not on any classical list - its inscription in 2023 says something about its status as living heritage worth safeguarding, and nothing about a classical canon. **Third**, it flattens Chhau, whose position is genuinely split: it is **included in the Ministry of Culture's nine** classical dance forms and is **not among the Sangeet Natak Akademi's eight**, and it separately holds a 2010 inscription. The silent assumption is that recognition is a single ladder on which a form occupies one rung. A correct formulation: "Chhau holds a 2010 inscription on the UNESCO Representative List and is included in the Ministry of Culture's list of nine classical dance forms, though not in the Sangeet Natak Akademi's eight. Garba, a Gujarati folk dance of Navratri, was inscribed on the same Representative List in 2023; inscription is a safeguarding recognition and is not a classical designation."

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that because both bodies are official, their statements are about the same thing. A national arts academy identifying a canon of classical forms and an international convention inscribing living heritage for safeguarding are answering different questions with different criteria. The clearest proof is that the same list which carries a mask dance and two folk dances also carries a festival of lights.

## Lesson 9 - Transmission: guru-shishya parampara, sampradaya, gharana - and what the sources do not record

Progress: 9/23 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the transmission principle common to all classical forms, the plurality it produces, the four Kathak gharanas, the monastic and community carriers, transmission by substitution, and an explicitly declared gap

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the guru-shishya statement and the sampradaya formulation, the four Kathak gharanas with their patrons and characteristics, the monastic carrier record for one Assamese form, the mahari-gotipua substitution record, and the named community carriers in the folk section
CA search: "Not applicable - transmission-structure lesson; the session's single current record is in Lesson 8"
CA found: None found; none claimed
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: one principle, six carrier structures

```text
                 THE PRINCIPLE, STATED IN THE SOURCES
   "True transfer of knowledge can only come through a GURU. The guru
    passes on the knowledge of the different traditions - SAMPRADAYAS -
    onto the disciple. This GURU-SHISHYA PARAMPARA forms the core of
    Indian classical art form."
                                  |
        +-------------+-----------+-----------+-------------+-----------+
        v             v           v           v             v           v
   HEREDITARY    TRAVELLING   ALL-MALE    HOUSEHOLD     MONASTIC    COMMUNITY
   TEMPLE-       TROUPE       TROUPE      LINEAGE       ORDER       CARRIER
   ATTACHED                               (gharana)
   PERFORMER
   devadasi /    Kusselavas   Kathakali   KATHAK:       SATTRIYA:   Chakyar
   Sadir;        (Kuchipudi)  troupes     Lucknow       Bhokots,    (Koothu)
   mahari                                 Jaipur        male monks  Nalike
   (Odissi)                               Raigarh       in sattras  (Aati Kalenja)
                                          Banaras                   Kalbelia
                                                                    Watal (Dumhal)
        |                                                           Bedia (Rai)
        v                                                           Keipeng
   WHEN A CARRIER FAILS: TRANSMISSION BY SUBSTITUTION                Muria, Rathwa,
   mahari system becomes defunct -> young boys dressed as females    Bodo, Yadav
   continue the art as GOTIPUAS; 'Nartala' continues at royal courts
```

### 1. The principle, and the plurality it explains

Distinct styles of dance have evolved in different regions of India, each with its own specific nuances. However, **all these dance forms are governed by the basic rules and guidelines laid down in the *Natya Shastra***, the principal rule being that **true transfer of knowledge can only come through a guru**. The guru passes on the knowledge of the different traditions - ***sampradayas*** - onto the disciple. This ***guru-shishya parampara*** forms the core of the Indian classical art form.

Read that as a two-part claim, because it resolves an apparent contradiction that candidates find confusing.

- **The rules are shared.** All the forms are governed by the same treatise tradition. That is why the same analytical vocabulary works on all eight.
- **The transmission is plural.** What is transmitted is a *sampradaya* - a particular tradition - and different gurus carry different ones. That is why eight forms, and multiple styles within one form, can all be governed by one treatise tradition without becoming identical.

The single sentence that captures it: **a shared grammar, transmitted through plural lineages, produces regional distinctiveness without doctrinal divergence.**

### 2. Why a person, and not a book?

It is worth pausing on why transmission is organised around a guru rather than around a text, because a Mains answer that can explain this has something to say beyond description.

Three reasons follow from earlier lessons. First, what is being transmitted is **embodied**: an inventory of gestures, movement units and whole-body postures that cannot be executed correctly from a verbal description alone. Second, the object of the art is an **emotional effect in an audience**, and whether that effect has been achieved is a judgement, which requires a judge - the guru is that judge. Third, the vocabulary is **fine-grained by design**, and fine distinctions degrade quickly without correction. A living corrector is the mechanism that keeps a codified art from drifting.

The cost of this design is equally clear and is the theme of Lesson 22: a tradition held in persons is **vulnerable in a way that a tradition held in books is not**. When the carrier community is dismantled, the repertoire goes with it unless something substitutes.

### 3. The four Kathak gharanas

The *gharana* - a lineage-school - is the household form of the transmission principle, and in this topic it is a **Kathak phenomenon specifically**, arising because Kathak is based on the Hindustani style of music.

| Gharana | Patron or founder | Recorded emphasis |
|---|---|---|
| **Lucknow** | reached its peak under the reign of **Nawab Wajid Ali Shah** | more importance on **expression and grace** |
| **Jaipur** | initiated by **Bhanuji** | **fluency, speed and long rhythmic patterns** |
| **Raigarh** | developed under the patronage of **Raja Chakradhar Singh** | unique in its emphasis on **percussion music** |
| **Banaras** | developed under **Janakiprasad** | greater use of the **floor**; special emphasis on **symmetry** |

Three observations that raise this from a memory table to an argument.

1. **Each gharana is attached to a named patron or founder.** Style here is not an abstract preference; it is what a particular household developed under a particular patron. Two of the four are attached to rulers, one to a named initiator and one to a named individual.
2. **The emphases are genuinely different axes.** Expression, speed, percussion and spatial symmetry are four distinct things to optimise. This is a real differentiation, not four ways of saying "excellent".
3. **The gharana is a Kathak structure in this topic.** ❌ Do not apply gharana labels to Bharatanatyam, Odissi, Kuchipudi or the rest merely because those forms also have lineages. They have *sampradayas* and named gurus; they are not described here through gharanas.

### 4. What this session does not record - stated openly

⚠️ Bharatanatyam is commonly discussed in terms of named stylistic schools, sometimes called *banis*. **The sources used for this session do not record any *bani* names for Bharatanatyam or for any other form.** This session therefore does not supply them, and a candidate should not invent them.

What *can* be said, and is fully supported, is the structural point: the transmission principle explicitly carries **different traditions - sampradayas - through different gurus**, so stylistic plurality inside one named form is exactly what the system is designed to produce. In Bharatanatyam specifically, the recorded instances of that plurality are the **four Thanjavur teachers known as the Tanjore Quartet**, under whom the form also came to be known as **Tanjore Natyam**, and the modern institutional line associated with the named twentieth-century figures in Lesson 10. Write that, and leave the unnamed schools unnamed. An examiner rewards a candidate who says "the record supports stylistic plurality through sampradaya transmission" and penalises one who invents four school names.

### 5. The monastic carrier, and why it is different

Sattriya's carrier is not a household and not a troupe. The art form derives its name from the Vaishnava monasteries known as ***Sattras***, where it was primarily practised, and the dance is **generally performed in a group by male monks known as *Bhokots*** as part of their **daily rituals** or on festivals.

Three differences from every other carrier in this topic follow, and each is examinable.

- **The occasion is daily, not periodic.** Where a court form performs when a patron commands and a temple form at ritual occasions, this tradition is embedded in a routine.
- **The institution outlives the individual by design.** A monastery recruits; a household must reproduce. That is a genuinely different survival mechanism.
- **The repertoire is institutionally authored.** The songs are compositions of the founding saint, known as **Borgeets**. There is no equivalent in this topic of a repertoire so directly tied to one named author within one institution.

### 6. Transmission by substitution: the mahari-gotipua case

The most instructive carrier event in the topic is Odissi's. The form was **primarily practised by the *maharis***, and with the advent of Vaishnavism in the region **the mahari system became defunct**. Instead, **young boys were recruited and dressed as females to continue the art form**; they came to be known as ***gotipuas***. Another variant of the art, ***Nartala***, continued to be practised at the royal courts.

This single sequence teaches four things that recur everywhere in the topic.

1. **A carrier can fail while the art survives** - if a substitute carrier is found.
2. **Substitution changes the art.** The Gotipua tradition, originating from the Mahari tradition around the **sixteenth century**, features **acrobatic and yogic movements** and is recorded as a **precursor to classical Odissi**. The substitute did not merely preserve; it developed its own idiom, which then fed back into the modern form.
3. **Parallel survival is possible.** *Nartala* continued at the royal courts at the same time, so the art had two carriers running in parallel with different patrons.
4. **A religious change can be a patronage event.** The advent of Vaishnavism is recorded here as what made the mahari system defunct - and in Kuchipudi's case the advent of Vaishnavism is recorded as what made that dance the monopoly of male Brahmins performed at temples. The same religious movement reorganised who could perform in two different regions.

### 7. The community carrier in the living field

Outside the classical eight, the carrier is very often a **named community** rather than a lineage or an institution, and naming it is what converts a folk-dance answer from a list into an analysis. Instances recorded in this topic include the **Chakyar** community for Chakyar Koothu, the **Nalike** community for Aati Kalenja, the **Kalbelia** community of Rajasthan, the **Kamar** tribe for Tera Tali, the **Bhil** tribe for Ghoomar, the **Watal** tribe for Dumhal, the **Bedia** tribe for Rai Nritya, the **Muria** tribes of Bastar for Gaur Muria, the **Rathwa** community for Rathwa ni Gher, the **Bodo** tribe for Bagurumba, the **Yadav** community for Raut Nacha, the **Tripuri** community for Garia and Lebang Boomani, and the **Keipeng** community for Meladom. Lessons 19 and 20 develop the field; the transmission point is made here because it is a transmission point: **in the living field, the community is the institution**.

### Examination use

For Prelims, the guru-shishya and sampradaya formulation, the four Kathak gharanas with patrons and emphases, the Bhokot and sattra terms, the mahari-gotipua-Nartala sequence with the sixteenth-century dating of Gotipua, and the community-to-dance pairs are all directly testable. The Kathak gharana table in particular is a ready-made four-pair matching item.

For Mains, this lesson supplies two arguments. The first explains **why** the art is transmitted through persons - embodiment, judgement and fine distinction - and therefore why it is vulnerable. The second, the mahari-gotipua case, is the single best compressed evidence in the topic that traditions survive by **changing their carriers**, which is the analytical core of any question on continuity and change in Indian performing arts.

### Traps introduced here

- Applying gharana vocabulary to forms other than Kathak in this topic.
- Inventing named stylistic schools for Bharatanatyam.
- Swapping the Kathak gharana emphases - expression and grace, speed and rhythm, percussion, and floor use with symmetry belong to four specific gharanas.
- Treating the gotipua tradition as a mere stopgap rather than as a tradition with its own idiom and its own name.
- Forgetting that Vaishnavism appears in this topic as a **patronage and access** event in two different regions, with opposite consequences for who performed.
- Omitting the carrier community when describing a folk form.

### Revision notes

1. Principle: **true transfer of knowledge only through a guru**; the guru passes on **sampradayas**; this is **guru-shishya parampara**, the core of the classical art form.
2. All the classical forms are governed by the rules laid down in the ***Natya Shastra***; plurality comes from plural lineages, not from divergent doctrine.
3. Kathak gharanas: **Lucknow** (peak under **Nawab Wajid Ali Shah**; expression and grace); **Jaipur** (initiated by **Bhanuji**; fluency, speed, long rhythmic patterns); **Raigarh** (patronage of **Raja Chakradhar Singh**; percussion); **Banaras** (**Janakiprasad**; floor use and symmetry).
4. The gharana is a **Kathak** structure in this topic, arising from its Hindustani musical base.
5. **No *bani* or school names are recorded** in this session's sources; do not supply them.
6. **Sattras** = Vaishnava monasteries; **Bhokots** = the male monks who perform Sattriya as part of daily rituals or at festivals; **Borgeets** = the founding saint's compositions.
7. Odissi carrier sequence: **maharis -> (Vaishnavism; mahari system defunct) -> gotipuas (boys dressed as females) -> Nartala in parallel at royal courts**.
8. **Gotipua** = 'single boy'; from the Mahari tradition around the **sixteenth century**; **acrobatic and yogic movements**; a **precursor to classical Odissi**.
9. Vaishnavism also made **Kuchipudi** the monopoly of **male Brahmins**, performed at temples, with dancers called **Bhagavathalus**.
10. In the living field, the **community is the institution**: Chakyar, Nalike, Kalbelia, Kamar, Bhil, Watal, Bedia, Muria, Rathwa, Bodo, Yadav, Tripuri, Keipeng.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Explain why Indian classical dance is transmitted through a person rather than through a text, and state the cost of that design. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

Three reasons make a living teacher necessary. The knowledge is **embodied** - inventories of gesture, movement unit and whole-body posture that cannot be executed correctly from description. The object is an **effect in an audience**, and whether it has been achieved is a judgement, which requires a judge. And the vocabulary is **fine-grained by design**, so small errors compound quickly without correction.

The sources state the principle: true transfer of knowledge comes only through a guru, who passes on a *sampradaya*. That also explains plurality - one treatise tradition, many lineages, hence regional distinctiveness without doctrinal divergence.

The cost is exposure. A tradition held in persons dies with the persons. When one temple-attached practitioner system became defunct, the art survived only because boys were recruited to continue it; when another community's position was dismantled, the form nearly vanished.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = three reasons for personal transmission, each argued 5 + the guru and sampradaya principle with the plurality consequence 3 + the cost stated with two named survival cases 2.**

### Concept check

**Question:** The mahari system became defunct and boys dressed as females continued the art as gotipuas. Why is this a better answer to "how do Indian performing traditions survive disruption?" than the twentieth-century revival stories that dominate this topic?

**Model answer:** Because it is a **survival mechanism rather than a rescue narrative**, and it is evidenced end to end. The sequence contains a cause - the advent of Vaishnavism in the region, which made the existing carrier system defunct; a substitution - young boys recruited and dressed as females to continue the art form, acquiring their own name; a parallel channel - the *Nartala* variant continuing at the royal courts under different patronage; and a consequence - the substitute tradition, with its acrobatic and yogic movements, becoming a precursor to the classical form as it is now practised. Nothing in that chain requires a rescuer. The art survived because a different set of bodies was found to carry it and because a second channel existed under different patrons. The twentieth-century revival stories are true and are taught later in this session, but they arrive with a built-in temptation to credit individuals with saving something, which flattens the more interesting question of what the carriers were doing in the meantime. The gotipua case answers that question directly, and it also supplies the qualification that every revival answer needs: **a substituted carrier does not reproduce the original; it develops an idiom of its own, which then becomes part of what is later called the tradition.**

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the gotipua tradition as a temporary male stand-in for an absent female tradition. It is recorded as a tradition in its own right, with its own name, its own acrobatic and yogic movement idiom, its own dating from around the sixteenth century, and its own status as a precursor to the classical form. Treating a substitute carrier as a placeholder is how the actual history of a form gets erased.

   |                            |

   CLOCK                        |

   |                            |

   |                    later popular among MEN AND GROUPS
   |
