```text
   CLOCK                elements of the recital
   |                    the TANJORE QUARTET: Chinnaiah, Ponniah, Vadivelu,
   |                    Sivanandam
   |                            |
   |            Alarippu -> Jatiswaram -> Shabdam -> Varnam -> Padam ->
   |                    Jawali -> Thillana
   |
   GRAMMAR       ...... ADAVU (steps) + HASTAMUDRA (hand gestures) +
   CLOCK                BHAVABHINAYA (facial expressions)
                        equal emphasis on TANDAVA and LASYA; major emphasis
                        on MUDRAS; knees mostly bent, weight even on both feet
                        EKCHARYA LASYAM: one dancer plays multiple roles
                        element symbolised: FIRE
```

### 1. Name, etymology and the alternative names

**Bharatanatyam**, described as the **oldest classical dance**, is named after **Bharata Muni** and ***natyam***, dance in Tamil. Some link '**Bharata**' to **Bhava** (expression), **Raga** (melody) and **Taal** (rhythm) - an etymological reading rather than a historical derivation, and worth quoting as such because it compresses three of the form's constituents into its name.

Two alternative names must be held with their contexts, because both are examinable and they belong to different periods:

- ***Dashiattam*** - the alternative name arising from the Sadir tradition of the temple dancers.
- ***Tanjore Natyam*** - the name under which the form also came to be known **under the four Thanjavur teachers**.

### 2. The Sadir origin, stated exactly

The origin of this dance form can be traced back to ***Sadir*** - the **solo dance performance of the temple dancers, or *devadasis*, in Tamil Nadu**, hence it was also referred to as ***Dashiattam***.

Three precise facts sit inside that sentence and all three are discriminating:

- the origin is **solo**, not troupe;
- the performers were **temple-attached**, not court-employed;
- the tradition has its own name, ***Sadir***, distinct from the modern name of the form.

❌ **Do not write that Bharatanatyam passed unchanged from an ancient temple origin to the modern stage.** Sadir repertoires, hereditary practitioners, the disruption of the devadasi system, and twentieth-century reconfiguration and revival all shaped the present form. The temple-to-stage sentence is the single most penalised formulation in this topic, and Lesson 22 explains exactly why.

### 3. Decline, near-disappearance and reconstruction

**With the decline of the Devadasi system, Bharatanatyam nearly vanished** but was **revived by E. Krishna Iyer (1897-1968)**. **Once limited to solo female dancers, it later gained popularity among men and groups.** **Rukmini Devi Arundale further gave it global recognition.**

Four analytical points, in the order an answer should make them.

1. **"Nearly vanished" is a strong recorded claim and should be used.** It establishes that the disruption was real and not merely a change of fashion.
2. **Two named actors, two different functions.** One is credited with the revival; the other with global recognition. Keeping the functions distinct is more accurate and more impressive than listing two names.
3. **The performer demography changed twice** - from a temple-attached hereditary tradition, to a reconstructed form limited to solo female dancers, to a form popular among men and groups. Any sentence about who performs Bharatanatyam therefore needs a time index.
4. ⚠️ **The reconstruction rested on knowledge held by the displaced.** Revival did not create the form from nothing; it reorganised and publicised repertoires transmitted by hereditary performers and teachers, at the very moment when the social position of those performers was being dismantled by **anti-nautch** campaigning and, eventually, by statutory prohibition of dedication - the mechanism is set out in Lesson 22, section 2. An answer that presents revival as pure recovery omits the displaced; an answer that presents the revivalists as inventors omits the continuity. Both halves belong in the sentence.

### 4. The Tanjore Quartet and the recital order

In the **early nineteenth century, four dance teachers of Thanjavur defined the elements of a Bharatanatyam recital**. The four, known as the **'Tanjore Quartet'**, are **Chinnaiah, Ponniah, Vadivelu and Sivanandam**, and under them Bharatanatyam also came to be known as **Tanjore Natyam**.

| Item | Recorded description |
|---|---|
| **Alarippu** (flowering bud) | opening piece with simple dance steps and rhythms, showing respect to God, the teacher and the audience, and seeking blessings |
| **Jatiswaram** | the **Nritta** component, devoid of expressions, including different poses and movements |
| **Shabdam** | the **dramatic** element with expressed words, including the abhinaya in the song; generally performed in praise of the glory of God |
| **Varnam** | **Nritya** is the main part; dance and emotions combine with *tala* (rhythm) and *raag* (melody) to tell a story |
| **Padam** | mastery over the **abhinaya** of the spiritual message; music becomes light, dance becomes emotional |
| **Jawali** | short **love-lyrics** performed at a **faster tempo** |
| **Thillana** | the concluding stage; **pure dance (Nritta)** with exuberant movement and intricate rhythmic variations |

⚠️ **A cross-link worth holding, because it is also a trap.** **Vadivelu**, one of the four Thanjavur teachers, is the same name recorded as having **further developed Mohiniyattam in the nineteenth century**. One named individual therefore appears in the recorded histories of two different classical forms in two different states. That is a genuine connection - nineteenth-century Thanjavur expertise reaching Travancore - and it is also an invitation to misattribute. Keep the two statements separate and state both.

### 5. Technique, stance and elemental association

- Bharatanatyam is often referred to as the **'fire dance'**, as it is the **manifestation of fire in the human body**.
- It uses **Adavu (steps)**, **Hastamudra (hand gestures)** and **Bhavabhinaya (facial expressions)**.
- **Performances start with the worship of Ganapati and Nataraja.**
- **Equal emphasis is given to both the Tandava and Lasya aspects**, with major emphasis on **mudras**.
- One of the principal mudras is **Katakamukha Hasta**, in which **three fingers are joined to symbolise 'Om'**.
- In a recital, the **knees are mostly bent** and the **weight is equally distributed across both the feet**.
- It features the **Ekcharya lasyam** style, where **one dancer plays multiple roles**.

The elemental associations are a five-form set in this topic and are best learned together rather than singly: **Bharatanatyam - fire; Kuchipudi - earth; Kathakali - sky or ether; Mohiniyattam - air; Odissi - water.** Note that the set covers five of the eight forms and that no elemental association is recorded here for Kathak, Manipuri or Sattriya. ❌ Do not invent the missing three.

### 6. Costume and music

- **Costume of men**: jacket and pajamas of **Kanchipuram silk**.
- **Costume of women**: made of **Benarasi silk**.
- **Key instruments**: **mridangam, veena and flute**.

❌ **Do not supply costume symbolism.** The record states materials, not meanings. A sentence such as "the red of the costume signifies devotion" is exactly the kind of invented detail that a well-prepared examiner is looking for.

⚠️ Note also a cross-form overlap in costume vocabulary: **Benarasi** is recorded both for Bharatanatyam's female costume and for the female costume in **Kuchipudi**. A costume material is therefore a weak discriminator between these two forms; use origin, repertoire and speech instead.

### 7. Proponents

Famous proponents recorded for this form: **Yamini Krishnamurthy, Lakshmi Viswanathan, Padma Subramanyam, Mrinalini Sarabhai, Mallika Sarabhai, Alarmel Valli**.

⚠️ Two cautions, both of which have been tested. First, **Yamini Krishnamurthy** appears in the recorded proponent lists of **both Bharatanatyam and Kuchipudi**, so she is not a discriminator between them. Second, ❌ **no award, title or honour is recorded here for any dancer named in this session.** Do not supply one. A fabricated honour is a factual error that the examiner can check in a second, and it costs more than the sentence was worth.

### 8. How to tell it from its neighbours

| Confused with | The discriminating feature |
|---|---|
| **Mohiniyattam** | Bharatanatyam has strong rhythmic footwork; Mohiniyattam is marked by a **marked absence of thumping** of footsteps and gentle footwork |
| **Kuchipudi** | Bharatanatyam's origin is **solo temple performance**; Kuchipudi's origin is **itinerant dance-drama**, its dancers may speak, and its recital is in **Telugu** |
| **Odissi** | Both use mudras and postures to express emotions, but Odissi adds the **torso-led tribhanga and chowk** with a largely static lower body |
| **Kathak** | Bharatanatyam's idiom is the **bent-knee, evenly weighted stance**; Kathak's is **intricate footwork and pirouettes** with an upright line |

### Examination use

This is the highest-density identification lesson in the session. For Prelims: the etymology and both alternative names; Sadir, devadasi and Dashiattam; E. Krishna Iyer with his dates and Rukmini Devi Arundale with her distinct function; the four Thanjavur teachers by name; the seven recital items in order with their elements; the fire association; the technique triad; Katakamukha Hasta; the bent-knee even-weight stance; Ekcharya lasyam; the two costume silks; the three instruments; and the proponent list.

For Mains, this form is the standard case study for the **reconstruction thesis**: the eight classical forms as we know them are the outcome of twentieth-century reconstruction and institutional recognition acting on much older regional practices, so continuity is real and so is the break. Bharatanatyam evidences both halves within one form, which is why it is the example to reach for whenever a question asks about change, revival or continuity in Indian performing arts.

### Traps introduced here

- The temple-to-stage narrative with no disruption in it.
- Crediting the revival to a single individual, or crediting the revivalists with inventing the form.
- Writing "a solo female dance" without a time index.
- Attributing the seven-item recital order to the ancient tradition rather than to four named early-nineteenth-century teachers.
- Assigning Vadivelu to only one of the two forms in whose history he appears.
- Using Yamini Krishnamurthy or a Benarasi costume as a Bharatanatyam-versus-Kuchipudi discriminator.
- Inventing costume symbolism, elemental associations for the three forms that have none recorded, or honours for a named dancer.

### Revision notes

1. **Bharatanatyam** - Tamil Nadu; named after **Bharata Muni** + ***natyam***; described as the **oldest classical dance**; 'Bharata' also read as **Bha**va + **Ra**ga + **Ta**al.
2. Alternative names: ***Dashiattam*** (from the Sadir/devadasi tradition) and ***Tanjore Natyam*** (under the Thanjavur teachers).
3. Origin: ***Sadir*** - the **solo** dance performance of the **devadasis** of Tamil Nadu.
4. Decline of the devadasi system -> the form **nearly vanished** -> **revived by E. Krishna Iyer (1897-1968)**; **Rukmini Devi Arundale** gave it **global recognition**.
5. Demography: **once limited to solo female dancers**, later popular **among men and groups**.
6. **Early nineteenth century**: four Thanjavur teachers defined the recital - the **Tanjore Quartet: Chinnaiah, Ponniah, Vadivelu, Sivanandam**.
7. **Recital order: Alarippu, Jatiswaram, Shabdam, Varnam, Padam, Jawali, Thillana.**
8. **'Fire dance'** - manifestation of **fire** in the human body.
9. Technique: **Adavu, Hastamudra, Bhavabhinaya**; opens with worship of **Ganapati and Nataraja**; **equal Tandava and Lasya**, major emphasis on mudras; **Katakamukha Hasta** = three fingers joined symbolising 'Om'.
10. Stance: **knees mostly bent, weight equally distributed on both feet**; style: **Ekcharya lasyam** - one dancer, multiple roles.
11. Costume: men - jacket and pajamas of **Kanchipuram silk**; women - **Benarasi silk**. Instruments: **mridangam, veena, flute**.
12. Proponents: **Yamini Krishnamurthy, Lakshmi Viswanathan, Padma Subramanyam, Mrinalini Sarabhai, Mallika Sarabhai, Alarmel Valli**.
13. Elemental set: **fire (Bharatanatyam), earth (Kuchipudi), sky/ether (Kathakali), air (Mohiniyattam), water (Odissi)**; none recorded for Kathak, Manipuri or Sattriya.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Examine the significance of the early-nineteenth-century definition of the Bharatanatyam recital for how the form is understood today. (20 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The significance is that the form's best-known feature was fixed by four named individuals at a datable moment, well before the twentieth-century reconstruction that is usually treated as its origin story.

**What was done.** Four dance teachers of Thanjavur, known as the Tanjore Quartet - Chinnaiah, Ponniah, Vadivelu and Sivanandam - defined the elements of the recital: *Alarippu*, *Jatiswaram*, *Shabdam*, *Varnam*, *Padam*, *Jawali* and *Thillana*. Under them the form also came to be known as Tanjore Natyam.

**Why it matters analytically.** First, it dates a repertoire. An answer can say that the sequence now performed was settled in the early nineteenth century, which is far stronger than calling it ancient. Second, it shows reorganisation preceding revival: the tradition had already been restructured by named practitioners before anti-nautch pressure, decline and twentieth-century reconstruction. Third, it supplies the design argument - the order moves from simple steps through pure movement to words, then to the fusion of story with rhythm and melody, then to pure expression, then to a light lyric at faster tempo, and closes with pure dance of exuberant movement.

**The caution.** Fixing a repertoire also narrows it. What became canonical is one settled selection, and the hereditary repertoires from which it was drawn are not recoverable from the canon itself.

Verdict: the recital order is evidence that this tradition has been deliberately reorganised more than once, and that its authority comes from named practice, not from antiquity.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = the pre-twentieth-century fixing stated as the thesis 3 + the four named teachers and the seven items in order 6 + the dating-a-repertoire argument 3 + the reorganisation-before-revival argument 4 + the design argument read off the order 3 + the narrowing caution 1.**

### Concept check

**Question:** State the single sentence about Bharatanatyam that carries the highest marks in a Mains answer, and explain why each of its clauses is necessary.

**Model answer:** The sentence: **"Modern Bharatanatyam is the outcome of twentieth-century reconstruction acting on the Sadir repertoire of the devadasis, whose social position was being dismantled at the very moment their knowledge was being reorganised for the stage - so the continuity is real, and so is the break."** Each clause does specific work. *Modern Bharatanatyam* time-indexes the subject, which immediately signals that you are not writing the unbroken-tradition answer. *Reconstruction acting on the Sadir repertoire* names both the process and the material, so continuity is asserted with evidence rather than with an adjective. *Of the devadasis* names the carrier community, without which the sentence becomes a story about individuals. *Whose social position was being dismantled at the very moment their knowledge was being reorganised* supplies the analytical core - the simultaneity of displacement and reconstruction, which is the fact that most answers omit. And *so the continuity is real, and so is the break* is the qualified verdict, which prevents the sentence from collapsing into either nostalgia or debunking. From that single sentence the rest of the answer writes itself: the named actors with their distinct functions, the Thanjavur teachers and the recital order as evidence of an earlier reorganisation, the change in performer demography, and the later institutional recognition.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that acknowledging disruption diminishes the form. It does the opposite: it produces a specific, evidenced account in place of a general one. The candidate who writes "Bharatanatyam is an ancient temple dance revived after independence" has written a sentence with two errors and no evidence; the candidate who writes the sentence above has written an argument.

## Lesson 11 - Kuchipudi: the itinerant dance-drama that became a solo concert form

Progress: 11/23 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: village name and the travelling actors, Siddhendra Yogi and Bhama Kalapam, the Vaishnava reorganisation, twentieth-century revival and the solo format, recital parts and named solo items, speech and the dance-drama character, costume, music and elemental association

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the full Kuchipudi section including the village and the Kusselavas, the eleventh-century formaliser and his work, the Vaishnava and Bhagavathalu record, the Vijayanagara and Golconda patronage, the twentieth-century revivers, the recital parts, the three named solo items, the language and instruments, and the costume and proponent lists
CA search: "Not applicable - regional form lesson; no genuine current linkage"
CA found: None found; none claimed
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: four stages in which the carrier changes and the form changes with it

```text
   STAGE 1: ITINERANT TROUPE
   Kuchelapuram village, Andhra Pradesh -> performers called KUSSELAVAS,
   travelling actors
                 |
   STAGE 2: FORMALISATION
   SIDDHENDRA YOGI formalises it, shapes it with YAKSHAGANA-style folk
   dramas, authors works including BHAMA KALAPAM
   ** DATING CONTESTED: one printed attribution says 11th century.
      Standard accounts place him SUBSTANTIALLY LATER - most commonly the
      17th century, with some accounts as early as the 14th. No century
      here is secure; write the attribution, not a date. **
                 |
   STAGE 3: VAISHNAVA REORGANISATION
   with the advent of Vaishnavism the form becomes a MONOPOLY OF MALE
   BRAHMINS and begins to be performed AT TEMPLES
   dancers called BHAGAVATHALUS | themes from the BHAGAVATA PURANA
   flourishes under VIJAYANAGARA and GOLCONDA rulers
                 |
   STAGE 4: TWENTIETH-CENTURY CHANGE
   stays mostly in VILLAGES until the 20th century
   one printed attribution credits BALASARASWATI and RAGINI DEVI with its
   revival ** the first of these two names is CONTESTED - see section 4 **
   LAKSHMINARAYAN SASTRI introduces SOLO PERFORMANCES and FEMALE DANCERS
                 |
                 v
   RESULT: a TEAM dance-drama form that now also has a solo concert life
           -> the dancer may combine the role of a SINGER
           -> hence it BECOMES A DANCE-DRAMA performance
           element symbolised: EARTH (the earthly elements in the human body)
```

### 1. Name, place and the first carrier

**Kuchipudi**, named after the village **Kuchelapuram** in **Andhra Pradesh**, was originally performed by **travelling actors called *Kusselavas***.

Two discriminators are already present. The form is named after a **village**, not after a text, a deity or a technique - the only one of the eight so named. And its first recorded carrier is a **travelling troupe**, which is the exact opposite of Bharatanatyam's temple-attached solo performer. When a question asks you to distinguish the two Andhra-and-Tamil neighbours, that contrast of origin - itinerant dance-drama against temple solo - is the first thing to write.

### 2. Siddhendra Yogi, and what "formalised" means here

**Siddhendra Yogi** formalised it, shaping it with **Yakshagana-style folk dramas** and authoring works like ***Bhama Kalapam***.

⚠️ **The date attached to this name is not secure, and this is the topic's sharpest chronology trap.** Some printed preparation material gives **"11th century"**. That attribution is an **outlier**. Standard accounts of the tradition place Siddhendra Yogi **substantially later**, most commonly in the **seventeenth century**, with a minority of accounts placing him as early as the **fourteenth**; the eleventh-century date is not supported in critical treatments, and the historical record for the figure is thin in every account.

✅ **How to write it.** Name the function, not the century: "Siddhendra Yogi is the saint-composer credited with formalising Kuchipudi as a dance-drama and with authoring *Bhama Kalapam*; the tradition's systematisation is most commonly placed in the seventeenth century, though the dating is contested and some accounts place him considerably earlier." That sentence is safe against every version of the item. ❌ Do not write "11th century" as a fact, and ❌ do not build any chronological argument on it - the date spine in Lesson 23 therefore carries this entry as contested and not as a fixed point.

Two things in the passage **are** secure and should be used. The shaping influence is **Yakshagana-style folk drama** - a named neighbouring performance tradition, which is itself a route out of this topic towards the theatre and folk-performance material. And the formaliser is credited with **authoring works**, which means the form acquired a written repertoire, not merely a technique.

⚠️ *Yakshagana* is a Karnataka performance tradition and has been set as a Karnataka pairing in an objective question. Its role here is as a **stylistic influence on Kuchipudi's formalisation**, not as an Andhra form. Keep the influence and the regional attribution separate.

### 3. The Vaishnava reorganisation, and its exact effect

**With the advent of Vaishnavism, the dance form became a monopoly of the male Brahmins and began to be performed at temples.** Kuchipudi focused on **Bhagavata Purana** stories, with dancers called ***Bhagavathalus***, and **flourished under Vijayanagara and Golconda rulers**.

This is the second appearance of Vaishnavism as a force that reorganises **who is permitted to perform**, and the comparison with Odissi is one of the most useful two-line arguments in the topic:

| Region | Effect of the advent of Vaishnavism |
|---|---|
| **Odisha** | the **mahari** system becomes **defunct**; boys dressed as females, the **gotipuas**, continue the art |
| **Andhra** | the form becomes a **monopoly of male Brahmins**, performed **at temples**, dancers called **Bhagavathalus** |

In both cases a religious movement produces an **access rule**, and in both cases the outcome is a narrowing of who may perform. The patronage half of the record - Vijayanagara and Golconda - adds the second dimension: the same period supplies both the access rule and the courtly support.

### 4. The twentieth-century change

**Kuchipudi stayed mostly in villages until the twentieth century.** One printed attribution credits **Balasaraswati and Ragini Devi** with reviving it, and records that **Lakshminarayan Sastri introduced solo performances and female dancers**.

⚠️ **The first of those two revival names is contested and must not be taught as secure.** **Balasaraswati** is canonically associated with **Bharatanatyam**, not with Kuchipudi, and no independent corroboration for a Kuchipudi revival role was reached for this session. Treat the attribution as **doubtful**: report it as something a printed source states, and do not write her into a Kuchipudi answer as a revivalist. The same name also appears, spelt *Balasaraswathi*, in this form's proponent list, and that listing inherits the same doubt.

⚠️ **Ragini Devi** is recorded in the same sentence and is retained here **only as a hedged attribution** - a twentieth-century figure named in connection with Kuchipudi's revival. No dates, biography, nationality or other affiliation is supplied for her by this session, and none should be invented.

✅ **What is safe to write.** The recorded proponent note makes one attribution explicit and unambiguous: **Natyakalanidhi Lakshminarayana Sastry** is described as **the father of the solo format of Kuchipudi**, and it is he who is recorded as introducing solo performances and female dancers. Build the twentieth-century paragraph on that, and mention the other two names only with the hedge attached.

Read this against Bharatanatyam and the symmetry is striking but inverted. Bharatanatyam moved **from solo female towards men and groups**; Kuchipudi moved **from team performance towards a solo format that included female dancers**. Two neighbouring forms, the same century, opposite trajectories - and in both cases the change is attributed to named individuals rather than to an anonymous process.

### 5. Features of the form

- It involves **difficult foot movements** and is **generally a team performance**.
- Most recitals are **based on stories of the Bhagavata Purana** but have a **secular theme**. There is a **predominance of Shringara rasa**.
- Each principal character introduces itself on the stage with a ***daaru***, a **small composition of dance and song specifically choreographed for the revelation of each character**.
- The dance involves **all three components** of classical dance: **Nritta, Natya and Nritya**. It is **similar to Bharatanatyam but has its own features**.
- The performance has these parts: ***Sollakath* or *Patakshara*** - the **Nritta** part, where movement of the body is made; and ***Kavutvams*** - the **Nritya** part, which **involves extensive acrobatics** and may also be performed as Nritta.
- The Kuchipudi dance style is a **manifestation of the earthly elements in the human body**.
- In a recital the dancer **may combine the role of a singer** into themselves; **hence it becomes a dance-drama performance**.
- **Both Lasya and Tandava elements are important.**

Note the tension inside the fifth bullet and do not smooth it away: recitals are **based on stories of the Bhagavata Purana** and yet have a **secular theme**, with a predominance of the rasa of love. That combination - devotional source material handled with a worldly emotional register - is a real feature of the form and is worth a sentence in any answer that compares Kuchipudi with the strictly devotional eastern forms.

### 6. The three named solo elements

Apart from group performances, there are popular **solo elements**:

| Solo element | What it is |
|---|---|
| ***Manduka Shabdam*** | tells the story of a **frog** |
| ***Tarangam*** | the dancer performs with the **feet on the edges of a brass plate**, balancing a **pot of water on the head** or **a set of diyas** |
| ***Jala Chitra Nrityam*** | the dancer **draws pictures on the floor with the toes** while dancing |

These three are the most reliable single-form identifiers in the whole topic, because no other form in this session records anything comparable. ❌ **Tarangam is Kuchipudi, not Bharatanatyam.** The brass-plate item is a standard planted confusion in option sets precisely because the two forms are otherwise similar.

### 7. Music, language and costume

- A Kuchipudi recital is generally accompanied with **Carnatic music**, **violin and mridangam** being the principal instruments.
- The recital is in the **Telugu** language.
- The dress of **male performers** is called ***Bagalbandi***; **female performers wear Benarasi**.

⚠️ Language is a strong discriminator here and should be used: **Telugu** for Kuchipudi against **Manipravalam**, a mixture of Malayalam and Sanskrit, for both Kerala forms.

### 8. Proponents

Famous proponents recorded: **Natyakalanidhi Lakshminarayana Sastry** (the father of the solo format), **Balasaraswathi**, **Radha Reddy and Raja Reddy**, **Yamini Krishnamurthy**, **Indrani Rahman**.

⚠️ **Balasaraswathi** in this list carries the same doubt set out in section 4: the name is canonically associated with **Bharatanatyam**, and the Kuchipudi attribution was not independently corroborated for this session. Report it as a printed listing; do not use it.

⚠️ Two further names cross forms. **Yamini Krishnamurthy** appears in the Bharatanatyam list as well, and **Indrani Rahman** is separately recorded as one of the two people through whose efforts **Odissi** gained international acclaim in the mid-twentieth century. Performer names are therefore **weak discriminators** in this topic and should not be used to identify a form unless the name appears in only one list.

### Examination use

For Prelims: the village name and the Kusselavas; Siddhendra Yogi as the credited formaliser, with *Bhama Kalapam* and the contested dating; the Yakshagana-style influence; the Vaishnava monopoly and the Bhagavathalus; Vijayanagara and Golconda patronage; the father of the solo format; Sollakath/Patakshara and Kavutvams with their elements; the three solo items; Carnatic accompaniment with violin and mridangam; Telugu; Bagalbandi and Benarasi; and the earth association.

For Mains, Kuchipudi is the best available evidence for a specific and under-used argument: **a form's performance organisation is a historical variable, not an essential property.** Team to solo, male-Brahmin monopoly to the introduction of female dancers, village to concert stage - all within a recorded chain with named agents. Paired with Bharatanatyam's inverse trajectory, it makes a complete answer to any question about change in the classical performing arts.

### Traps introduced here

- Attributing *Tarangam* to Bharatanatyam.
- Writing "11th century" for Siddhendra Yogi as though it were established; it is an outlier against accounts that place him substantially later.
- Naming Balasaraswati as a Kuchipudi revivalist; the attribution is contested and she is canonically a Bharatanatyam figure.
- Treating Yakshagana as an Andhra form because of its role in Kuchipudi's formalisation.
- Writing that Kuchipudi is a solo female form, or that it is exclusively a team form - both are true only of particular periods.
- Losing the Bhagavata-Purana-source-with-secular-theme combination by describing the form as purely devotional.
- Using a proponent's name as a discriminator when the name appears in two lists.
- Forgetting that the dancer may combine the singer's role, which is what makes the performance a dance-drama.

### Revision notes

1. **Kuchipudi** - Andhra Pradesh; named after the village **Kuchelapuram**; originally performed by travelling actors called ***Kusselavas***.
2. **Siddhendra Yogi** is credited with formalising it with **Yakshagana-style folk dramas** and authoring ***Bhama Kalapam***. ⚠️ **Dating contested**: a printed "11th century" is an outlier; standard accounts place him substantially later, most commonly the **seventeenth century**, some as early as the fourteenth. Write the function, not a century.
3. With **Vaishnavism**: **monopoly of male Brahmins**, performed **at temples**; dancers called ***Bhagavathalus***; themes from the **Bhagavata Purana**; flourished under **Vijayanagara and Golconda**.
4. Stayed mostly in **villages till the twentieth century**; **Lakshminarayan Sastri** introduced **solo performances and female dancers** and is called the **father of the solo format**. ⚠️ A printed revival attribution to **Balasaraswati** is **contested** - she is canonically a **Bharatanatyam** figure; **Ragini Devi** is retained only as a hedged attribution.
5. Features: **difficult foot movements**; generally a **team performance**; **secular theme** despite Bhagavata Purana sources; **predominance of Shringara rasa**; involves **all three components**.
6. ***Daaru*** = small dance-and-song composition revealing each character.
7. Parts: ***Sollakath / Patakshara*** = **Nritta**; ***Kavutvams*** = **Nritya**, with extensive acrobatics, and may also be performed as Nritta.
8. Solo elements: ***Manduka Shabdam*** (frog story), ***Tarangam*** (feet on the edges of a brass plate; pot of water or diyas on the head), ***Jala Chitra Nrityam*** (pictures drawn on the floor with the toes).
9. Element: **earth**. Music: **Carnatic**, with **violin and mridangam**. Language: **Telugu**.
10. Costume: men - ***Bagalbandi***; women - **Benarasi**.
11. Proponents: **Lakshminarayana Sastry** (father of the solo format), **Radha and Raja Reddy**, **Yamini Krishnamurthy**, **Indrani Rahman**; **Balasaraswathi** is listed in the same place but carries the contested-attribution warning above.
12. The dancer may combine the **singer's role**, which is why the recital **becomes a dance-drama**.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Explain how Kuchipudi's organisation as a performance changed across its recorded stages, and what the sequence demonstrates. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

Four stages are recorded. It began with **travelling actors**, the *Kusselavas*, of a named village. It was then **formalised** by a saint-composer credited with authoring *Bhama Kalapam* and with shaping the form through folk-drama style. With Vaishnavism it became a **monopoly of male Brahmins** performed at temples, its dancers called *Bhagavathalus*, flourishing under Vijayanagara and Golconda patronage. In the twentieth century, having stayed mostly in villages, it acquired a **solo format and female dancers** through the figure recorded as the father of that format.

The sequence demonstrates that performance organisation is a historical variable, not an essential property: troupe to temple monopoly to concert solo, with access rules and patronage changing at each step.

Caution: the century attributed to the formaliser is contested, and one printed revival name is doubtful; state the stages, not those attributions.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = four stages in order with a named feature each 6 + the historical-variable conclusion drawn explicitly 2 + the two attribution cautions 2.**

### Concept check

**Question:** Kuchipudi and Bharatanatyam are the pair most often confused in this topic. Build the shortest set of facts that separates them reliably, and explain why the obvious differences are not enough.

**Model answer:** The obvious differences fail because the two forms genuinely overlap: Kuchipudi is recorded as **similar to Bharatanatyam**, both involve all three elements, both give weight to Lasya and Tandava, both draw on devotional material, both use Carnatic-tradition accompaniment with the mridangam, both dress female performers in Benarasi, and they share at least one recorded proponent. A reliable separation therefore has to rest on features that belong to one form only. Four do the work. **Origin and first carrier:** temple-attached solo performance by devadasis against an itinerant troupe of travelling actors from a named village. **Speech:** the Kuchipudi dancer may take on the singer's role and characters are introduced by a *daaru*, so speech and self-introduction belong to Kuchipudi; Bharatanatyam's solo device is the opposite, one dancer playing multiple roles in the *Ekcharya lasyam* style. **A property-based solo item:** *Tarangam* on the edges of a brass plate with a pot or diyas balanced on the head, and *Jala Chitra Nrityam* drawn on the floor with the toes, have no Bharatanatyam counterpart. **Language and element:** Telugu recital and the earth association against a Tamil-Nadu form associated with fire. Any two of those four settle the identification; none of the shared features does.

**Misconception to avoid:** Trying to separate the two by "Kuchipudi is more dramatic" or "Bharatanatyam is more graceful". Both are impressionistic, both are contestable, and neither is in the record. Identification demands are won with facts that belong to exactly one form, which is why the *daaru*, the brass plate and the language are worth more than any comparative adjective.

## Lesson 12 - Kathakali: what happens when gesture has to carry an entire drama

Progress: 12/23 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the two parent dance-dramas, the name, the martial and Sanskrit-theatre connections, patronage collapse and revival, the five make-up types and their colour code, the performance frame, the gesture treatise, the song-text and language, staging and instruments

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the full Kathakali section including the two parent traditions and their patrons, the etymology, the Koodiyattam and martial-art relation, the feudal-breakdown and 1930s revival record, the all-male troupe statement, the five vesham types with the colour code, the opening items, the language and song-text, the gesture treatise, the staging and lighting, the drums, the elemental association and the proponent list
CA search: "Not applicable - regional form lesson; no genuine current linkage"
CA found: None found; none claimed
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the colour code, which is the most testable block in the topic

```text
   VESHAM (make-up) - FIVE TYPES:  PACHA | KATHI | THADI | KARI | MINUKKU

   +------------------------------+---------------------------------------+
   | GREEN                        | nobility, divinity and virtue         |
   +------------------------------+---------------------------------------+
   | RED PATCHES BESIDE THE NOSE  | royalty                               |
   +------------------------------+---------------------------------------+
   | BLACK                        | evil and wickedness                   |
   +------------------------------+---------------------------------------+
   | YELLOW                       | saints and women                      |
   +------------------------------+---------------------------------------+
   | COMPLETELY RED PAINTED FACE  | evil                                  |
   +------------------------------+---------------------------------------+
   | WHITE BEARD                  | beings with higher consciousness      |
   |                              | and divinity                          |
   +------------------------------+---------------------------------------+

   CAUTION BUILT INTO THE TABLE: RED APPEARS TWICE, WITH DIFFERENT MEANINGS.
   Red PATCHES BESIDE THE NOSE = royalty.  A COMPLETELY RED FACE = evil.
   The discriminator is EXTENT, not hue.

   THE PERFORMANCE FRAME
   KELIKOTTU  ->  TODAYAM  ->  the dance-drama itself  ->  DAWN
   (calling the   (invoking       gesture-led,             with the continuous
   attention of   the blessings   eye and eyebrow          sound of chenda and
   the audience)  of the Gods)    movement carrying        maddala marking BOTH
                                  the rasas                beginning AND end
```

### 1. The two parent traditions, and the name

**In the temples of Kerala, two forms of dance-drama, *Ramanattam* and *Krishnattam*, evolved under the patronage of feudal lords**, narrating episodes from the **Ramayana** and the **Mahabharata**. These folk drama traditions later became the **source of Kathakali**, which derived its name from the words ***Katha*** meaning **story** and ***Kali*** meaning **drama**.

Three facts to lock down immediately.

- **Two parents, not one.** *Ramanattam* and *Krishnattam* are both named, and the form is separately recorded as possibly having **evolved from Ramanattam**. Name both.
- **The patron class is named.** **Feudal lords**, not a royal court and not a temple establishment. That specific patron class is what makes the later decline intelligible.
- **The name is a compound of story and drama.** Not of dance.

The form is further recorded as **closely related to *Koodiyattam*** - the Sanskrit drama tradition - **and to other ancient martial art performance**, and as **a wonderful combination of music, dance and drama**.

⚠️ **Boundary note.** Koodiyattam is the Sanskrit theatre tradition and is owned by the theatre topic; it appears here only as a named relation. Do not import theatre material into a dance answer; do name the relation, because "closely related to the Sanskrit drama tradition and to martial art performance" is exactly the kind of precise linkage that distinguishes a prepared answer.

### 2. Decline and revival: the clearest patronage argument in the topic

**However, with the breakdown of the feudal set-up, Kathakali began declining as an art form. It was revived in the 1930s by the famous Malayali poet V. N. Menon under the patronage of Mukunda Raja.**

This is the most economical patronage argument available anywhere in this session, and it should be used whenever a question asks about art and patronage:

```text
   PATRON CLASS          ART FORM                 OUTCOME
   feudal lords    ->    two temple dance-dramas  ->  Kathakali emerges
        |
   BREAKDOWN of the feudal set-up
        |
        v
   support base collapses -> the form DECLINES
        |
   NEW PATRON: Mukunda Raja  +  NEW AGENT: a named poet, 1930s
        |
        v
   REVIVAL - note that revival required BOTH a patron AND an agent
```

⚠️ **The same named poet appears twice in this topic.** He is credited with the 1930s revival of Kathakali under the patronage of Mukunda Raja **and** with the revival of **Mohiniyattam along with Kalyani Amma**. One individual is therefore recorded as central to the twentieth-century recovery of **both** Kerala classical forms. State both attributions, keep them separate, and do not merge the two revivals into one event.

⚠️ A further observation, marked as an inference and not as a recorded fact: several of the proponent names in this topic carry the prefix **Kalamandalam** - among them **Kalamandalam Kshemavathy** for Mohiniyattam. The prefix indicates a training affiliation rather than a family name. ⚠️ This session does **not** record the founding date, location or governance of that institution and does not supply them; note the pattern, and route any institutional claim to the heritage and institutions topic.

### 3. Features of the form

- Kathakali is **generally an all-male troupe performance**.
- There is **minimal use of props**. However, **very elaborate facial make-up along with a head gear** is used for different characters.
- It involves **both dance and drama, and the two cannot be clearly separated**.
- Most recitals are a **grand representation of the eternal conflict between good and evil**, drawing themes from the **epics and the puranas**. It is also called the **'Ballad of the East'**.
- **Gestures are the crown jewel of the entire dance-drama.** The form is **remarkable in the representation of the rasas through movements of the eye and eyebrows**, through which the story is conveyed. **Nine important facial expressions called *Navarasas*** are taught. **Extensive hand gestures** are also used, and the *mudras* are based on the ***Hastalakshana Deepika***, a treatise on the language of hand gestures. The form therefore **calls for strenuous training**.
- **Music is important to rightfully convey the entire drama**; different compositions are used during the performance to **give depth to the drama**.
- It **symbolises the element of sky or ether**.

### 4. Make-up as a sign system

The make-up, or ***vesham***, is of **five types - *Pacha*, *Kathi*, *Thadi*, *Kari* and *Minukku***. Different colours have their own significance, set out in the visual above.

Two analytical points worth a sentence each in an answer.

1. **Make-up here is not decoration; it is a legible code.** In a performance without scenery, the audience must be able to place a character morally within seconds of their entrance. Green for nobility, black for evil, yellow for saints and women, a completely red face for evil, a white beard for beings of higher consciousness - this is an information system doing the work that costume, lighting and dialogue do elsewhere.
2. **The red distinction is the exam's favourite.** Red **patches beside the nose** indicate **royalty**; a **completely red painted face** indicates **evil**. The variable is the extent of the colour, not the colour itself. ❌ Do not collapse the two.

❌ Beyond the six meanings recorded above, **do not supply further colour symbolism**, and do not attach the five *vesham* names to particular colours unless you can support the attachment. This session records the five names and the six colour meanings and does not map one list onto the other.

### 5. Text, language and staging

- The **language used for Kathakali songs is *Manipravalam***, a **mixture of Malayalam and Sanskrit**. The form **may have evolved from Ramanattam**. The **text of Kathakali songs is called *Attakkatha***.
- Performance is **generally in open-air theatres covered with coarse mats, or on temple premises** with the lush green trees of Kerala providing a backdrop. **A brass lamp is used for lighting.**
- **The arrival of dawn, accompanied by a continuous sound of drums, *chenda* and *maddala*, marks the beginning and end of a Kathakali recital.**

That last line rewards close reading. Dawn marks **both** the beginning and the end - which tells you the performance frame is the night, and that the drums are the temporal marker rather than merely the accompaniment. A performance that runs from dusk to dawn in an open-air setting, lit by a single brass lamp, with minimal props and characters legible entirely from their painted faces, is a coherent design in which every element solves a problem created by the setting. That is the sentence that turns a list of staging facts into an argument.

### 6. Proponents

Famous proponents recorded: **Guru Kunchu Kurup, Gopinath, Kottakal Sivaraman, Rita Ganguly**.

⚠️ Note the spelling variation across recorded material for the third name, which also appears as **Kottakkal Sivaraman**. Reproduce the name as you find it and do not treat a spelling difference as a different person.

### 7. Why this form is the strongest evidence for the composite-art claim

Return to Lesson 2's proposition that dance is a complete art uniting music, sculpture, poetry and drama. Kathakali evidences all four constituents simultaneously and explicitly: a **song-text** with its own name and its own mixed language; **music** described as necessary to convey the drama, with compositions chosen to give it depth; **drama** so integral that dance and drama cannot be clearly separated; and a visual, sculptural treatment of the human figure through elaborate make-up and headgear that makes each character a moving image. If a Mains question asks you to substantiate the composite-art claim with one example, this is the example.

### Examination use

For Prelims, this lesson is unusually dense: *Ramanattam* and *Krishnattam* with their epics and their feudal-lord patronage; the *Katha* + *Kali* etymology; the Koodiyattam and martial-art relations; the feudal-breakdown decline and the 1930s revival with its named poet and patron; the all-male troupe; minimal props with elaborate make-up and headgear; the five *vesham* names; the six colour meanings including the red distinction; the 'Ballad of the East'; *Manipravalam*; *Attakkatha*; *Navarasas*; *Hastalakshana Deepika*; the open-air theatre with coarse mats and the brass lamp; *chenda* and *maddala*; the dawn frame; the sky or ether association; and the proponents.

For Mains, the transferable argument is the **patronage chain**: an art form supported by a specific social class declines when that class declines, and recovery requires both a new patron and a named agent. The concrete evidence - feudal lords, breakdown, decline, Mukunda Raja and the 1930s revival - is compact enough to deploy in any question about art, patronage and social change.

### Traps introduced here

- Naming only one parent tradition.
- Reading the name as story plus dance; *Kali* here is drama.
- Treating Koodiyattam as a Kathakali variant rather than as a named related Sanskrit drama tradition.
- Collapsing the red-patch and fully-red-face meanings.
- Mapping the five *vesham* names onto the six colour meanings without support.
- Describing Kathakali as scenically elaborate; props are minimal and the elaboration is on the body.
- Assigning *Attakkatha* to another form, or confusing the song-text name with the language name.
- Forgetting that the same named poet is credited with reviving both Kerala classical forms.

### Revision notes

1. **Parents: *Ramanattam* and *Krishnattam***, two temple dance-dramas of Kerala under the patronage of **feudal lords**, narrating **Ramayana** and **Mahabharata** episodes.
2. Name: ***Katha*** (story) + ***Kali*** (drama). Closely related to ***Koodiyattam*** (Sanskrit drama) **and other ancient martial art performance**.
3. **Breakdown of the feudal set-up -> decline**; **revived in the 1930s by the Malayali poet V. N. Menon under the patronage of Mukunda Raja**.
4. Generally an **all-male troupe**; **minimal props** but **elaborate facial make-up and head gear**.
5. ***Vesham* types: *Pacha*, *Kathi*, *Thadi*, *Kari*, *Minukku***.
6. Colour code: **green = nobility, divinity, virtue; red patches beside the nose = royalty; black = evil and wickedness; yellow = saints and women; completely red face = evil; white beard = beings with higher consciousness and divinity.**
7. Theme: the **eternal conflict between good and evil**, from the epics and puranas; also called the **'Ballad of the East'**.
8. Gesture: rasas conveyed through **eye and eyebrow movement**; **Navarasas** taught; mudras based on ***Hastalakshana Deepika***; **strenuous training**.
9. Language **Manipravalam** (Malayalam + Sanskrit); song-text called ***Attakkatha***.
10. Staging: **open-air theatres with coarse mats** or temple premises; **brass lamp**; **dawn with continuous *chenda* and *maddala*** marks **both** beginning and end.
11. Element: **sky or ether**.
12. Proponents: **Guru Kunchu Kurup, Gopinath, Kottakal Sivaraman, Rita Ganguly**.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Explain how Kathakali evidences the claim that Indian dance is a composite art, and identify the boundary that must still be observed. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The root conception calls dance a complete art uniting music, sculpture, poetry and drama. Kathakali evidences all four constituents simultaneously, which is why it is the example to reach for when the claim has to be substantiated.

**Poetry.** The form has a named song-text, *Attakkatha*, composed in *Manipravalam*, a mixture of Malayalam and Sanskrit - a literary vehicle created for performance.

**Music.** Music is recorded as necessary to convey the drama, with different compositions used to give it depth, and the *chenda* and *maddala* mark the recital's beginning and end at dawn.

**Drama.** Dance and drama are recorded as inseparable in it, and the recitals are a grand representation of the conflict between good and evil drawn from the epics and puranas.

**Sculpture.** Elaborate facial make-up with headgear, organised into five types and a colour code, treats the performer as a moving image whose meaning is read visually before any gesture.

**The boundary.** The form is recorded as closely related to the Sanskrit drama tradition and to martial art performance. Both relations should be named and neither should be imported: dramaturgy belongs to the theatre topic and martial arts to their own, and what is owned here is the danced grammar that dominates this form.

Verdict: Kathakali substantiates the composite-art claim as no other form does, provided the naming of its neighbours is kept distinct from annexing them.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the composite-art claim stated as the proposition to be evidenced 2 + four constituents each evidenced with a named feature 8 + the two recorded neighbour relations named 3 + the boundary rule that names without importing 2.**

### Concept check

**Question:** Kathakali uses minimal props but extremely elaborate make-up, performs in the open air by the light of a single brass lamp, and runs through the night to dawn. Show that these are not four unrelated facts.

**Model answer:** They are four solutions to one problem: **how to make a full-length drama legible in a setting that supplies no visual aids**. Start with the setting. An open-air theatre covered with coarse mats, or a temple courtyard backed by trees, offers no stage machinery, no scenery and no controllable lighting; the single available light source is a brass lamp. Under those conditions, painted scenery would be invisible and props would be lost, so **minimal props** is not austerity but arithmetic. What remains visible in lamplight is the **human face and the outline of the head**, which is precisely where the form concentrates its resources - elaborate make-up with headgear, organised into a colour code that places a character morally at first sight, so that the audience needs no introduction and no programme. The same constraint explains why the **rasas are carried by eye and eyebrow movement**: within a lit face, the features that read at a distance are the ones that move most sharply against a painted background. Finally, the **night-to-dawn frame** is what makes the lamp-lit economy possible in the first place, and the continuous sound of the drums at dawn marks the frame at both ends. Setting determines lighting, lighting determines what is visible, visibility determines where the artistry is concentrated, and the temporal frame holds the whole arrangement together.

**Misconception to avoid:** Explaining the make-up as pure symbolism of divine and demonic types. The symbolism is real and recorded, but symbolism alone does not explain why the face is elaborated rather than the costume, the set or the props. The performance conditions do.

## Lesson 13 - Mohiniyattam: the form defined by what it does not do

Progress: 13/23 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the name and the solo female tradition, nineteenth-century development and Travancore patronage, obscurity and revival, the absence of thumping, the borrowed grace and borrowed vigour, the 40 movement units, the Kasavu costume, language, instruments and the elemental association

Book context: Queried - the full Mohiniyattam section including the etymology, the nineteenth-century developer, the Travancore and named-ruler patronage, the obscurity-and-revival record with both named revivers, the technique and absence-of-thumping statement, the Lasya dominance, the narrative subject, the language, the costume and jewellery, the elemental association, the 40 movements and the instruments and proponents
CA search: "Not applicable - regional form lesson; no genuine current linkage"
CA found: None found; none claimed
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: a form positioned between its two neighbours

```text
                       KATHAKALI                      BHARATANATYAM
                    (Kerala, same state)            (Tamil Nadu, neighbour)
                    all-male troupe                 strong rhythmic footwork
                    dance-drama                     bent-knee, even weight
                    VIGOUR                          GRACE and ELEGANCE
                          \                              /
                           \                            /
                            \                          /
                             v                        v
                          M O H I N I Y A T T A M
                  "combines the grace and elegance of Bharatanatyam
                   with the vigour of Kathakali"
                                    |
              +---------------------+---------------------+
              |                                           |
      WHAT IT ADDS                              WHAT IT SUBTRACTS
      LASYA dominant                            a MARKED ABSENCE of
      solo female performance                   THUMPING of footsteps
      40 basic movements: ATAVAKUL / ATAVUS     -> footwork is GENTLE
      white/off-white KASAVU saree with         NO elaborate facial
      gold-coloured brocade                     make-up
      ghungroo on the ankles
      element: AIR
                                    |
                                    v
        THE IDENTIFICATION RULE: this form is most reliably identified by a
        NEGATIVE - no thumping, no elaborate facial make-up - which is why
        an option set will describe it by what is absent
```

### 1. Name and basic character

**Mohiniyattam**, or the **Dance of an Enchantress** - '**Mohini**' meaning **beautiful woman** and '**attam**' meaning **dance** - is essentially a **solo dance performance by women**.

The name is a compound of a figure and an activity, and the figure matters: the form **generally narrates the story of the feminine dance of Vishnu**. That single recorded line is the form's narrative subject and is a good discriminator, because no other form in this topic has a comparable subject statement.

### 2. Development, patronage and revival

- It was **further developed by Vadivelu in the nineteenth century**.
- It **gained prominence under the rulers of Travancore** in the present state of Kerala; **the patronage of Swathi Thirunal, the Travancore ruler in the nineteenth century, is notable**.
- **After it had fallen to obscurity, the famous Malayali poet V. N. Menon revived it along with Kalyani Amma.**

Three points that repay attention.

1. ⚠️ **Vadivelu is one of the four Thanjavur teachers** who defined the Bharatanatyam recital in the early nineteenth century. The same named individual therefore appears in the recorded development of **two forms in two states**, and this is the clearest single instance in the topic of expertise moving across a regional boundary. State it as two separate recorded facts about the same name, not as a single merged claim.
2. **The patronage is courtly and named.** Travancore rulers generally and Swathi Thirunal specifically. Set this beside Kathakali's feudal-lord patronage and you have two different patronage structures inside one state.
3. **The decline is recorded as "obscurity", not as collapse**, and the revival has **two named agents**, one of whom also revived Kathakali. Name both; the second name is frequently the discriminating detail in an option set.

### 3. Technique: the positive and the negative

- Mohiniyattam **combines the grace and elegance of Bharatanatyam with the vigour of Kathakali**.
- There is a **marked absence of thumping of footsteps**, and the **footwork is gentle**.
- It has its **own Nritta and Nritya aspects** like the other classical dances.
- The **Lasya aspect (beauty and grace) is dominant**; hence it is **mainly performed by female dancers**.
- ***Atavakul* or *Atavus*** is the collection of **40 basic dance movements**.

The borrowing statement in the first bullet is the analytically interesting one, and it should not be reduced. Mohiniyattam is recorded as taking something from **each** of its two neighbours - grace and elegance from the Tamil form, vigour from the other Kerala form - which means that describing it as simply "graceful" drops half of the record. The **absence of thumping** is then what distinguishes its handling of rhythm from Bharatanatyam's, despite the shared grace.

### 4. Language, costume and instruments

- The dance is accompanied by music and songs; the story is presented in the language called ***Manipravalam***, **a mix of Sanskrit and Malayalam**.
- **Costume is of special importance.** **White and off-white** are the principal colours of the sarees, called ***Kasavu***, with the presence of **gold-coloured brocade designs**. There is **no elaborate facial make-up**. The dancer wears a **leather strap with bells (ghungroo) on her ankles**.
- **Musical instruments used are cymbals, veena, drums and flute, among others.**
- The **element of air** is symbolised through a Mohiniyattam performance.

⚠️ Two cautions on costume. First, ❌ **do not assign a symbolic meaning to the white and gold**; the record states the colours and the brocade, not a meaning. Second, the **Kasavu** saree together with the **absence of elaborate facial make-up** is the single most reliable costume-based identification in this topic, precisely because the neighbouring Kerala form is defined by the opposite - elaborate facial make-up with headgear.

⚠️ **Manipravalam is shared by both Kerala forms** and is therefore **not** a discriminator between Kathakali and Mohiniyattam. It **is** a strong discriminator against Kuchipudi's Telugu and against the Hindustani-accompanied eastern and northern forms.

### 5. The negative-identification rule

This form supplies the clearest instance of a rule that applies across the topic: **some forms are most reliably identified by what they lack**.

| Feature absent | Why the absence identifies |
|---|---|
| **No thumping of footsteps** | separates it from Bharatanatyam and Kathak, both of which foreground percussive footwork |
| **No elaborate facial make-up** | separates it from Kathakali, the other Kerala form |
| **No group or troupe organisation** | it is **essentially a solo performance**, unlike Kuchipudi's team recitals and Sattriya's monastic groups |

❌ And one absence that must not be invented: **Nagabandha is not a Mohiniyattam posture.** The figure-of-eight body connection belongs to **Manipuri**. Both forms are lyrical, both are dominated by grace, and an option set will exploit that similarity.

### 6. Proponents

Famous proponents recorded: **Sunanda Nair, Kalamandalam Kshemavathy, Madhuri Amma, Jayaprabha Menon**.

### Examination use

For Prelims: the etymology of both halves of the name; the solo female character; Vadivelu and the nineteenth century; Travancore and Swathi Thirunal; the two revivers; the grace-plus-vigour formulation; the absence of thumping; Lasya dominance; the 40 *Atavakul*; *Manipravalam*; the white and off-white **Kasavu** with gold brocade; the absence of elaborate facial make-up; the ghungroo; cymbals, veena, drums and flute; and the air association.

For Mains, this form contributes a precise and unusual point: **a classical form can be constituted by selective borrowing from its neighbours**, and the record says so explicitly. That is a stronger claim about the plurality of Indian classical dance than any general statement about regional diversity, because it shows the forms as mutually permeable rather than as sealed regional units - which is exactly the argument a question about cultural synthesis in the performing arts is looking for.

### Traps introduced here

- Describing the form as purely graceful and dropping the recorded borrowing of vigour.
- Assigning Nagabandha to this form.
- Assigning the 40 *Atavakul* to Bharatanatyam.
- Using *Manipravalam* to separate the two Kerala forms, which share it.
- Attributing the revival to one agent when two are recorded.
- Supplying a symbolic meaning for the white and gold costume.
- Forgetting that the developer named here is also one of the four Thanjavur teachers of another form.

### Revision notes

1. **Mohiniyattam** = **Dance of an Enchantress**; '**Mohini**' = beautiful woman, '**attam**' = dance. **Kerala.**
2. Essentially a **solo dance performance by women**; **generally narrates the story of the feminine dance of Vishnu**.
3. **Further developed by Vadivelu in the nineteenth century** - the same name as one of the four **Tanjore Quartet** teachers.
4. Prominence under the **rulers of Travancore**; patronage of **Swathi Thirunal** notable.
5. Fell into **obscurity**; **revived by the Malayali poet V. N. Menon along with Kalyani Amma** - the same poet credited with the 1930s Kathakali revival.
6. Technique: **grace and elegance of Bharatanatyam + vigour of Kathakali**; **marked absence of thumping**; **gentle footwork**; **Lasya dominant**; has its own **Nritta and Nritya** aspects.
7. ***Atavakul* / *Atavus*** = **40 basic dance movements**.
8. Language: ***Manipravalam*** (Sanskrit + Malayalam) - **shared with Kathakali**.
9. Costume: **white and off-white *Kasavu* sarees** with **gold-coloured brocade**; **no elaborate facial make-up**; **ghungroo** on a leather ankle strap.
10. Instruments: **cymbals, veena, drums, flute**. Element: **air**.
11. Proponents: **Sunanda Nair, Kalamandalam Kshemavathy, Madhuri Amma, Jayaprabha Menon**.
12. ❌ **Nagabandha is Manipuri, not Mohiniyattam.**

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Explain why Mohiniyattam is most reliably identified by what it does not do. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

Its positive features are shared. It is lyrical, Lasya-dominated, solo, female-performed and accompanied in *Manipravalam* - and each of those fits at least one other form, *Manipravalam* being shared with the other Kerala form.

Its absences are not shared. There is a **marked absence of thumping** of footsteps, with gentle footwork, which separates it from the percussive idioms of Bharatanatyam and Kathak. There is **no elaborate facial make-up**, which separates it from the other Kerala form, defined by exactly that. And it is **essentially solo**, unlike the team and monastic-group traditions.

Two positives do discriminate and should accompany the absences: the collection of forty basic movements, and the white or off-white *Kasavu* saree with gold brocade.

Caution: do not extend the negative method by adding absences the record does not state.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the shared-positives problem stated 2 + three recorded absences each with the form it excludes 5 + the two discriminating positives 2 + the caution against inventing absences 1.**

### Concept check

**Question:** Mohiniyattam is recorded as combining the grace and elegance of one form with the vigour of another. What does this single sentence establish about the relationship between India's classical dance forms, and what does it not establish?

**Model answer:** It establishes that the forms are **mutually permeable** rather than sealed regional units. A form can be constituted, in part, by deliberately taking qualities from its neighbours - here, grace and elegance from a Tamil Nadu form and vigour from the other Kerala form - which means the map of Indian classical dance is not a set of independent regional developments but a field in which qualities travel. The evidence is reinforced by a second, independent fact from the same form's history: the individual recorded as having further developed it in the nineteenth century is also one of the four Thanjavur teachers who defined the recital order of the Tamil form. Expertise travelled as well as qualities. What the sentence does **not** establish is any chronological claim about which form is older, any claim that one form derived from another, or any hierarchy among them. "Combines the qualities of A and B" is a statement about character, not about descent; the form has its own origin, its own patronage history under named Travancore rulers, its own movement inventory of forty units, its own costume and its own narrative subject. Borrowing a quality is not being derived from a source.

**Misconception to avoid:** Turning the borrowing statement into a genealogy - for example, writing that Mohiniyattam "developed out of" Bharatanatyam and Kathakali. Nothing in the record supports descent. The safest and most accurate formulation keeps the verb the record uses: it **combines** qualities of the two, while remaining a distinct form with its own history.

## Lesson 14 - Kathak: one name, four gharanas, and three social settings

### Visual: one form in three social settings, and four lineages that survived them

