```text
   storytellers who recited verses       STYLES OF DANCING
   from the epics, WITH GESTURES         [the source also uses a strongly
   AND MUSIC                             evaluative phrase for this period -
   traditional dance form of             see section 2 for how to handle it]
   UTTAR PRADESH
                      \                        /
                       \                      /
                        v                    v
                 SETTING 3: THE TWENTIETH CENTURY
                 the classical style REVIVED by LADY LEELA SOKHEY
                                  |
                 FOUR GHARANAS carry the style forward
   +-------------+-------------------------+----------------------------+
   | LUCKNOW     | peak under Nawab        | expression and grace       |
   |             | Wajid Ali Shah          |                            |
   +-------------+-------------------------+----------------------------+
   | JAIPUR      | initiated by Bhanuji    | fluency, speed, long       |
   |             |                         | rhythmic patterns          |
   +-------------+-------------------------+----------------------------+
   | RAIGARH     | patronage of Raja       | percussion music           |
   |             | Chakradhar Singh        |                            |
   +-------------+-------------------------+----------------------------+
   | BANARAS     | developed under         | greater use of the floor;  |
   |             | Janakiprasad            | emphasis on symmetry       |
   +-------------+-------------------------+----------------------------+
```

### 1. Origin, name and region

**Tracing its origins from the *Ras Leela* of Brajbhoomi, Kathak is the traditional dance form of Uttar Pradesh.** It **derived its name from the '*Kathika*' or the storytellers who recited verses from the epics, with gestures and music**.

The etymology is the key to the form's character and should always be written out. The root figure is not a dancer but a **storyteller**, and the storyteller's tools are already three of the four constituents of the Natya Veda - recited **verse**, **gesture** and **music**. A form whose name means "belonging to the storyteller" is a form in which narrative is the organising purpose, and that is why Kathak retains items like *Gat Bhav*, dance without any music or chanting used to outline mythological episodes.

⚠️ Note the shared root. The ***Ras Leela*** of Brajbhoomi appears here as Kathak's origin, and the ***Ras Leela*** Radha-Krishna theme is separately recorded as a recurring theme of **Manipuri**. The same devotional material feeds a north Indian storytelling form and a north-eastern Vaishnava form. State it as a shared source, not as a shared derivation.

### 2. The court branch, and the language used to describe it

The recorded statement is: **"During the Mughal era, the dance form degenerated into a lascivious style and branched off into court dance. It was also influenced by Persian costumes and styles of dancing."**

⚠️ **This sentence requires careful handling, and handling it well is itself a mark of preparation.** Separate the two kinds of content inside it.

- **The descriptive content is usable.** The form **branched off into court dance** during the Mughal era, and it was **influenced by Persian costumes and styles of dancing**. Both are ordinary, evidenced statements about patronage and cross-cultural influence, and both belong in an answer.
- **The evaluative content is not a neutral description.** "Degenerated into a lascivious style" is a **moral verdict on a performance tradition and on the women who carried it**, of a kind produced in abundance by nineteenth- and twentieth-century reform discourse. It belongs to the same vocabulary as the **anti-nautch** campaigning taught in Lesson 22, section 2, and its effect is to convert a change of patronage into a story of decline and rescue.

✅ **The safe and stronger formulation for an answer:** "Under Mughal patronage, Kathak developed a court branch and absorbed Persian costume and movement influence; the courtesan settings in which much of that development took place were later described in strongly moralising terms by reform-era writers, and that language should be distinguished from the historical record of patronage and influence."

That formulation loses nothing factual, gains a source-critical observation, and avoids reproducing a judgement as though it were evidence. It also connects directly to the **Dadra** entry in the folk section of this session, recorded as **extremely popular among the courtesans of the Lucknow region** - the same milieu, described without the verdict.

**The classical style of Kathak was revived by Lady Leela Sokhey in the twentieth century.**

### 3. The four gharanas

**An important feature of Kathak is the development of different gharanas, as it is based on the Hindustani style of music.**

| Gharana | Patron / founder | Recorded emphasis |
|---|---|---|
| **Lucknow** | reached its peak under the reign of **Nawab Wajid Ali Shah** | more importance on **expression and grace** |
| **Jaipur** | initiated by **Bhanuji** | **fluency, speed and long rhythmic patterns** |
| **Raigarh** | developed under the patronage of **Raja Chakradhar Singh** | unique in its emphasis on **percussion music** |
| **Banaras** | developed under **Janakiprasad** | **greater use of the floor**; special emphasis on **symmetry** |

Two things make this table exam-productive beyond simple recall. First, **the causal statement is given**: gharanas developed **because** the form is based on the Hindustani musical style - so the lineage-school structure is inherited from the music tradition rather than invented by the dance. Second, **each emphasis is a distinct technical axis**, and a question that describes a Kathak style by one of these four axes is asking you to name the gharana.

⚠️ **Jaipur appears twice in this topic with two different referents.** The **Jaipur gharana** here is a Kathak lineage initiated by Bhanuji. Jaipur also names lineages in the music topic. Read the question's subject before answering; a stem that names a vocalist is not asking about dance.

### 4. Technique

**Kathak is characterised by the use of intricate footwork and pirouettes.**

That single line is the form's physical signature and is the cleanest contrast in the topic with Bharatanatyam's bent-knee, evenly weighted stance. Where the Tamil form lowers the centre of gravity and distributes weight, Kathak turns on the vertical axis and drives rhythm through the feet. When an option set describes a dancer executing rapid spins with percussive footwork, no further discrimination is needed.

### 5. The eight recital elements

| Element | What it is |
|---|---|
| ***Ananda*** | the **introductory item** through which the dancer enters the stage |
| ***Thaat*** | comprising **soft and varied movements** |
| ***Todas* and *Tukdas*** | **small pieces of fast rhythm** |
| ***Jugalbandi*** | the **main attraction** of a Kathak recital - a **competitive play between the dancer and the tabla player** |
| ***Padhant*** | a special feature in which the dancer **recites complicated *bols* and demonstrates them** |
| ***Tarana*** | similar to *thillana*; comprises **pure rhythmic movements before the end** |
| ***Kramalaya*** | the **concluding piece**, comprising **intricate and fast footwork** |
| ***Gat Bhav*** | **dance without any music or chanting**, used to outline different **mythological episodes** |

Three of these are among the best identifiers in the topic. ***Jugalbandi*** is the only recorded instance in this session of accompaniment framed as a **contest**. ***Padhant*** is the only recorded instance of the dancer **verbally reciting** the rhythmic syllables before demonstrating them. ***Gat Bhav*** is the only recorded instance of **narrative without any sound at all**.

⚠️ Note the cross-topic vocabulary. ***Thaat*** here names a Kathak recital element of soft and varied movements. The same word names a parent-scale category in the music topic. ***Tarana*** here is a Kathak item similar to *thillana*; the same word names a vocal form in the music topic. Two shared words, two different subjects - read the stem.

### 6. Musical setting

**Kathak is generally accompanied with *dhrupad* music. *Taranas*, *thumris* and *ghazals* were also introduced during the Mughal period.**

This is the most explicit music-dance linkage in the topic and it carries the social history in compressed form: the older accompaniment is the austere classical vocal form, and the Mughal period adds three lighter and more lyrical genres. Patronage change is audible in the accompaniment.

⚠️ **Boundary note.** *Dhrupad*, *thumri*, *tarana* and *ghazal* as musical forms are owned by the music topic. They appear here only as the accompaniment that identifies Kathak and dates a change in it.

### 7. Proponents

Famous proponents recorded: **Birju Maharaj, Lacchu Maharaj, Sitara Devi, Damayanti Joshi**.

⚠️ **Sitara Devi** is also recorded in the music topic among the exponents associated with the Benaras lineage. A shared name across two topics is not evidence of a shared category; record each attribution where it belongs.

### Examination use

For Prelims: the Ras Leela origin and the *Kathika* etymology; Uttar Pradesh; the Mughal court branch and Persian influence; Lady Leela Sokhey and the twentieth century; the four gharanas with patrons and emphases; intricate footwork and pirouettes; the eight recital elements with *Jugalbandi*, *Padhant* and *Gat Bhav* as the signatures; *dhrupad* accompaniment with *taranas*, *thumris* and *ghazals* introduced in the Mughal period; and the four proponents.

For Mains, Kathak carries two arguments that no other form in this session carries as well. The first is **syncretism**: a form with a temple-storyteller origin that developed a court branch under Mughal patronage and absorbed Persian costume and movement, whose accompaniment records that change genre by genre. The second is **source criticism**: the recorded description of that period is openly evaluative, and a candidate who separates the patronage history from the moral verdict is doing exactly what a "critically examine" directive asks for.

### Traps introduced here

- Reproducing the moralising verdict as though it were a neutral historical description.
- Attributing the gharana emphases to the wrong lineages.
- Confusing the Kathak *Thaat* and *Tarana* with the identically named music-topic terms.
- Treating Persian influence as the origin of the form rather than as an influence on its court branch.
- Forgetting that the gharana structure is explained by the form's Hindustani musical base.
- Assigning *Jugalbandi* or *Padhant* to another form.

### Revision notes

1. **Kathak** - traditional dance form of **Uttar Pradesh**; origins traced from the ***Ras Leela* of Brajbhoomi**; name from ***Kathika***, storytellers who recited epic verses with gestures and music.
2. **Mughal era**: branched off into **court dance**; influenced by **Persian costumes and styles of dancing**. The source's accompanying phrase is an **evaluative verdict**, not a neutral description - separate the two.
3. The classical style was **revived by Lady Leela Sokhey** in the twentieth century.
4. **Gharanas developed because the form is based on the Hindustani style of music.**
5. **Lucknow** - peak under **Nawab Wajid Ali Shah**; **expression and grace**.
6. **Jaipur** - initiated by **Bhanuji**; **fluency, speed, long rhythmic patterns**.
7. **Raigarh** - patronage of **Raja Chakradhar Singh**; **percussion music**.
8. **Banaras** - under **Janakiprasad**; **greater use of the floor**, **symmetry**.
9. Technique: **intricate footwork and pirouettes**.
10. Recital: ***Ananda, Thaat, Todas and Tukdas, Jugalbandi, Padhant, Tarana, Kramalaya, Gat Bhav***.
11. ***Jugalbandi*** = competitive play with the tabla player; ***Padhant*** = reciting complicated *bols* and demonstrating them; ***Gat Bhav*** = dance without music or chanting.
12. Accompaniment: **dhrupad**; **taranas, thumris and ghazals introduced during the Mughal period**.
13. Proponents: **Birju Maharaj, Lacchu Maharaj, Sitara Devi, Damayanti Joshi**.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Kathak is the clearest instance in Indian classical dance of a tradition reshaped by successive patrons." Examine, with attention to the language in which that reshaping has been described. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The claim holds: the reshaping is legible in the form itself, not only in its history.

Three patronage settings are recorded. The first is devotional and popular: origins traced from the *Ras Leela* of Brajbhoomi and a name derived from the *Kathika*, storytellers who recited epic verses with gestures and music. The second is courtly: during the Mughal era the form branched off into court dance and absorbed Persian costumes and styles of dancing. The third is institutional and modern: the classical style was revived by Lady Leela Sokhey in the twentieth century, and four lineage-schools - Lucknow under Nawab Wajid Ali Shah, Jaipur initiated by Bhanuji, Raigarh under Raja Chakradhar Singh, and Banaras under Janakiprasad - carried differentiated styles forward, each attached to a named patron or founder.

The reshaping is audible as well as visible. The accompaniment is recorded as generally *dhrupad*, with *taranas*, *thumris* and *ghazals* introduced during the Mughal period; and the gharana structure itself is explained by the form's basis in the Hindustani musical style. Patron, repertoire, accompaniment and lineage change together.

The language requires a qualification. The court phase is described in the available record with an explicit moral verdict rather than a neutral one - a mode of description characteristic of reform-era writing about courtesan performance, and visible in the same period's treatment of the Lucknow *Dadra*.

Verdict: the patronage claim holds on the evidence; the decline narrative attached to it is a judgement, and should be reported as such rather than adopted.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = three patronage settings each with named evidence 5 + the four gharanas with patron and emphasis 3 + the accompaniment change as independent corroboration 3 + the source-critical separation of verdict from record 3 + a two-part verdict that accepts the claim and qualifies the language 1.**

### Concept check

**Question:** Kathak's name derives from the storytellers who recited epic verses with gestures and music. Which single item in its recital preserves that origin most directly, and what does its survival tell you about how traditions carry their history?

**Model answer:** The item is ***Gat Bhav*** - dance without any music or chanting, used to outline different mythological episodes. It preserves the storyteller origin more directly than any other element because it strips away everything the court and concert settings added - the accompaniment, the sung text, the rhythmic contest with the drummer - and leaves only what the *Kathika* fundamentally did: convey a narrative from the epics through the body. What its survival shows is that a tradition can carry its earlier history **inside its repertoire** rather than only in accounts about it. The form acquired a court branch, absorbed Persian costume and movement influence, developed four differentiated lineage-schools under named patrons, changed its accompaniment as new genres were introduced, and was reconstructed in the twentieth century - and through all of that it retained one item in which a dancer tells a mythological story in silence. An answer that wants to show how continuity and change coexist in Indian performing arts can do it with that one item, and does not need a general statement about timeless traditions.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that *Gat Bhav* is therefore the "original" or "pure" Kathak and the rest is accretion. Nothing in the record supports that hierarchy. The item preserves the narrative function of the origin; it does not certify that any current item is unchanged from an earlier period, and ranking parts of a living repertoire by supposed purity is exactly the move this session refuses.

## Lesson 15 - Odissi: torso, sculpture and a reconstructed lineage

### Visual: the two postures, the static base and the moving torso

```text
   T R I B H A N G A                        C H O W K
   "the three-bended form of the body"      "hands spread out, depicts
   INNATE to Odissi                          masculinity"
   also used extensively by the
   AMARAVATI sculptors
   also seen in the Harappan bronze
   figure discussed in the sculpture topic

          head bends                             hands spread wide
             \                                    ____________
              \                                  |            |
           torso bends                           |            |
              /                                  |            |
             /                               knees turned out, weight low
        hips bend                            a squared, grounded stance
        THREE BENDS, one line

   THE MOVEMENT PRINCIPLE
   +-------------------------------------------------------------------+
   | During the dance, the LOWER BODY REMAINS LARGELY STATIC, and there |
   | is only MOVEMENT OF THE TORSO. Hand gestures play an important     |
   | role in conveying expressions during the Nritya part.              |
   +-------------------------------------------------------------------+
                                   |
                                   v
   CONSEQUENCE: the dancer creates intricate GEOMETRICAL SHAPES and
   PATTERNS with the body -> the form is known as "MOBILE SCULPTURE"
```

### 1. The earliest evidence and the name

**The caves of Udayagiri-Khandagiri provide some of the earliest examples of Odissi dance.** The dance form **derives its name from the '*Odra nritya*' mentioned in the *Natya Shastra***.

Both halves are examinable and they belong to different kinds of evidence.

- The **cave evidence** is archaeological and visual - figures carved at a named site.
- The **name evidence** is textual - a term appearing in the treatise itself.

⚠️ A form with both a textual name in the *Natyashastra* and early sculptural evidence is unusually well anchored, and candidates therefore over-claim. What the two together support is that **a regional dance idiom of that name was known early**; they do not establish that the modern recital order, technique or repertoire existed then. Lesson 21 sets out the general limit; here, note simply that the modern form is a **reconstruction**, as the carrier history in section 2 makes unavoidable.

### 2. The carrier sequence, in four moves

1. It was **primarily practised by the '*maharis*'** and **patronised by the Jain King Kharavela**.
2. **With the advent of Vaishnavism in the region, the Mahari system became defunct.**
3. **Instead, young boys were recruited and dressed as females to continue the art form. They came to be known as '*Gotipuas*'.**
4. **Another variant of this art, '*Nartala*', continued to be practised at the royal courts.**

Four observations, each of which is a mark in an answer.

- **The earliest named patron is a Jain king.** Kharavela's patronage of a temple-associated female performing tradition is a useful and unexpected fact, and it prevents the assumption that every dance patronage in early India was Brahmanical.
- **The displacing force is religious, and it is the same force that reorganised Kuchipudi.** Vaishnavism appears twice in this topic as an access rule.
- **The substitute carrier acquired a name and an identity.** *Gotipua* means **'single boy'**; the tradition originates from the Mahari tradition around the **sixteenth century**, features **acrobatic and yogic movements**, and is recorded as a **precursor to classical Odissi**.
- **A parallel court channel existed.** *Nartala* continued at the royal courts, so the art survived through **two** channels with different patrons, not one.

**In the mid-twentieth century, Odissi gained international acclaim due to the efforts of Charles Fabri and Indrani Rahman.**

⚠️ Note the precise claim: **international acclaim**, not revival from nothing. Two named agents, one of whom is also recorded among Kuchipudi's proponents. Keep the function precise - this pair is credited with international recognition, in the same way that Rukmini Devi Arundale is credited with global recognition for Bharatanatyam and E. Krishna Iyer with its revival. **Revival, recognition and reconstruction are three different functions**, and an examiner testing this topic's named actors is usually testing whether you know which function each performed.

### 3. Technique and the sculptural description

- It is **similar to Bharatnatyam in the use of Mudras and postures to express emotions**.
- The ***tribhanga*** posture, the **three-bended form of the body**, is **innate to Odissi**. The ***Chowk*** posture, **with hands spread out, depicts masculinity**.
- **During the dance, the lower body remains largely static, and there is only movement of the torso.** **Hand gestures play an important role in conveying expressions during the *Nritya* part.**
- The form is **unique in its representation of gracefulness, sensuality and beauty**. The dancers **create intricate geometrical shapes and patterns with their bodies**; hence it is known as **'mobile sculpture'**.

The torso statement is the technical core and it explains the sculptural description. If the lower body is largely static and the movement is carried by the torso, then what the audience sees is a **succession of held shapes** rather than a continuous travelling of the body through space. A held shape is what a sculpture is - hence "mobile sculpture", which is a technical consequence and not a compliment.

### 4. The five-part recital, with two ways of ending

| Element | What it is |
|---|---|
| ***Mangalacharan*** | the beginning, where a **flower is offered to Mother Earth** |
| ***Batu nritya*** | comprising the dance; it has **Tribhanga and Chowk** postures |
| ***Pallavi*** | includes **use of facial expressions** and the **representation of the song** |
| ***Tharijham*** | consisting of **pure dance before the conclusion** |
| **Conclusion** | of **two types**: ***Moksha***, which includes **joyous movements signifying liberation**; or ***Trikhanda majura***, in which the performer **takes leave from the gods, the audience and the stage** |

⚠️ The **two conclusions** are a favourite discriminating detail, and the second is the more interesting: taking leave **from the gods, the audience and the stage** mirrors the three addressees of the Bharatanatyam opening item - God, teacher and audience. The classical recital, in both forms, opens and closes by naming the parties to whom the performance is owed.

### 5. Music, lyrics and costume

- **Odissi dance is accompanied by Hindustani classical music**, and the instruments generally used are ***manjira*** (cymbals), ***pakhawaj*** (drums), **sitar** and **flute**.
- **The lyrics of the *Gita Govinda*, written by Jayadeva, are used**, along with **compositions of some local poets**.
- The **woman dancer wears an elaborate hairstyle**, with **silver jewellery** and a **long necklace**. Dancers wear an **elaborate belt on the waist** and **ghungroo** on the feet. Female performers wear **bright colour Bomkai and Sambalpuri sarees**.
- The dance form **symbolises the element of water**.

⚠️ **The accompaniment is a powerful discriminator.** An eastern Indian form accompanied by **Hindustani** classical music cuts directly against the assumption that southern and eastern forms use Carnatic music. Set it beside Kuchipudi's Carnatic accompaniment and the contrast is a ready-made identification pair.

⚠️ **Jayadeva appears twice in this topic.** The *Gita Govinda* supplies Odissi's lyrics, and **compositions of Jayadeva and Chandidas are used extensively** in **Manipuri**. The shared poet is a real link between two Vaishnava traditions, and it is also a reason why "Vaishnava devotional lyrics" is a **useless discriminator** between them. Use the instruments instead: *manjira*, *pakhawaj*, sitar and flute against the *pung*.

### 6. Proponents

Famous proponents recorded: **Guru Pankaj Charan Das, Guru Kelucharan Mohapatra, Sonal Mansingh, Sharon Lowen (USA), Anandini Dasi (Argentina)**.

⚠️ The last two entries carry a recorded country attribution. That is a fact about the form's international reach in the period after the mid-twentieth-century acclaim, and it is worth one sentence in an answer about the globalisation of Indian classical dance. ❌ No honour or award is recorded here for any of these names; do not supply one.

### Examination use

For Prelims: Udayagiri-Khandagiri; *Odra nritya* in the *Natya Shastra*; the maharis and Kharavela; the Vaishnava displacement, the gotipuas and *Nartala*; Charles Fabri and Indrani Rahman with the mid-twentieth century; tribhanga and chowk with their descriptions; the static lower body with torso movement; 'mobile sculpture'; the five recital elements and **both** conclusions; Hindustani accompaniment with *manjira*, *pakhawaj*, sitar and flute; the *Gita Govinda* and Jayadeva; Bomkai and Sambalpuri sarees with silver jewellery; the water association; and the proponents with their two country attributions.

For Mains, Odissi is the single best case for the **carrier argument**: a form whose practising community was displaced, whose art was continued by a substituted carrier that developed its own idiom and its own name, which ran in parallel with a court variant, and which was later brought to international attention by two named individuals. Every stage of that chain is recorded, which is what makes it usable as evidence rather than as assertion.

### Traps introduced here

- Assuming Carnatic accompaniment because the form is not north Indian.
- Using Vaishnava devotional lyrics to separate Odissi from Manipuri; they share a poet.
- Giving Odissi one conclusion when two are recorded.
- Crediting Fabri and Rahman with reviving the form rather than with its international acclaim.
- Treating the gotipua tradition as a gap rather than as a precursor with its own idiom.
- Assigning tribhanga to a form other than Odissi, or forgetting its sculptural use.

### Revision notes

1. **Odissi** - Odisha. Earliest examples: the caves of **Udayagiri-Khandagiri**. Name from ***Odra nritya*** mentioned in the ***Natya Shastra***.
2. Practised by the ***maharis***; patronised by the **Jain King Kharavela**.
3. **Vaishnavism -> the Mahari system became defunct -> young boys dressed as females continued the art as *Gotipuas*.** ***Nartala*** continued at the **royal courts**.
4. ***Gotipua*** = 'single boy'; from the Mahari tradition around the **sixteenth century**; **acrobatic and yogic** movements; **precursor to classical Odissi**.
5. **Mid-twentieth century: international acclaim through Charles Fabri and Indrani Rahman.**
6. ***Tribhanga*** = three-bended form, **innate** to Odissi, also used extensively by the **Amaravati sculptors**; ***Chowk*** = hands spread out, **depicts masculinity**.
7. **Lower body largely static; movement of the torso**; hand gestures important in the *Nritya* part; hence **'mobile sculpture'**.
8. Recital: ***Mangalacharan*** (flower offered to Mother Earth) -> ***Batu nritya*** -> ***Pallavi*** -> ***Tharijham*** -> conclusion, either ***Moksha*** (joyous movements signifying liberation) or ***Trikhanda majura*** (leave taken from the gods, the audience and the stage).
9. Accompaniment: **Hindustani classical music**; **manjira, pakhawaj, sitar, flute**.
10. Lyrics: ***Gita Govinda*** by **Jayadeva**, plus local poets.
11. Costume: elaborate hairstyle, **silver jewellery**, long necklace, elaborate waist belt, **ghungroo**; **Bomkai and Sambalpuri** sarees. Element: **water**.
12. Proponents: **Pankaj Charan Das, Kelucharan Mohapatra, Sonal Mansingh, Sharon Lowen (USA), Anandini Dasi (Argentina)**.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Odissi's modern history is a sequence of substituted carriers rather than a single revival." Critically examine. (20 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The proposition is well supported, and it is a better description of this form's history than any rescue narrative.

**The sequence.** The form was primarily practised by the *maharis* and patronised by the Jain king Kharavela. With the advent of Vaishnavism in the region the mahari system became defunct. Young boys were then recruited and dressed as females to continue the art, becoming known as *gotipuas*; the tradition dates from around the sixteenth century, features acrobatic and yogic movements, and is recorded as a precursor to the classical form now performed. A further variant, *Nartala*, continued in parallel at the royal courts. In the mid-twentieth century the form gained international acclaim through two named individuals.

**Why "substitution" is the right word.** No stage was a recovery of the previous one. The substitute carrier acquired its own name, its own movement idiom and its own eventual status in the lineage of the modern form - so what survived was transformed in surviving.

**Why "single revival" fails.** The two named mid-century figures are credited with international **acclaim**, not with revival from nothing, and a parallel court channel had been running throughout.

**The qualification.** The record is compressed and names no dates for the mahari decline, so the chain is a sequence of recorded states rather than a dated timeline.

Verdict: continuity here is carried by replacement, which is why the modern form cannot be read directly off its early sculptural evidence.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = the proposition preferred over a rescue narrative with a reason 2 + the four-stage sequence with patron, cause, substitute and parallel channel 8 + the substitution-transforms argument 4 + the correct function assigned to the mid-century figures 3 + the dating qualification 2 + a verdict linking replacement to the sculptural limit 1.**

### Concept check

**Question:** Odissi is described as "mobile sculpture". Show that this description is a technical consequence of a recorded movement principle rather than a compliment, and state the one thing it must not be used to prove.

**Model answer:** The description follows directly from two recorded technical facts. First, **during the dance the lower body remains largely static and there is only movement of the torso** - so the dancer is not travelling through space in the way a footwork-driven form does; the body stays substantially in place while its upper structure deflects. Second, the dancer **creates intricate geometrical shapes and patterns with the body**, using two named postures, the three-bended *tribhanga* and the squared, hands-spread *chowk*. A body that remains in place and articulates through the torso produces a succession of held, geometrically defined shapes - which is exactly what a piece of sculpture is, except that here the shape changes. "Mobile sculpture" is therefore a description of the movement mechanics, and it can be derived from the technique without ever using the word beautiful. What it must **not** be used to prove is that the form as now performed is the form depicted in early sculpture. The temptation is obvious, because the form has early cave evidence, a *Natyashastra* name, a shared posture with the Amaravati sculptors, and a description that invites the comparison. But the carrier history is decisive against it: the practising community's system became defunct, the art was continued by a substituted carrier that developed its own acrobatic and yogic idiom, a court variant ran in parallel, and the modern form gained international currency only in the mid-twentieth century.

**Misconception to avoid:** Running the analogy backwards - concluding that because Odissi looks sculptural and sculptors used *tribhanga*, the sculptures are depictions of Odissi. The shared posture shows a shared movement idiom across arts and regions, which is precisely why the same posture appears in Amaravati work far from Odisha. Shared idiom, not shared choreography.

## Lesson 16 - Manipuri: devotion, restraint and the drum at the centre

Progress: 16/23 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the mythological origin, the fifteenth-century Vaishnava turn, the eighteenth-century revival and the Santiniketan introduction, the veil and the restraint principle, Nagabandha, the Ras Leela theme, the pung and the accompanying instruments, and the two related Manipuri forms

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the full Manipuri section including the mythological origin, the Vaishnava turn and the Krishna theme, the eighteenth-century ruler and the Santiniketan introduction, the devotion-not-sensuality statement, the veil and facial-expression record, the Tandava and Lasya balance, the costume, the Nagabandha posture, the Ras Leela theme, the drum and the other instruments, the poets whose compositions are used, the two related forms and the proponent list
CA search: "Not applicable - regional form lesson; no genuine current linkage"
CA found: None found; none claimed
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: a form built around a deliberate subtraction

```text
   ORIGIN AND TURNS
   mythological origin: the celestial dance of SHIVA and PARVATI in the
   valleys of Manipur, along with the local 'GANDHARVAS'
             |
   15th century: prominence with the advent of VAISHNAVISM
                 KRISHNA becomes the central theme
             |
   18th century: RAJA BHAG CHANDRA of Manipur tries to revive it
             |
   modern:    RABINDRANATH TAGORE brings it back into the limelight by
              introducing it at SANTINIKETAN (West Bengal)

   THE DESIGN DECISION
   +---------------------------+     +----------------------------------+
   | WHAT IS CLOSED            |     | WHAT CARRIES THE LOAD INSTEAD    |
   |---------------------------|     |----------------------------------|
   | faces COVERED with a      | ->  | HAND GESTURES                    |
   | THIN VEIL                 |     | GENTLE MOVEMENT OF FEET          |
   | facial expression of      |     | slow and gracious movements of   |
   | LESSER IMPORTANCE         |     | HAND and KNEE positions          |
   |                           |     | the NAGABANDHA posture: the body |
   | emphasis on DEVOTION      |     | connected through curves in the  |
   | and NOT SENSUALITY        |     | shape of '8'                     |
   +---------------------------+     +----------------------------------+
                          |
                          v
   THE SOUND: the drum - PUNG - is an INTRICATE ELEMENT of the recital
   with FLUTE, KHARTALS (wood clapper), DHOLS and others
   theme: RAS LEELA (the Radha-Krishna love story)
   texts: compositions of JAYADEVA and CHANDIDAS used extensively
```

### 1. Origin and the two turning points

**The Manipuri dance form finds its mythological origin in the celestial dance of Shiva and Parvati in the valleys of Manipur along with the local '*Gandharvas*'.**

Note the form of that sentence: it is explicitly a **mythological** origin, and it is **locally specified** - not a generic divine origin but one set in the valleys of Manipur with local celestial musicians. Reproduce that specificity; it is the difference between a recalled fact and a recalled formula.

**The dance gained prominence with the advent of Vaishnavism in the fifteenth century. Krishna became the central theme of this dance form. It is performed generally by female dancers.**

This is the **third** appearance of Vaishnavism as a decisive force in this topic, and it is the one that works differently from the other two. In Odisha it made the existing carrier system defunct; in Andhra it produced a male-Brahmin monopoly; here it is recorded as bringing the form to **prominence** and supplying its **central theme**. The general lesson for an answer: a religious movement's effect on a performing tradition is **region-specific and cannot be generalised** - it displaced in one place, restricted in another and elevated in a third.

**In modern times, Raja Bhag Chandra of Manipur in the eighteenth century tried to revive Manipuri dance. Rabindranath Tagore brought back the dance form into the limelight when he introduced it in Shantiniketan (West Bengal).**

⚠️ Two agents, two centuries, two kinds of action. A ruler **attempting** a revival in the eighteenth century, and a poet **introducing** the form at an institution outside its home state in the modern period. The second is the only instance in this topic of a classical form being carried into national visibility through an **educational institution in another region** - a point that belongs in any answer on modern institutions and the arts.

### 2. The restraint principle

- **Manipuri dance is unique in its emphasis on devotion and not sensuality.**
- **The faces are covered with a thin veil, and facial expression is of lesser importance. Hand gestures and gentle movement of feet are important.**
- **While the dance incorporates both Tandava and Lasya, emphasis is laid on the latter.**
- **The female dancers wear unique long skirts. The focus is mainly on slow and gracious movements of hand and knee positions.**

The word **unique** in the first bullet is the source's own, and it is worth using, because the contrast it draws is real. Odissi is recorded as unique in its representation of **gracefulness, sensuality and beauty**; Manipuri is recorded as unique in its emphasis on **devotion and not sensuality**. Two eastern Vaishnava forms, adjacent in the syllabus, described in directly opposing terms. That pairing is the most efficient way to remember both.

The veil is the structural consequence. A form that has removed sensuality from its expressive register and covers the face has closed the channel through which the erotic and the emotional are most directly communicated, and it must therefore load the hands, the feet and the knee positions instead. Lesson 5 set this out as a general principle - a form is partly defined by which channels of *abhinaya* it privileges and which it closes - and Manipuri is its clearest instance.

### 3. Nagabandha

**Nagabandha mudra, in which the body is connected through curves in the shape of '8', is an important posture in the Manipuri dance form.**

❌ **Nagabandha is Manipuri, not Mohiniyattam.** This is the single most deliberately planted regional confusion in the classical-forms portion of the topic, and the reason is easy to see: both forms are dominated by *Lasya*, both are performed mainly by women, both are described in the vocabulary of grace, and both have names that sound alike to an unprepared ear. The discriminators are hard and specific: **veiled face, pung drum, Ras Leela theme, long skirt and Nagabandha** against **Kasavu saree, absence of thumping, 40 Atavakul, Manipravalam and the feminine dance of Vishnu**.

### 4. Theme, sound and text

- ***Ras Leela*** (the **Radha-Krishna love story**) is a **recurring theme** of the Manipuri dance recital.
- **The drum - *pung* - is an intricate element of the recital.** **Flute, *khartals* (wood clapper), *dhols*** and others also accompany the music.
- **Compositions of *Jayadeva* and *Chandidas* are used extensively.**

The ***pung*** is the strongest single identifier this form has. No other form in this session names a drum as **an intricate element of the recital** in that way, and the word itself is unmistakable. When an option set names an instrument, the drum is what decides the answer: *pung* for Manipuri, *chenda* and *maddala* for Kathakali, *khol* for Sattriya, *mridangam* for Bharatanatyam and Kuchipudi, *pakhawaj* for Odissi, *tabla* in the Kathak *jugalbandi*.

⚠️ **Jayadeva is shared with Odissi**, whose lyrics come from his *Gita Govinda*. **Chandidas is recorded only here.** If a stem names both poets together, it is pointing at Manipuri; if it names the *Gita Govinda*, it is pointing at Odissi.

### 5. Two related Manipuri forms

**Thang-ta and Sankirtana are also influenced by Manipuri dance.**

Both deserve a line, because both appear elsewhere in this session and in recorded objective material.

- ***Thang-Ta*** is separately recorded among the folk dances of **Manipur**: ***Thang*** means **sword** and ***Ta*** means **spear**, and the performance is a unique display of **skill, creativity and agility** in which the performers enact a **mock fight sequence - leaping to attack and defend**. It is developed with the martial forms in Lesson 19.
- ***Sankirtana*** is named here as influenced by Manipuri dance. ⚠️ Its full treatment as a musical and devotional practice belongs to the music topic; this session records only the relation.

### 6. Proponents

Famous proponents recorded: **the Jhaveri sisters (Nayana, Suverna, Ranjana and Darshana)**, **Guru Bipin Singha**, **N Madhabi Devi**.

⚠️ The **Jhaveri sisters** are recorded as a set of four named siblings. That is unusual enough in this topic to be a reliable identification cue, and the four given names should be reproduced when the question permits.

### Examination use

For Prelims: the Shiva-Parvati and Gandharva mythological origin; the fifteenth-century Vaishnava turn with Krishna as the central theme; Raja Bhag Chandra and the eighteenth century; Tagore and Santiniketan; devotion and not sensuality; the thin veil with facial expression of lesser importance; both aspects with emphasis on Lasya; the long skirts; **Nagabandha** and its figure-of-eight description; *Ras Leela*; the *pung* with flute, *khartals* and *dhols*; Jayadeva and Chandidas; Thang-ta and Sankirtana as influenced forms; and the four Jhaveri sisters with Guru Bipin Singha and N Madhabi Devi.

For Mains, this form supplies the sharpest available evidence for two claims. First, that **Indian classical dance is not a single aesthetic**: two eastern Vaishnava forms are described in directly opposing terms on sensuality, using the source's own word *unique* for each. Second, that **institutions outside a form's home region can be decisive for its national visibility**, evidenced by a Bengal institution and a Bengali poet carrying a Manipur form into the limelight.

### Traps introduced here

- Assigning Nagabandha to Mohiniyattam.
- Treating facial expression as central because the form is classical; here it is expressly of lesser importance.
- Using Vaishnava devotion or Jayadeva to separate Manipuri from Odissi.
- Crediting Tagore with reviving the form; he is recorded as bringing it back into the limelight by introducing it at Santiniketan.
- Generalising the effect of Vaishnavism across regions.
- Forgetting the two related Manipur forms named in the record.

### Revision notes

1. **Manipuri** - Manipur. Mythological origin: the **celestial dance of Shiva and Parvati** in the valleys of Manipur, with the local ***Gandharvas***.
2. Prominence with **Vaishnavism in the fifteenth century**; **Krishna** becomes the central theme; performed **generally by female dancers**.
3. **Raja Bhag Chandra**, eighteenth century, **tried to revive** it; **Rabindranath Tagore** brought it into the limelight by introducing it at **Santiniketan**.
4. **Unique in its emphasis on devotion and not sensuality.**
5. **Faces covered with a thin veil; facial expression of lesser importance; hand gestures and gentle foot movement important.**
6. Incorporates **both Tandava and Lasya**, with **emphasis on Lasya**; female dancers wear **unique long skirts**; focus on **slow and gracious movements of hand and knee positions**.
7. ***Nagabandha* mudra** - body connected through curves in the shape of **'8'**. **Manipuri, not Mohiniyattam.**
8. Theme: ***Ras Leela*** - the **Radha-Krishna** love story.
9. Sound: the drum ***pung*** is an **intricate element**; with **flute, *khartals* (wood clapper), *dhols***.
10. Texts: compositions of ***Jayadeva*** and ***Chandidas*** used extensively. Jayadeva is **shared with Odissi**; Chandidas is not.
11. ***Thang-ta*** and ***Sankirtana*** are also **influenced by** Manipuri dance.
12. Proponents: the **Jhaveri sisters (Nayana, Suverna, Ranjana, Darshana)**, **Guru Bipin Singha**, **N Madhabi Devi**.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Assess the significance of the introduction of Manipuri at Santiniketan for the modern history of Indian classical dance. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

Its significance is structural rather than local. Rabindranath Tagore brought the form back into the limelight by introducing it at Santiniketan, in West Bengal - and it is the only instance in this topic of a classical form gaining national visibility through an **educational institution outside its home region**.

Three consequences follow. It shows that institutions, not only patrons or gurus, can carry a form into wider view. It shows that visibility could travel without the form leaving its own carriers, since the tradition continued in Manipur. And it precedes and prefigures the later institutional layer of academies, canons and safeguarding lists.

Set beside an eighteenth-century royal attempt to revive the same form, it also shows two different kinds of intervention - princely and educational - acting on one tradition across two centuries.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the institution-outside-the-home-region point stated precisely 3 + three consequences each distinct 5 + the contrast with the earlier royal attempt 2.**

### Concept check

**Question:** Odissi is described as unique in its representation of gracefulness, sensuality and beauty; Manipuri is described as unique in its emphasis on devotion and not sensuality. Both are eastern Vaishnava traditions drawing on Radha-Krishna material and both use compositions of the same poet. How can two traditions with so much shared material be described in opposite terms?

**Model answer:** Because the shared material is the **subject**, and the descriptions are about the **treatment**. The Radha-Krishna narrative and Jayadeva's verse supply both forms with the same emotional situation - longing, play, separation and union - and each tradition then makes an independent decision about which expressive channels will carry it. Odissi opens the channels: the torso deflects through *tribhanga*, the body creates geometrical shapes, facial expression is used in the *Pallavi*, and the form is described in terms of gracefulness, sensuality and beauty. Manipuri closes one of the principal channels: the face is covered with a thin veil, facial expression is expressly of lesser importance, the emphasis is on devotion and not sensuality, and the load is carried by hand gestures, gentle foot movement and slow movements of the hand and knee positions. The same story is being told with a different set of instruments, and the traditions are differentiated by their **restraint rules** rather than by their content. This also explains why the devotional-Vaishnava description is useless for identification: it is what the two forms share. The identification has to be made on the channel decision and its physical markers - veil, *pung*, *Nagabandha*, long skirt on one side; torso deflection, *tribhanga* and *chowk*, *pakhawaj* and Hindustani accompaniment on the other.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that Manipuri is the more devotional of the two because it removes sensuality. The Odissi repertoire is built on the *Gita Govinda*, a devotional text, and its recital opens with a flower offered to Mother Earth and may close with movements signifying liberation. Both are devotional traditions; they differ on what is permissible in the expressive register, which is a question about performance convention, not about depth of devotion.

## Lesson 17 - Sattriya: a dance form whose institution is a monastery

Progress: 17/23 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the fifteenth-century founder and the monastic setting, the treatise mention, the two absorbed traditions, the Bhokot carrier and daily ritual, the Borgeet repertoire, technique and costume, the two modern streams, and Ankia Naat and Bhaona

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the full Sattriya section including the founder and century, the sattra derivation, the Natya Shastra mention, the Bhakti inspiration, the two amalgamated Assamese traditions, the devotional focus, the three components, the Bhokot and daily-ritual record, the instruments and the Borgeet attribution, the rhythmic and postural emphasis, the strict rules, the costume record for both sexes, the two modern streams and the Ankia Naat and Bhaona records
