CA found: None found; none claimed
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the only form in this topic whose carrier is an institution by design

```text
   FOUNDER AND SETTING
   introduced in modern form by the Vaishnava Saint SANKARDEV in the
   15th century CE in ASSAM
   inspired by the BHAKTI MOVEMENT
   name derives from the Vaishnava monasteries known as 'SATTRAS',
   where it was primarily practised
   finds mention in the ancient text 'NATYA SHASTRA' of sage Bharat Muni

   WHAT WAS AMALGAMATED
   +-----------------------+   +-----------------------+
   |       OJAPALI         | + |       DEVADASI        |  = SATTRIYA
   | (Assamese tradition)  |   | (Assamese tradition)  |
   +-----------------------+   +-----------------------+

   THE CARRIER                         THE REPERTOIRE
   performed generally IN A GROUP      songs are compositions of
   by MALE MONKS known as 'BHOKOTS'    SANKARDEV known as 'BORGEETS'
   as part of their DAILY RITUALS
   or on FESTIVALS                     instruments: KHOL (drum),
                                       MANJIRA (cymbals), FLUTE

   THE MODERN SPLIT                    THE RELATED DRAMATIC FORMS
   two separate streams:               ANKIA NAAT - play or musical drama,
     GAYAN-BHAYANAR NACH               originally written in BRAJAVALI,
     KHARMANAR NACH                    a mix of Assamese and Maithili
                                       BHAONA - a similar form, based on
                                       stories of Lord Krishna
```

### 1. Founder, date, setting and text

**Sattriya dance in modern form was introduced by the Vaishnava Saint Sankardev in the fifteenth century CE in Assam.** The art form **derives its name from the Vaishnava monasteries known as '*Sattras*', where it was primarily practised**. It **finds mention in the ancient text '*Natya Shastra*' of sage Bharat Muni**. It is **inspired from the Bhakti movement**.

Four separable claims sit in that paragraph and a careful answer keeps them separate.

- **"In modern form"** is the source's own qualification and it is doing real work. The claim is not that the founder created dance in Assam, but that he introduced the form **as it now exists**.
- **The name comes from an institution**, not from a place, a text or a technique. No other form among the eight is named after the institution that houses it.
- **The treatise mention** anchors the form in the same textual tradition as the rest, which is one reason it sits among the classical eight.
- **The Bhakti movement** supplies the devotional impulse, which routes outward to the medieval history and religion topics for the movement itself.

⚠️ Sattriya's admission to the national academy's eight-form list is widely **reported** to have occurred in **2000**, and a bounded check for this session did not reach a primary record confirming that year. Use it, if at all, as a reported year. The analytically stronger observation - and the one that cannot be challenged - is that **the list has been added to within living memory**, which by itself proves that the eight-form canon is an institutional act rather than an ancient classification.

### 2. What was amalgamated

**The dance form was an amalgamation of various dance forms prevalent in Assam, mainly *Ojapali* and *Devadasi*.**

⚠️ **Read the second name carefully.** *Devadasi* here names **a dance tradition prevalent in Assam** that was absorbed into the new form. It is **not** the Tamil Nadu devadasi tradition from which Bharatanatyam's Sadir derives, and the two must not be merged merely because the word is the same. This is a genuine trap, and the discipline it demands - check the region before assuming the referent - applies across this topic.

### 3. The carrier and the occasion

- **The focus of the Sattriya recitals is to own the devotional aspect of dance and narrate mythological stories of Vishnu.**
- **Sattriya dance also includes *Nritta*, *Nritya* and *Natya*.**
- **The dance is generally performed in a group by male monks known as '*Bhokots*' as part of their daily rituals or even on festivals.**

The carrier statement is the most distinctive in the eight-form set and was set out structurally in Lesson 9. Restate the three consequences here because they are what an answer is built from: the occasion is **daily** rather than periodic; the institution **recruits** rather than reproduces, which gives it a different survival mechanism; and the repertoire is **institutionally authored**.

### 4. Repertoire, sound and technique

- **Khol (drum), cymbals (*Manjira*) and flute** form the major accompanying instruments. **The songs are a composition of Sankardev known as '*Borgeets*'.**
- **There is great emphasis on rhythmic syllables and dance postures along with footwork. It combines both *Lasya* and *Tandava* elements.**
- **The Sattriya dance tradition has strictly laid down rules in respect to hand gestures and footwork, and it plays a very important role.**

Two points.

The ***Borgeet*** attribution is exceptional in this topic. Elsewhere a form's texts come from a poet outside the tradition - Jayadeva for Odissi and Manipuri, Chandidas for Manipuri - or from a general body of epic and puranic material. Here the songs are **the founder's own compositions**, carried inside the institution he founded. Founder, institution, repertoire and carrier are a single package, and that integration is what makes the form so durable.

The **strictly laid down rules** statement matters for the codification argument of Lesson 6: an art transmitted inside a monastic order can afford - and needs - a stricter rule set than one transmitted through competing households, because its purpose is ritual correctness rather than stylistic distinction.

### 5. Costume

- **Male dancers**: **Dhoti** and ***Paguri*** (turban).
- **Female dancers**: traditional Assamese jewellery, ***Ghuri*** and ***Chador***, made in **Pat silk**.
- **Waist cloth is worn by both men and women.**

⚠️ Note that a **female costume is recorded** for a tradition described as generally performed by male monks. Both statements stand together: the monastic performance is by *Bhokots*, and the tradition's costume record includes attire for female dancers. Do not delete either half.

### 6. The two modern streams, and the dramatic forms

**In modern times, Sattriya dance has evolved into two separate streams - the *Gayan-Bhayanar Nach* and the *Kharmanar Nach*.**

***Ankia Naat***: a type of Sattriya, it **involves play or musical-drama**. It was **originally written in a mix of the Assamese and Maithili language called *Brajavali***. Another similar form is ***Bhaona***, which is **based on stories of Lord Krishna**.

⚠️ ***Brajavali*** is the third mixed performance language in this topic, and the set should be learned together: ***Manipravalam*** (Sanskrit + Malayalam) for both Kerala forms; ***Brajavali*** (Assamese + Maithili) for *Ankia Naat*; and **Telugu** as a single-language recital medium for Kuchipudi. Mixed literary languages created for performance are a recurring feature of Indian dance-drama, and naming two of them with their components is a strong Mains detail.

⚠️ **Boundary note.** *Ankia Naat* and *Bhaona* are dance-drama forms and are owned here because danced movement is central to them and because they are recorded as types of Sattriya. Their treatment as **theatre** - dramaturgy, stagecraft as a theatrical tradition, and comparison with other folk theatre - belongs to the theatre topic.

### Examination use

For Prelims: Sankardev and the fifteenth century CE; the *sattra* derivation; the *Natya Shastra* mention; the Bhakti inspiration; *Ojapali* and *Devadasi* as the amalgamated Assamese traditions; Vishnu narratives; all three components; *Bhokots* and daily rituals; *khol*, *manjira* and flute; *Borgeets* as Sankardev's compositions; rhythmic syllables, postures and footwork with strictly laid-down rules; both *Lasya* and *Tandava*; Dhoti and *Paguri*; *Ghuri* and *Chador* in **Pat silk**; the waist cloth worn by both; the two modern streams; and *Ankia Naat* with *Brajavali* and *Bhaona*.

For Mains, Sattriya answers a question no other form can: **what does it take for a performing tradition to survive without a court, without a hereditary caste of performers, and without a twentieth-century rescuer?** The answer is an institution with a recruitment mechanism, a daily ritual occasion, an authored repertoire and a strict rule set. Deployed against Kathakali's patronage collapse and Bharatanatyam's carrier displacement, it makes the case that **institutional form, not antiquity, predicts survival**.

### Traps introduced here

- Merging the Assamese *Devadasi* tradition with the Tamil Nadu devadasi tradition.
- Dropping "in modern form" from the founder statement.
- Treating the form as having no female costume record because the monastic performance is by male monks.
- Confusing the two modern streams, or omitting one.
- Attributing *Brajavali* to the wrong pair of languages, or to the wrong form.
- Attributing the *Borgeets* to a poet other than the founder.
- Asserting the recognition year as established when it is reported.

### Revision notes

1. **Sattriya** - Assam; introduced **in modern form** by the Vaishnava saint **Sankardev** in the **fifteenth century CE**; inspired by the **Bhakti movement**.
2. Name from the Vaishnava monasteries called ***Sattras***; finds mention in the ***Natya Shastra***.
3. Amalgamation of Assamese traditions, **mainly *Ojapali* and *Devadasi*** - the latter an **Assamese** tradition, not the Tamil one.
4. Focus: the **devotional aspect** and **mythological stories of Vishnu**; includes **Nritta, Nritya and Natya**.
5. Performed **generally in a group by male monks, the *Bhokots***, as part of **daily rituals** or at festivals.
6. Instruments: ***Khol*** (drum), ***Manjira*** (cymbals), **flute**. Songs: ***Borgeets***, compositions of **Sankardev**.
7. Great emphasis on **rhythmic syllables, dance postures and footwork**; combines **Lasya and Tandava**; **strictly laid down rules** for hand gestures and footwork.
8. Costume: men - **Dhoti and *Paguri*** (turban); women - Assamese jewellery, ***Ghuri* and *Chador*** in **Pat silk**; **waist cloth** worn by both.
9. Two modern streams: ***Gayan-Bhayanar Nach*** and ***Kharmanar Nach***.
10. ***Ankia Naat*** - a type of Sattriya; play or musical-drama; originally in ***Brajavali***, a mix of **Assamese and Maithili**. ***Bhaona*** - similar, based on stories of **Lord Krishna**.
11. Mixed performance languages: **Manipravalam** (Sanskrit + Malayalam), **Brajavali** (Assamese + Maithili).
12. Academy recognition **reported as 2000**; treat as reported.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Assess the proposition that the survival of a classical performing tradition in India has depended more on its institutional form than on its antiquity. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

The proposition holds on comparative evidence.

Sattriya's institutional form is a Vaishnava monastery. The *sattra* recruits rather than inherits, its performance is embedded in the daily ritual of resident monks called *Bhokots*, its repertoire consists of the founder's own *Borgeets*, and its rules for hand gesture and footwork are strictly laid down. The package reproduces itself without an external patron.

Kathakali's institutional form was patronage by feudal lords. When that set-up broke down the art declined, and recovery required both a new patron and a named agent in the 1930s.

Bharatanatyam's carrier was a hereditary temple-attached community. When that community's position was dismantled the form nearly vanished, and what returned was a reconstruction.

The record makes none of the three older than the others; what differs is who held the art.

Qualification: institutional form predicts resilience, not permanence; a monastery would not survive the collapse of its order.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the proposition restated as a testable comparison 1 + the monastic package itemised with four features 3 + the patronage-collapse case with its recovery conditions 2 + the carrier-displacement case with its reconstruction outcome 2 + the observation that antiquity is not the differentiating variable 1 + a qualification that limits the claim to resilience 1.**

### Concept check

**Question:** Sattriya is amalgamated from Assamese traditions "mainly *Ojapali* and *Devadasi*". Why is the second of these a trap, and what general rule does it teach?

**Model answer:** It is a trap because the same word names two entirely different things in this topic. In the Bharatanatyam section, *devadasi* names the **temple dancers of Tamil Nadu** whose solo *Sadir* performance is the recorded origin of that form and whose system's decline nearly caused the form to vanish. In the Sattriya section, *Devadasi* names **one of the dance traditions prevalent in Assam** that was absorbed into the fifteenth-century Vaishnava form. A candidate who carries the Tamil referent north will produce a confident and completely false sentence - that Sattriya derives from the devadasi system of the south - and will also import the anti-nautch and revival narrative into a tradition where the record places none of it. The general rule is: **a shared term does not imply a shared referent; check the region before resolving the word.** The same discipline is needed at several other points in this session - *Jaipur*, which names both a Kathak lineage and a music lineage; *Thaat* and *Tarana*, which name both Kathak recital items and music-topic categories; and *Ras Leela*, which is both Kathak's recorded origin material and Manipuri's recurring theme.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that an unusual overlap must be an error in the source. The record is internally consistent: it names an Assamese tradition and a Tamil one, and both are called by a term that had wide currency. The error would be to resolve the ambiguity by assuming that the more familiar meaning must be the intended one.

## Lesson 18 - Chhau and Gaudiya Nritya: the frontier where classification is actually decided

Progress: 18/23 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the mask dance and its three styles, the martial and narrative content, the international inscription, the ninth-form question, and a claimed Bengal form that holds no recognition

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the Chhau box with the etymology, the mask and martial-movement record, the natural-theme narrations, the three named styles with their states and the mask exception, the inscription record, the eight-versus-nine statement, and the Gaudiya Nritya box in full
CA search: "Not applicable - the session's single current record, which bears on recognition, is stated in Lesson 8"
CA found: None found; none claimed
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: two forms at the classification boundary, from opposite directions

```text
                      THE BOUNDARY OF "CLASSICAL"
   ===============================================================
                      |                          |
              C H H A U                  G A U D I Y A   N R I T Y A
                      |                          |
   arrives from BELOW the line:       arrives from ABOVE the line:
   a mask dance with vigorous         claims Natya Shastra roots,
   martial movements                  a named historical reviver,
                      |               temple sculptural evidence,
   HOLDS:                             and a named modern reconstruction
   - Ministry of Culture's list                   |
     of NINE classical forms          HOLDS:
   - UNESCO inscription, 2010         - nothing formal
                      |               - "has NOT YET been recognised by the
   DOES NOT HOLD:                       Sangeet Natak Akademi as a
   - a place among the national         classical dance form"
     academy's EIGHT
   ===============================================================
   WHAT THE PAIR PROVES: the line is drawn by DECISIONS, not by the
   properties of the dance. Evidence, antiquity, treatise mention and
   reconstruction can all be present without recognition; martial folk
   origin and mask performance do not prevent it.

   CHHAU: THREE STYLES
   +----------------------+--------------------+------------------------+
   | SARAIKELA CHHAU      | MAYURBHANJ CHHAU   | PURULIA CHHAU          |
   | JHARKHAND            | ODISHA             | WEST BENGAL            |
   |                      | artists do NOT     |                        |
   |                      | wear masks         |                        |
   +----------------------+--------------------+------------------------+
```

### 1. Chhau: name, technique and content

The word '***Chhau***' originated from the **Hindi word '*Chhaya*', meaning shadow**. It is a form of **mask dance** that uses **vigorous martial movements** to narrate **mythological stories**. Some narrations also use **natural themes** such as ***Sarpa Nritya*** (serpent dance) or ***Mayura Nritya*** (peacock dance).

Four elements, each of which is a separate testable item: the **etymology from shadow**, the **mask**, the **vigorous martial movement** and the **two named natural-theme narrations**.

⚠️ The two natural themes connect outwards to the impersonation-led folk forms of Lesson 20 - the peacock dances of Tamil Nadu and Madhya Pradesh, the snake dance among the Tamil animal-impersonation set, the tiger dance of Karnataka and the bison dance of Bastar. Animal impersonation is a widespread idiom across the Indian dance field, and Chhau carries it inside a mask tradition.

### 2. The three styles, and the exception that is always tested

**There are three main styles of Chhau dance:**

| Style | State | Note |
|---|---|---|
| **Saraikela Chhau** | **Jharkhand** | - |
| **Mayurbhanj Chhau** | **Odisha** | **artists do not wear masks** |
| **Purulia Chhau** | **West Bengal** | - |

❌ **The exception is the whole point of the table.** Chhau is introduced as a **mask dance**, and one of its three styles is recorded as not using masks. A candidate who has learned "Chhau is a mask dance" and stops there will fail an item built on **Mayurbhanj**. The correct formulation is: "Chhau is a mask dance in which the Mayurbhanj style is performed without masks."

⚠️ Note also that the three styles belong to **three different states**, which makes Chhau the clearest single case against the one-state-one-form assumption in the folk field - just as Kerala's two classical forms are the clearest case against it in the classical field.

### 3. The inscription

**In 2010, UNESCO inscribed Chhau in the Representative List of Intangible Cultural Heritage of Humanity.**

Set it beside the other two dance-related Indian records taught in this session - **Kalbelia folk songs and dances (2010)** and **Garba of Gujarat (2023)** - and the pattern is informative: the international safeguarding instrument has reached a **mask-and-martial tradition**, a **community-specific folk tradition** and a **mass participatory festival dance**, and none of the three is among the national academy's eight. The instrument is not tracking the classical canon; it is tracking living practice.

### 4. The ninth-form question

**Till date, the Sangeet Natak Akademi has recognised eight classical dance forms, whereas the Ministry of Culture has recognised nine classical dance forms, including Chhau.**

This is the exact sentence to reproduce. Three things make it valuable beyond recall.

1. **It names both bodies and both counts**, so it cannot be reduced to a single number.
2. **It identifies the differing item precisely.** Chhau is the difference between the two lists.
3. **It is an explicit instance of institutional disagreement.** Two organs of the same state answer the same question differently, which is the strongest available evidence that "classical" is a decision rather than a description. Any answer on the classification of Indian dance should use it.

### 5. Gaudiya Nritya: everything except the decision

***Gaudiya Nritya*** is a **Bengali classical dance** whose name traces its origin to **Gaur, the capital city of Bengal once upon a time**. It is **believed** that this dance has its **roots in the *Natya Shastra***. During the **Bhakti movement**, it **is said** that **Chaitanya Mahaprabhu revived** Gaudiya Nritya. The **sculptures of this dance form have also been noted on the walls of many temples of Bengal**. Later, the dance form was **reconstructed by Mahua Mukherjee**. **However, this dance has not yet been recognised by the Sangeet Natak Akademi as a classical dance form.**

⚠️ **Preserve the hedged verbs.** The record says *believed*, *is said* and *noted*. Reproducing those qualifications is not weakness; it signals that you can distinguish a claim from an established fact, which is precisely what the examiner is testing when a question invites you to assess evidence.

Now compare the evidence base with the eight recognised forms:

| Evidence type | Gaudiya Nritya | Present in the recognised eight? |
|---|---|---|
| Claimed *Natya Shastra* roots | yes (believed) | Odissi's name is derived from a *Natya Shastra* term; Sattriya finds mention in it |
| A named historical reviver | yes (said to be Chaitanya Mahaprabhu) | Kathakali, Mohiniyattam, Manipuri, Kuchipudi, Bharatanatyam all have named revivers |
| Temple sculptural evidence | yes (noted on Bengal temple walls) | Odissi has Udayagiri-Khandagiri; dancers appear at four named temple sites |
| A named modern reconstruction | yes (Mahua Mukherjee) | every recognised form has a twentieth-century reconstruction or revival story |
| **Academy recognition** | **no** | **yes, by definition** |

The table is the argument. On **every evidential axis** that the recognised forms rely on, this form has a comparable claim; the only row on which it differs is the one that has nothing to do with evidence. That is what it means to say that classification is an institutional act.

### 6. How to use the pair in an answer

The two cases work as a matched pair and should be deployed together.

- **Chhau shows that the recognition line is not fixed even within the government.** One list contains it, another does not, and an international instrument has inscribed it. Three bodies, three different answers about the same dance.
- **Gaudiya Nritya shows that the line is not determined by evidence.** Treatise claim, historical reviver, sculptural record and modern reconstruction are all present, and the form sits outside the canon.

Together they support a single thesis sentence: **"Classical" in Indian dance names the outcome of institutional decisions taken in the twentieth century and after, not a property that inheres in a form - which is why one dance is classical on one official list and not on another, and why a form with the standard evidential profile of a classical tradition can hold no classical status at all.**

### Examination use

For Prelims: the *Chhaya* etymology; mask dance with vigorous martial movements; *Sarpa Nritya* and *Mayura Nritya*; the three styles with their states; the Mayurbhanj no-mask exception; the 2010 inscription; the eight-versus-nine sentence with Chhau as the difference; and for Gaudiya Nritya - Gaur, the claimed *Natya Shastra* roots, Chaitanya Mahaprabhu, the Bengal temple sculptures, Mahua Mukherjee and the non-recognition.

For Mains, this lesson is where the recognition thesis becomes demonstrable rather than assertable. Most candidates can state that classical status is a modern institutional act; very few can evidence it with an intra-governmental disagreement and a fully qualified non-recognised case. That evidence converts a familiar observation into an argument.

### Traps introduced here

- Describing Chhau as a mask dance without the Mayurbhanj exception.
- Assigning the three styles to the wrong states.
- Treating the 2010 inscription as conferring classical status.
- Saying Chhau is "not a classical dance"; it is in the ministry's nine and not in the academy's eight.
- Dropping the hedged verbs from the Gaudiya Nritya record.
- Treating Gaudiya Nritya as recognised because it is described as a Bengali classical dance; the description is of its character, and the record expressly states that recognition has not been given.

### Revision notes

1. ***Chhau*** - from the Hindi ***Chhaya***, meaning **shadow**; **West Bengal, Odisha and Jharkhand**.
2. A **mask dance** using **vigorous martial movements** to narrate **mythological stories**; natural themes include ***Sarpa Nritya*** (serpent) and ***Mayura Nritya*** (peacock).
3. Three styles: **Saraikela (Jharkhand), Mayurbhanj (Odisha), Purulia (West Bengal)**. **Mayurbhanj artists do not wear masks.**
4. **UNESCO inscribed Chhau in the Representative List in 2010.**
5. **Academy: eight. Ministry of Culture: nine, including Chhau.** Chhau is the difference between the two lists.
6. ***Gaudiya Nritya*** - a **Bengali classical dance**; name from **Gaur**, once the capital of Bengal.
7. **Believed** to have roots in the ***Natya Shastra***; **said** to have been revived by **Chaitanya Mahaprabhu** during the Bhakti movement; sculptures **noted** on the walls of many Bengal temples.
8. **Reconstructed by Mahua Mukherjee**; **not yet recognised** by the academy as a classical dance form.
9. Thesis: recognition is an **institutional decision**, not a property of the dance.
10. Evidence: **Chhau** shows official bodies disagreeing; **Gaudiya Nritya** shows the standard evidential profile without recognition.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"The boundary between classical and folk dance in India is administrative rather than aesthetic." Critically examine. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The proposition is largely sustainable, though the categories are not empty.

The administrative character is directly evidenced. The Sangeet Natak Akademi recognises eight classical dance forms; the Ministry of Culture recognises nine, the difference being Chhau. Two organs of the same government give different answers about the same dance, which is impossible if the boundary tracks a property of the dance itself. Gaudiya Nritya completes the demonstration from the other side: it is described as a Bengali classical dance, is believed to have roots in the *Natya Shastra*, is said to have been revived by Chaitanya Mahaprabhu, has sculptural evidence on Bengal temple walls and has been reconstructed by a named modern figure - and has not been recognised. Every evidential axis on which the recognised eight rest is present; only the decision is absent.

A third line of evidence comes from the international safeguarding instrument, whose Indian dance-related inscriptions are Chhau in 2010, Kalbelia folk songs and dances in 2010 and Garba of Gujarat in 2023 - none of them among the academy's eight. A different body asking a different question produces an entirely different set.

The qualification matters. Codification is real: the recognised forms carry named recital orders, treatise-derived vocabularies, strict transmission rules and, in one case, strictly laid-down rules for hand gesture and footwork. That is a genuine difference in kind of organisation.

Verdict: the difference in organisation is real; the boundary drawn around it is administrative, revisable and, as Chhau shows, contested within government.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the intra-governmental disagreement named precisely with both bodies and both counts 4 + the non-recognised case with at least three evidential axes 4 + the international instrument as a third differently-constituted list with its three dated records 3 + the codification qualification stated as a real difference in organisation 3 + a verdict that separates organisation from boundary 1.**

### Concept check

**Question:** Chhau is a mask dance, and one of its three styles is performed without masks. Beyond the factual correction, what does this tell you about how folk-performance categories should be handled?

**Model answer:** It tells you that in this field a **category name describes a tendency, not a definition**. Chhau is introduced as a mask dance because masks characterise the tradition as a whole, and yet Mayurbhanj Chhau - one of the three main styles, from Odisha, alongside Saraikela in Jharkhand and Purulia in West Bengal - is recorded as performed without them. The label is accurate at the level of the tradition and false at the level of one of its major styles. The handling rule that follows applies everywhere in the living field: **state the general characterisation, then state the internal variation, and never treat the label as a test that every instance must pass.** The same discipline is needed for several other statements in this session - that a certain Bihar form expresses women's experience yet is practised solely by males playing female roles; that a form described as generally performed by male monks nonetheless has a recorded female costume; that a form dominated by grace is recorded as borrowing vigour from a neighbouring form. In each case the interesting content is in the exception, and an answer that reports only the label has thrown away the part that carries marks.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that the label is therefore wrong or useless. "Mask dance" remains the correct general characterisation of Chhau and is exactly what an examiner expects to see. The error is not in using the label but in using it exclusively, so that the one style that departs from it becomes invisible.

## Lesson 19 - Reading a folk dance by function - Part A: ritual embodiment, martial occasion, agrarian calendar

Progress: 19/23 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: why the state label is the weakest way to learn this field, the general definition of folk dance, and the three functional families of ritual embodiment and deity worship, martial and hunting performance, and the agrarian and seasonal calendar

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the Folk Dances of India introduction and the complete state-wise table, read in full, with every entry's community, occasion, property, formation, theme and instrument record
CA search: "Not applicable - living-tradition field lesson; the session's single current record is stated in Lesson 8"
CA found: None found; none claimed
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: four questions that turn a list into knowledge

```text
   A FOLK DANCE IS NAMED. ASK IN THIS ORDER:

   1. WHAT OCCASION?        sowing | harvest | monsoon | post-harvest |
                            a named festival | a deity's worship | a wedding |
                            a hunt | a battle memory | a fair
   2. WHO PERFORMS?         a named community or tribe | women only |
                            men only | both | males playing female roles |
                            a priestly or specialist lineage
   3. WHAT IS HELD OR WORN? sticks | bamboo | pots | baskets | cymbals tied
                            to the body | masks | animal costume | shields |
                            spears | nothing (clapping)
   4. WHAT SHAPE?           circle | rows | line | mock-battle formation |
                            seated | solo | 4-6 | 8-40

   THE STATE IS THE FIFTH QUESTION, NOT THE FIRST.
   A state label tells you where to find the dance. The four questions above
   tell you what the dance IS - and they are what an examiner can build a
   question from.
```

### 1. What folk dance is recorded to be

**India's many legends and local traditions have created diverse folk dances, which are spontaneous, simple, and performed by common people without formal training. Folk dances are simple and beautiful but stay limited to certain communities and regions.**

⚠️ **Handle this definition carefully, because it contains an implicit evaluation.** Two of its terms - *simple*, and *without formal training* - are comparative, and the comparison is with the codified classical forms. Used uncritically, they generate exactly the hierarchy this session refuses.

Three corrections are available from within the record itself, and using them is what separates a good answer from an average one.

1. **"Without formal training" is not "without transmission".** The record names the carrier community for dozens of these forms - the Nalike for one Tulu ritual art, the Chakyar for a Kerala solo form, the Kamar tribe for a seated Rajasthani form, the Bedia for a Bundelkhand peacock dance, the Watal for a Kashmiri form. A community that reproduces a repertoire across generations is running a transmission system; it is simply not running an examinable curriculum.
2. **"Simple" is contradicted by the record's own descriptions.** A dance performed with the feet on the edges of a brass plate while balancing a pot of water is a classical solo item, but so are the seated Rajasthani form in which cymbals are tied to different parts of the performer's body, the Tripura form in which four to six women balance earthen pitchers while moving only the lower half of the body, and the Mizoram form in which dancers step between long bamboo poles tapped in rhythm. None of that is simple.
3. **"Limited to certain communities and regions" is a statement about reach, not about worth** - and it is precisely the feature that an international safeguarding instrument exists to address, which is why three Indian dance-related inscriptions are folk or mask traditions rather than classical forms.

✅ **The formulation to use in an answer:** folk dance is distinguished from the recognised classical forms by its **occasion-bound, community-carried and locally transmitted character**, not by an absence of skill or a lower place on a ladder. ❌ Never write that folk dance is primitive, crude or undeveloped.

### 2. Family one: ritual embodiment and deity worship

In this family the performance is **an act of worship or an encounter with the divine**, not a representation of one. The strongest instances record the performer as **embodying** a deity or spirit.

| Dance | Region | The ritual content |
|---|---|---|
| **Theyyam** | Kerala and Karnataka | a **ritual dance** where performers in **elaborate costumes embody deities and spirits**, symbolising devotion and local mythology |
| **Buta Kola** | Tulu Nadu - southern Karnataka and northern Kerala | **highly stylised**, held in honour of the **local deities worshipped by the Tulu people**; involves music, dance, recital and elaborate costumes |
| **Nagaradhane** | Tulu Nadu | part of **cobra worship**; two rituals - ***Aashleshabali*** and the longer, colourful ***Nagamandala***, which depicts the **union of male and female snakes** |
| **Aati Kalenja** | Tulu Nadu | ancient Tulu folk art performed by the **Nalike community** in **Aati (July-August)**; celebrates **Kalenja, a nature spirit** who brings prosperity, wards off evil and blesses people; Kalenja wears **coconut and palm-leaf attire** and carries a **palm-leaf and bamboo umbrella** |
| **Padayani** | Kerala | a **martial dance** from **southern Kerala's Bhagavati temples**, performed to worship **Goddess Kali**; dancers wear **large masks (*kolams*)** and enact divine and semi-divine stories, featuring **Bhairavi, Kalan, Yakshi and Pakshi** |
| **Gambhira** | West Bengal | performed in **Malda district** during **Chaitra Sankranti**; **masks made out of neem and fig trees**; songs originate from Hindu mythology, especially **shakti and shaiva** worship |
| **Ghanta Mrudangam** | Odisha | tribal folk dance of the **Vaishnava community**, mainly in **Ganjam district**; related to the worship of **Radha, Krishna and Lord Jagannath** |
| **Danda Jatra** (Danda Naata) | Odisha | **one of the oldest folk arts of India**; a unique blend of **dance, drama and music**; narrates stories and lore about **Shiva**, with the theme generally **social harmony and brotherhood** |
| **Chakyar Koothu** | Kerala | a **solo performance by the Chakyar community**; the performer **dresses as a snake**, combines **prose and poetry in Malayalam**, and wears **colourful headgear, a large black moustache and red body spots** |
| **Garadi** | Puducherry | performed to celebrate the **victory of Lord Rama over Ravana**; dancers distinguished as ***Vanaras*** (monkeys) celebrate the victory, wearing **10 *Anjalis* (iron rings) on each of their legs** |
| **Singhi Chham** | Sikkim | dancers dressed in **furry lion costumes** symbolising the **snow lion**, paying tribute to **Khang-Chen Dzong Pa (Kanchenjunga Peak)** |
| **Dalkhai** | Odisha | usually performed during **Dussehra**; themed on stories from the **Ramayana, Mahabharata and tales of Lord Krishna**; at the **beginning and end of every stanza, a word signifying girlfriend is used** |
| **Raut Nacha** | Chhattisgarh | performed by the **Yadav community**, especially during the festival of **Diwali** |
| **Alkap** | Jharkhand and West Bengal | performed by groups with singers, mixing **folklore, myths and comic sketches**, mainly during **Shiva's Gajan festival** |
| **Karma Naach** | eastern India, especially the **Chota Nagpur Plateau** | performed during the tribal festival of ***Karma*** by many tribes; dancers form a **circle** and dance with their **arms around each other's waist** |
| **Tarangamel** | Goa | celebrates the **youthfulness** of the region; performed during **Dussehra and Holi**; **rainbow-like costumes with multi-coloured flags and streamers** |
| **Charba** | Himachal Pradesh | performed during the **Dussehra** festivities |
| **Rathwa ni Gher** | Gujarat | tribal dance of the **Rathwa community**, performed during **Holi (Kavant Festival)**, with **elaborate makeup, whirling steps and traditional music** |
| **Hojagiri** | Tripura | performed during **Lakshmi Puja**; see Lesson 20 for its technique |
| **Kaikottikali** | Kerala | performed by **both men and women at the time of Onam** to celebrate the **rich harvest**; ***Airukali* and *Tattamakali*** are similar forms |
| **Hulivesha** | Karnataka | male dancers painted like tigers honour **Goddess Durga, whose favourite animal is the tiger**; generally during **Navaratri** - see Lesson 20 for the impersonation technique |
| **Pata Kunitha** | Karnataka | a **religious** Mysore folk dance performed by men using **long bamboo poles (*pata*) decorated with ribbons**; its vibrant display **attracts all religions**; ***Puja Kunitha*** is a popular variant near **Bengaluru and Mandya** |

Three analytical observations to carry into an answer.

- **Embodiment is the strongest claim in the family and it is recorded explicitly** for the Kerala and Tulu forms: performers **embody deities and spirits**. That is categorically different from representing a deity in a narrative, and it is the reason these forms are inseparable from their ritual calendars.
- **Festival occasion is the most common organising principle.** Navaratri, Dussehra, Holi, Diwali, Onam, Chaitra Sankranti, Lakshmi Puja, the Gajan festival, the Karma festival and the Tulu month of Aati each anchor at least one named dance. If you are asked to write about dance and the festival calendar, this table is the answer.
- **One entry names social harmony as its theme.** The Odisha *Danda Jatra*, described as one of the oldest folk arts of India and a blend of dance, drama and music, has **social harmony and brotherhood** as its general theme, and the Karnataka *Pata Kunitha* is recorded as **attracting all religions**. Both are directly usable in an answer about the social functions of performance.

### 3. Family two: martial, war and hunting performance

Here the content is **combat or the hunt**, enacted rather than fought.

| Dance | Region | The martial content |
|---|---|---|
| **Thang-Ta** | Manipur | ***Thang*** means **sword** and ***Ta*** means **spear**; a unique display of **skill, creativity and agility** in which performers enact a **mock fight sequence - leaping to attack and defend** |
| **Paika** | Jharkhand and southern Odisha | a **martial folk dance**; performed with **wooden spears and shields**; showcases battle skills and agility in **infantry-like formations**; the word ***Paika*** means **battle** |
| **Rangma** | Nagaland | the **war dance**; dancers in **colourful costumes, jewellery and colourful headgears** enact **mock war formations and traditions** |
| **Kolkali-Parichakali** | Kerala and Lakshadweep | a popular **martial dance** of southern Kerala and Lakshadweep; dancers use **wooden sticks and shields** to perform **fight sequences, starting slow and building to a fast, energetic climax** |
| **Padayani** | Kerala | recorded as a **martial dance**, though its occasion is temple worship of Goddess Kali - see family one |
| **Perini** | Telangana | performed in honour of **Lord Shiva**; depicted in the sculptures near the **Garbha Gudi (sanctum sanctorum) of the Ramappa Temple at Warangal**; called the **'Dance of Warriors'**, performed by warriors; reached its pinnacle during the rule of the **Kakatiya** dynasty |
| **Mosak Sumani** | Tripura | related to the **hunting of wild animals**; the **hunting sequences** are shown through **gestures and postures in rhythmic steps** |
| **Garadi** | Puducherry | enacts the **victory of Rama over Ravana** with dancers as *Vanaras* - a narrative battle rather than a combat drill |
| **Chhau** | West Bengal, Odisha, Jharkhand | a mask dance using **vigorous martial movements** - taught in Lesson 18 |

Four points that make this family analytically valuable.

1. **Martial performance sits at the boundary of dance and martial art**, and the topic records the connection twice: Kathakali is described as closely related to **ancient martial art performance**, and this family contains forms that are explicitly combat-derived. ⚠️ Martial arts as a discipline are owned by the theatre, martial arts and sports topic; here the **danced enactment** is owned.
2. **Perini is the only folk form in this session with a named temple sculptural attestation.** Its depiction near the sanctum of a named Kakatiya temple, together with a named dynasty and a described peak, makes it the single strongest folk entry for any question connecting dance, sculpture and dynastic patronage.
3. **Property distinguishes the martial forms from one another**: sword and spear named in the Manipur form's very name; wooden spears and shields in Odisha; wooden sticks and shields in Kerala and Lakshadweep; costume, jewellery and headgear in Nagaland.
4. **Formation is recorded as military in two cases** - infantry-like formations in one, mock war formations in another - which is a rare instance of a folk dance's spatial organisation being described in functional terms.

### 4. Family three: the agrarian and seasonal calendar

Here the occasion is a point in the **agricultural year**, and the dance is part of how that point is marked.

| Dance | Region | Calendar position and content |
|---|---|---|
| **Garia** | Tripura | the **Garia festival marks the commencement of the sowing season** for the **Tripuri tribal community**; the dance accompanies **prayers and pujas for a bountiful harvest** and is **held in April** |
| **Lebang Boomani** | Tripura | a **harvest dance** of the **Tripuri community**, performed **after the Garia festival during the monsoon**; dancers use **bamboo clappers to mimic catching colourful insects called *lebang***, believed to **foretell the success of the harvest** |
| **Meladom** | Tripura | a **post-harvest** dance believed to have **originated from the process of weaving**; at this time **cotton is collected to start weaving**; the **Keipeng community** participates |
| **Phag** (Faag) | Himachal Pradesh | a **farmer's folk dance** performed in **February-March during harvest**; women wear **ghagra-choli with dupatta**, men wear **colourful pagdis (turbans)** |
| **Jhumair** | West Bengal, Jharkhand and Odisha | a **tribal harvest dance** with two types - ***Janani* (women)** and ***Mardana* (men)**; popular at **fairs**; **resembles dances in Bhimbetka rock art** |
| **Jhoomar** | Punjab | performed by the **tribal Sikhs** in Punjab and adjoining areas during the **harvest season**; performed **in a circle**; the **movement of the arms to the tune of the drums** is its main forte; costumes the same as in Bhangra; **carried to India by the traders from Baluchistan** |
| **Jawara** | Madhya Pradesh | the **harvest dance** popular in the **Bundelkhand** region; includes **balancing a basket full of jawar on the head**; accompanied by **heavy instrumental music** |
| **Lambadi** | Andhra Pradesh | a **Banjara** folk dance depicting **farmers' daily life**, with women using **hand gestures to show sowing and planting** |
| **Kaikottikali** | Kerala | performed at **Onam** to celebrate the **rich harvest** |
| **Bagurumba** | Assam | a **nature-themed** folk dance of Assam's **Bodo tribe**, performed with instruments like ***sifung, kham* and *serja*** |
| **Bihu** | Assam | Assam's popular **group dance** by men and women, marked by **colourful dress, lively formations, quick hand moves and brisk steps** |

Three observations.

- **The Tripura set is a complete agricultural cycle in three dances.** Sowing in April, the monsoon between, and the post-harvest turn to weaving - three named dances, three named points in one community's year, one after another. If a question asks how performance is embedded in the agricultural calendar, this is the evidence, and it is far stronger than a general statement.
- **Two entries record a claimed external or prehistoric connection.** One Punjab harvest dance is recorded as **carried to India by traders from Baluchistan**, and one eastern harvest dance is recorded as **resembling dances in Bhimbetka rock art**. ⚠️ Treat both as recorded claims and reproduce them with their hedging - the second in particular is a **resemblance**, not a descent.
- **The agrarian family is where the gendered division of the field is clearest**, and it is recorded rather than assumed: a harvest dance with separate women's and men's types under separate names, a Banjara dance in which women's hand gestures depict sowing and planting, and a Himachal harvest dance with distinct recorded dress for each.

### Examination use

For Prelims, every row in the three tables is a potential pair-matching item, and the specific details are what decide them: the two Tulu cobra-worship rituals by name, the *kolams* of the Kerala Kali temple dance, the neem and fig masks of the Malda form, the ten iron rings on each leg in Puducherry, the *Vanaras*, the snow lion and Kanchenjunga, the sword-and-spear etymology, the meaning of *Paika*, the Ramappa Temple and the Kakatiyas, the April sowing festival and the monsoon insect-catching dance, and the Baluchistan and Bhimbetka claims.

For Mains, this lesson supplies the structure that makes a folk-dance answer analytical: **occasion, community, property and formation** - four evidenced axes, each of which can be illustrated from several states, in place of a list. It also supplies the anti-hierarchy argument, which any question that pairs folk with classical dance is implicitly testing.

### Traps introduced here

- Reproducing "simple" and "without formal training" as a verdict rather than as a description of transmission mode.
- Describing folk dance as primitive or as a stage preceding classical dance.
- Confusing the two Tulu cobra-worship rituals, or attributing them to the wrong region.
- Treating Padayani as purely martial or purely ritual; it is recorded as both.
- Reading the Bhimbetka resemblance as evidence of descent.
- Assigning Perini to a state other than Telangana, or forgetting its temple and dynasty.
- Losing the distinction between the Tripura sowing festival and the Tripura harvest dance that follows it.

### Revision notes

1. Folk dances are recorded as **spontaneous, simple, performed by common people without formal training**, and **limited to certain communities and regions** - a description of **transmission and reach**, not a ranking.
2. **Embodiment forms**: **Theyyam** (Kerala and Karnataka) - performers **embody deities and spirits**; **Buta Kola** (Tulu Nadu) - local Tulu deities; **Nagaradhane** (Tulu Nadu) - cobra worship, ***Aashleshabali*** and ***Nagamandala***; **Aati Kalenja** (Tulu Nadu) - **Nalike** community, month of **Aati**, the nature spirit **Kalenja**.
3. **Padayani** (Kerala) - martial dance at **Bhagavati temples**, worship of **Kali**, masks called ***kolams***, characters **Bhairavi, Kalan, Yakshi, Pakshi**.
4. **Gambhira** (WB) - **Malda**, **Chaitra Sankranti**, masks of **neem and fig**, **shakti and shaiva** songs.
5. **Ghanta Mrudangam** (Odisha) - **Ganjam**, Vaishnava community, **Radha, Krishna, Jagannath**. **Danda Jatra** - one of the **oldest folk arts**, dance-drama-music, **Shiva** stories, theme **social harmony and brotherhood**. **Dalkhai** - **Dussehra**, a word signifying girlfriend at the **start and end of every stanza**.
6. **Chakyar Koothu** (Kerala) - **Chakyar** community, solo, **dresses as a snake**, prose and poetry in **Malayalam**, black moustache and **red body spots**.
7. **Garadi** (Puducherry) - **Rama over Ravana**, dancers as ***Vanaras***, **10 *Anjalis*** on each leg. **Singhi Chham** (Sikkim) - **snow lion**, tribute to **Kanchenjunga**.
8. Festival anchors: **Navaratri** (Hulivesha), **Dussehra** (Charba, Tarangamel, Dalkhai), **Holi** (Tarangamel, Rathwa ni Gher/Kavant), **Diwali** (Raut Nacha - **Yadav**), **Onam** (Kaikottikali, with *Airukali* and *Tattamakali*), **Gajan** (Alkap), **Karma** (Karma Naach), **Lakshmi Puja** (Hojagiri).
9. **Pata Kunitha** (Karnataka) - religious, **bamboo poles (*pata*) with ribbons**, **attracts all religions**; variant ***Puja Kunitha*** near **Bengaluru and Mandya**.
10. **Martial**: **Thang-Ta** (sword + spear, Manipur); **Paika** (= **battle**; wooden spears and shields; **infantry-like formations**); **Rangma** (Nagaland **war dance**, mock war formations); **Kolkali-Parichakali** (Kerala and **Lakshadweep**, sticks and shields, slow to fast climax); **Perini** (Telangana, **Shiva**, **Ramappa Temple, Warangal**, **'Dance of Warriors'**, **Kakatiya** peak); **Mosak Sumani** (Tripura, **hunting**).
11. **Agrarian cycle, Tripura**: **Garia** (commencement of **sowing**, **April**) -> **Lebang Boomani** (harvest dance in the **monsoon**, **bamboo clappers**, insects called ***lebang*** foretelling harvest success) -> **Meladom** (**post-harvest**, from **weaving**, **Keipeng** community).
12. Other agrarian: **Phag** (HP, **Feb-March harvest**); **Jhumair** (**Janani**/**Mardana**, resembles **Bhimbetka** rock-art dances); **Jhoomar** (Punjab harvest, circle, **carried by traders from Baluchistan**); **Jawara** (Bundelkhand, **basket of jawar** on the head); **Lambadi** (**Banjara**, sowing and planting gestures); **Bagurumba** (**Bodo**, ***sifung, kham, serja***); **Bihu** (Assam group dance).

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Examine the proposition that ritual dance in India is better described as embodiment than as representation. (20 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The proposition holds for one substantial family of Indian dance, and the record uses the stronger word deliberately.

**The evidence for embodiment.** In one Kerala and Karnataka ritual form, performers in elaborate costumes are recorded as **embodying deities and spirits**. In Tulu Nadu, a highly stylised form is held in honour of local deities; a second is part of cobra worship with two named rituals, one of them depicting the union of male and female snakes; a third, performed by a named community in a named month, celebrates a nature spirit who brings prosperity and wards off evil, its performer wearing coconut and palm-leaf attire and carrying a palm-leaf umbrella. In these the performance is the encounter, not a depiction of one.

**The contrast within the same field.** Other ritual forms clearly represent: a Puducherry dance enacts the victory of Rama over Ravana with dancers as *Vanaras* wearing ten iron rings on each leg; an Odisha form narrates lore about Shiva with social harmony as its theme; a Kerala temple dance enacts divine and semi-divine stories with named masked characters.

**The test.** Ask what the occasion requires. Where the deity must be present, the performer is the deity; where a story must be told, the performer stands for someone.

Verdict: embodiment and representation are two functions available to ritual dance, and the family cannot be described by either alone.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = the proposition narrowed to a family rather than to the whole field 2 + four embodiment cases with their communities, occasions and named rituals 9 + three representational cases with their specific detail 6 + a test that decides between the two 2 + a verdict admitting both functions 1.**

### Concept check

**Question:** Three Tripura dances - one in April, one in the monsoon and one after the harvest - are recorded in this topic. Why is that sequence worth more in an answer than any single one of them?

**Model answer:** Because together they demonstrate something that no single dance can: that **performance is structurally embedded in the productive year of a community**, rather than being an occasional ornament to it. The *Garia* festival marks the **commencement of the sowing season** for the Tripuri tribal community, is held in **April**, and its dance accompanies prayers and pujas for a bountiful harvest - so the cycle opens with a performance addressed to an outcome that is not yet determined. *Lebang Boomani* follows **after the Garia festival, during the monsoon**, and its dancers use bamboo clappers to mimic catching insects called *lebang*, which are **believed to foretell the success of the harvest** - so the middle of the cycle is marked by a performance that reads a sign. *Meladom* is **post-harvest** and is believed to have originated from the **process of weaving**, cotton being collected at that time, with the **Keipeng community** participating - so the cycle closes by turning to the next kind of work. Petition, divination, and the turn to a new task: three functions, three moments, three named dances. An answer that cites one of them has a fact; an answer that cites the sequence has an argument about how performance and subsistence are related, and can then generalise it to the Himachal harvest dance, the Bundelkhand basket dance and the Banjara sowing gestures.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading these as seasonal celebrations in a general sense. The record is more specific: one **marks the commencement** of sowing, one is **believed to foretell** the harvest's success, and one **originates from** a subsequent craft process. Precision about function is what distinguishes the answer, and generalising to "they celebrate the seasons" discards exactly what was worth having.

## Lesson 20 - Reading a folk dance by function - Part B: property, formation, impersonation and community carriers

### Visual: what the dancer holds, how the dancers stand, and who the dancer becomes

```text
   WHAT IS HELD OR WORN                       HOW THE DANCERS STAND
   ----------------------------------         --------------------------------
   STICKS      Kolattam (AP, 8-40)            CIRCLE   Garba (around the
               Dandiya Raas (Guj)                       lamp-lit Garbha deep)
               Kolkali-Parichakali (+shields)           Kummi (TN, clapping)
   BAMBOO      Cheraw (Mizoram, tapped)                 Jhoomar (Punjab)
               Pata Kunitha (Karnataka, poles)          Jhora (Uttarakhand)
               Lebang Boomani (clappers)                Karma Naach (arms round
   POTS        Matki (MP, several on head)               each other's waist)
               Hojagiri (Tripura, pitchers)             Fugdi (circles or rows)
               Garba (around the pot)          ROWS     Fugdi (Konkan)
   BASKET      Jawara (MP, jawar on head)      SEATED   Tera Tali (Rajasthan)
   CYMBALS     Tera Tali (tied to the body)    SOLO     Matki, Chakyar Koothu
   FLOOR-      Tippani (Guj, two sticks        4-6      Hojagiri (Tripura)
   BEATERS     joined by a block)              8-40     Kolattam (AP)
   NOTHING     Kummi (rhythmic clapping        MOCK-    Rangma (war formations)
               instead of instruments)         BATTLE   Paika (infantry-like)

                          WHO THE DANCER BECOMES
   ANIMAL: tiger (Hulivesha, KA) | bison (Gaur Muria, CG) | peacock
   (Mayilattam, TN-Kerala; Rai Nritya, MP) | snow lion (Singhi Chham, SK)
   | serpent (Kalbelia, RJ; Chakyar Koothu, KL) | bull, bear, demon and
   snake (the Tamil Attam set) | frog (Manduka Shabdam - a Kuchipudi solo)
   MASK / OBJECT: Gambhira (neem and fig) | Padayani (kolams) | Butta
   Bommalu (basket-toy masks) | Chhau (mask dance, Mayurbhanj excepted)
   HEADGEAR: Dumhal (tall conical hat) | Mayilattam (beaks and feathers)
```

### 1. Family four: property-led forms

In this family the **object is the technique**. Remove the stick, the pot or the bamboo and there is no dance left, which is why the property is always the discriminating detail in an identification item.

| Dance | Region | The object and what it does |
|---|---|---|
| **Kolattam** (Kolannalu) | Andhra Pradesh | a **stick dance** performed at **village festivals** by **groups of 8-40**, with the **sticks creating the main rhythm** |
| **Dandiya Raas** | Gujarat | the most popular **Raas**, performed during **Navratri**, where **men and women dance in circles with sticks**; **Raas** is linked to **Krishna and Radha's lilas** and is performed with Garba in western India; **Garba is another form, known as Raas Garba** |
| **Cheraw** | Mizoram | performed using **bamboo sticks**; **likely to have a foreign origin**; **men tap long pairs of bamboo in rhythmic beats, and girls dance to the beats of the bamboo** |
| **Pata Kunitha** | Karnataka | men use **long bamboo poles (*pata*) decorated with ribbons** |
| **Tippani** | Gujarat | originating from the **Saurashtra** region; performed **exclusively by women**, who **beat the floors with *tippani*** - **two long wooden sticks joined by a square wooden or iron block** - to the beats of a folk song |
| **Matki** | Madhya Pradesh | performed by the women of the **Malwa** region at **weddings and other festivities**; **mainly solo**, while **balancing a number of earthen pots on the head**; ***Aada* and *Khada Nach*** are popular variants |
| **Hojagiri** | Tripura | a group of **four to six women or young girls** move **only the lower half of the body** while **balancing earthen pitchers and other props**; performed during **Lakshmi Puja** |
| **Jawara** | Madhya Pradesh | **balancing a basket full of jawar on the head**, with **heavy instrumental music** |
| **Tera Tali** | Rajasthan | performed by the **Kamar tribe**; **women sit on the ground**, and **cymbals (*Manjiras*) are tied to different parts of the body** of the performer, which is **quite unique** |
| **Garba** | Gujarat | women dance in circles with **rhythmic claps** around a **lamp-lit earthen pot, the *Garbha deep*** |
| **Kummi** | Tamil Nadu and Kerala | the counter-case: **rhythmic clapping instead of instruments** |

Three points.

- **The Tripura pitcher dance is the most technically demanding entry in the folk table**, and it deserves a sentence in any answer that rejects the "simple" characterisation: four to six performers, earthen pitchers and props balanced, and movement restricted to the **lower half of the body only**.
- **Two Gujarat forms use a stick and are not the same.** *Dandiya Raas* uses **hand-held sticks in a circle**; *Tippani* uses a **composite floor-beating implement** made of two long wooden sticks joined by a block, and is performed **exclusively by women** from Saurashtra. ❌ Do not merge them.
- **The clapping form is analytically useful precisely because it has no property.** A dance whose rhythm is produced by **rhythmic clapping instead of instruments** shows that the property-led family is a design choice, not a necessity.

### 2. Family five: formation-led forms

Here the **shape made by the dancers** is the primary content.

| Dance | Region | Formation and content |
|---|---|---|
| **Garba** | Gujarat | performed during **Navratri**; **women dance in circles with rhythmic claps around a lamp-lit earthen pot (*Garbha deep*)**; **inscribed on the UNESCO Representative List in 2023** |
| **Ghoomar** (Gangore) | Rajasthan | a traditional folk dance performed by the women of the **Bhil tribe**; characterised by the **pirouetting movements** of the women, which bring into prominence the **multi-coloured vibrancies of the flowing Ghaghra** |
| **Fugdi** | Konkan region - Maharashtra and Goa | performed **by women during festivals**; they dance in **varied formations, mostly in circles or rows**; it has **many sub-types as per local customs** |
| **Kummi** | Tamil Nadu and Kerala | performed usually by **Tamil women standing in a circular formation**; uses **rhythmic clapping instead of instruments**; common during **Pongal and religious festivals**; variants include **Kolattam and Pinnal Kolattam** |
| **Jhoomar** | Punjab | performed **in a circle**; the **movement of the arms to the tune of the drums** is its main forte |
| **Jhora** | Uttarakhand | a **Kumaoni** folk dance performed by **men and women** during **festivals and fairs**; dancers **move in circles, singing folk songs**, symbolising **joy, unity and community bonding** |
| **Karma Naach** | eastern India, especially the Chota Nagpur Plateau | dancers form a **circle** and dance with their **arms around each other's waist** |
| **Bihu** | Assam | **lively formations**, quick hand moves and brisk steps |
| **Rangma** | Nagaland | **mock war formations** |
| **Paika** | Jharkhand and southern Odisha | **infantry-like formations** |

⚠️ **The circle is the single most widespread formation in the Indian folk field**, and it appears in at least six states across four regions. It is therefore a **weak discriminator** on its own; what discriminates is what is at the centre (a lamp-lit pot), what is in the hands (sticks, nothing, arms around a waist), who is dancing (women only, men and women) and on what occasion (Navratri, Pongal, the harvest, a fair, the Karma festival).

⚠️ **A word that recurs with different referents.** *Kolattam* names an Andhra stick dance in its own right **and** a recorded variant of the Tamil clapping dance. Both records stand; note the region before resolving the reference.

### 3. Family six: impersonation-led forms

Here the dancer **becomes a figure** - an animal, a character or a masked being.

| Dance | Region | What is impersonated |
|---|---|---|
| **Hulivesha** | Karnataka | male dancers **painted like tigers** dance and portray an **angry tiger** to honour **Goddess Durga**, whose favourite animal is the tiger; generally during **Navaratri** |
| **Gaur Muria** (Bison horn) | Chhattisgarh | an important **ritualistic** dance of the **Muria tribes** of the **Bastar** region; the dance **imitates the movements of a bison** and is performed **in a group by both men and women** |
| **Mayilattam** | Tamil Nadu and Kerala | **young girls dressed as peacocks**, with **colourful headgears, beaks and feathers**; also known as the **peacock dance**; similar dances include ***Kaalai Attam*** (bull), ***Karadi Attam*** (bear), ***Aali Attam*** (demon) and ***Paampu Attam*** (snake) |
| **Rai Nritya** | Madhya Pradesh | originated in the **Bundelkhand** region; mainly performed by the **Bedia tribe**; it is the **peacock dance of the women** |
| **Singhi Chham** | Sikkim | **furry lion costumes** symbolising the **snow lion** |
| **Kalbelia** | Rajasthan | a **sensuous** folk dance performed by the women of the **Kalbelia community**; the **costumes and dance movements are similar to that of the serpents**; the ***Been*** - a **wind instrument played by snake charmers** - is its popular musical instrument; **UNESCO inscribed Kalbelia folk songs and dances in the Representative List in 2010** |
| **Chakyar Koothu** | Kerala | the performer **dresses as a snake** |
| **Butta Bommalu** | Andhra Pradesh | literally **basket toys**; a popular dance form of the **West Godavari district**; dancers wear **toy-like masks** and entertain with **graceful movements and non-verbal music** |
| **Dumhal** | Jammu and Kashmir | performed by the **Watal tribe**; **colourful costumes with a tall conical hat for men**; performers **dance and sing to the drum beats** |
| **Gambhira** / **Padayani** / **Chhau** | WB / Kerala / three states | mask traditions treated in Lesson 19 and Lesson 18 |

Two analytical points worth making in an answer.

- **Two different peacock dances are recorded in two different states**, by different performers and under different names. ❌ Do not merge them; the Tamil form is danced by young girls with beaks and feathers and sits inside a four-member animal-impersonation set, while the Madhya Pradesh form is a women's dance of a named Bundelkhand tribe.
- **The Tamil *Attam* set is a complete impersonation vocabulary in one place** - peacock, bull, bear, demon and snake - and is the single most efficient evidence that animal impersonation is a systematic idiom rather than a scattering of curiosities. Joined to the Karnataka tiger, the Bastar bison, the Sikkim snow lion, the Rajasthani serpent movement and Chhau's serpent and peacock narrations, it supports a general statement about the Indian dance field.

### 4. Family seven: life-cycle, labour and social forms

Here the subject is **human social life** - marriage, separation, celebration, beauty, courtesan performance.

| Dance | Region | Subject and detail |
|---|---|---|
| **Domkach** | Bihar | a **group dance, usually by women**, accompanied by folk songs that celebrate **marriage rituals, social bonding and joy** |
| **Jat-Jatin** | Bihar | popular in the **northern parts of Bihar, especially the regions of Mithila**; unique in its representation of the **tender love and quarrel of a married couple** |
| **Biraha** and its variant **Bidesia** | Bihar | folk dances of rural Bihar showing **women's pain when partners are away**; **however, this dance form is practised solely by males, who play the role of female characters as well** |
| **Lavani** | Maharashtra | derived from ***Lavanya*** (**beauty**); **flourished during the Peshwa era (eighteenth century)**; performed to the **rapid beats of the *dholki***; women wear the **nine-yard *Nauvari* saree** along with **heavy jewellery like bangles, *kamar-band*, anklets and earrings** |
| **Dadra** | Uttar Pradesh | the **semi-classical** form of a dance popular in Uttar Pradesh, **accompanied by the music of the same style**; **extremely popular among the courtesans of the Lucknow region** |
| **Bhangra** / **Giddha** | Punjab | **Bhangra** is the **highly energetic** folk dance of Punjab, accompanied by **catchy drum beats**, a popular form of **celebration during festivities**; **Giddha is the female counterpart of the male Bhangra** |
| **Jhora** | Uttarakhand | symbolises **joy, unity and community bonding** |
| **Tarangamel** | Goa | celebrates the **youthfulness** of the region |
| **Gotipua** | Odisha | ***Gotipua*** means **'single boy'**; originating from the **Mahari tradition around the sixteenth century**; features **acrobatic and yogic movements**; a **precursor to classical Odissi** |

⚠️ **The Bihar entry is the most instructive single line in the folk table.** A dance whose subject is **women's pain when partners are away** is **practised solely by males, who play the role of female characters as well**. Three things follow. First, **subject and performer do not have to match**, which is the same lesson taught by the monastic form that carries the Lasya aspect and by the boys who continued a women's temple tradition. Second, **migration is a recorded theme of Indian folk performance** - the variant name itself refers to the one who has gone abroad. Third, **gendered performance conventions in the folk field are as codified as they are in the classical field**, which is another reason the "without formal training" description must be read narrowly.

⚠️ **The Lucknow courtesan entry should be read against Lesson 14.** The same milieu that is described with a moral verdict when it appears in the history of a classical form is described here, plainly, as the setting in which a semi-classical form was extremely popular. Placing the two records side by side is a legitimate and powerful source-critical observation for a Mains answer.

### 5. The roll of named community carriers

Collected in one place, because a folk-dance answer that names carriers is immediately better than one that names only states.

| Community or tribe | Dance | Region |
|---|---|---|
| **Bodo** | Bagurumba | Assam |
| **Muria** | Gaur Muria (Bison horn) | Bastar, Chhattisgarh |
| **Yadav** | Raut Nacha | Chhattisgarh |
| **Rathwa** | Rathwa ni Gher | Gujarat |
| **Watal** | Dumhal | Jammu and Kashmir |
| **Nalike** | Aati Kalenja | Tulu Nadu |
| **Chakyar** | Chakyar Koothu | Kerala |
| **Bedia** | Rai Nritya | Bundelkhand, Madhya Pradesh |
| **Bhil** | Ghoomar / Gangore | Rajasthan |
| **Kalbelia** | Kalbelia | Rajasthan |
| **Kamar** | Tera Tali | Rajasthan |
| **Banjara** | Lambadi | Andhra Pradesh |
| **Tripuri** | Garia, Lebang Boomani | Tripura |
| **Keipeng** | Meladom | Tripura |
| **Tulu people** | Buta Kola, Nagaradhane | Tulu Nadu |
| **Vaishnava community of Ganjam** | Ghanta Mrudangam | Odisha |
| **tribal Sikhs** | Jhoomar | Punjab |
| **tribes of the Chota Nagpur Plateau** | Karma Naach | eastern India |

### 6. The argument against a folk-classical hierarchy

Assemble what this session has established, and the hierarchy becomes indefensible on the record's own evidence.

1. **The boundary is administrative.** One official list contains nine forms, another contains eight, and the difference is a mask-and-martial tradition. A form with treatise claims, a named historical reviver, temple sculptures and a modern reconstruction holds no classical status at all.
2. **Technical demand is not the dividing line.** Balancing several earthen pots while dancing solo, moving only the lower half of the body while balancing pitchers, stepping between tapped bamboo poles, dancing with cymbals tied to different parts of the body while seated - these are recorded in the folk field.
3. **Transmission exists on both sides of the line.** The classical forms transmit through a guru carrying a *sampradaya*; the folk forms transmit through named communities. Both are systems; they differ in whether the system is codified and examinable.
4. **The traffic runs both ways.** A boys' tradition that arose when a temple tradition lapsed is recorded as a **precursor to classical Odissi**; a folk-drama style shaped the formalisation of a classical Andhra form; two temple dance-dramas became a classical Kerala form; a classical Assamese form is an amalgamation of two earlier Assamese traditions. ❌ The classical forms did not descend to the folk; several of them **came out of it**.
5. **The international instrument does not use the hierarchy at all.** Its three Indian dance-related records in this topic are a mask tradition, a community folk tradition and a mass participatory festival dance.

✅ **The sentence for an answer:** "Classical and folk name **different modes of organisation and transmission** - codified and guru-transmitted against occasion-bound and community-carried - and not different levels of worth; the historical traffic between them runs in both directions, and the boundary itself is drawn by institutions that do not agree on where it lies."

### Examination use

For Prelims, every row of the four tables is a potential pair-matching item, and the details that decide them are the objects, the numbers and the named variants: eight to forty dancers with sticks, two long wooden sticks joined by a block, several pots on the head, four to six with pitchers and only the lower body moving, cymbals tied to the body while seated, a lamp-lit *Garbha deep*, basket-toy masks, a tall conical hat, the *Been*, the *dholki*, the nine-yard *Nauvari* saree, and the *Aada*, *Khada Nach*, *Airukali*, *Tattamakali*, *Pinnal Kolattam*, *Puja Kunitha*, *Bidesia* and *Giddha* variants.

For Mains, this lesson supplies the anti-hierarchy argument in a form that can be defended point by point, plus the carrier roll that lets you write about communities rather than about states. Either alone will lift an answer on folk and classical dance above the standard treatment.

### Traps introduced here

- Merging the two Gujarat stick forms, or the two peacock dances.
- Using "performed in a circle" as an identification.
- Assigning the *Been* to a form other than the Rajasthani serpent dance.
- Calling the Punjab female counterpart a separate unrelated dance rather than the recorded counterpart of Bhangra.
- Dropping the male-performer detail from the Bihar separation dance.
- Writing that classical forms evolved out of folk forms as a general law, or that folk forms are degraded classical ones; the record shows specific, named traffic in both directions.
- Treating the community carrier as optional detail.

### Revision notes

1. **Property-led**: **Kolattam** (AP, sticks, **8-40**); **Dandiya Raas** (Guj, sticks, Navratri, **Raas Garba**); **Cheraw** (Mizoram, bamboo tapped by men, girls dance, **likely foreign origin**); **Pata Kunitha** (KA, ribboned bamboo poles); **Tippani** (Guj, **Saurashtra**, **exclusively women**, **two sticks joined by a block**, floor-beating); **Matki** (MP, **Malwa**, weddings, solo, pots on the head; **Aada, Khada Nach**); **Hojagiri** (Tripura, **4-6**, only the **lower half** of the body, pitchers, **Lakshmi Puja**); **Jawara** (MP, basket of jawar); **Tera Tali** (RJ, **Kamar** tribe, **seated**, **cymbals tied to the body**); **Garba** (pot = ***Garbha deep***).
2. **Formation-led**: **Garba** (circle, **UNESCO 2023**); **Ghoomar/Gangore** (RJ, **Bhil**, **pirouetting**, flowing **Ghaghra**); **Fugdi** (Konkan, women, circles or rows, many sub-types); **Kummi** (TN-Kerala, circular, **clapping instead of instruments**, **Pongal**; variants **Kolattam, Pinnal Kolattam**); **Jhoomar**, **Jhora**, **Karma Naach** (arms round the waist), **Bihu**, **Rangma**, **Paika**.
3. **Impersonation-led**: **Hulivesha** (KA, **tiger**, **Durga**, Navaratri); **Gaur Muria** (CG, **Muria**, **Bastar**, **bison**); **Mayilattam** (TN-Kerala, **peacock**; with ***Kaalai Attam*** bull, ***Karadi Attam*** bear, ***Aali Attam*** demon, ***Paampu Attam*** snake); **Rai Nritya** (MP, **Bedia**, **peacock dance of the women**); **Singhi Chham** (SK, **snow lion**); **Kalbelia** (RJ, **serpent** movements, ***Been***, **UNESCO 2010**); **Butta Bommalu** (AP, **basket toys**, **West Godavari**, toy-like masks, **non-verbal music**); **Dumhal** (J&K, **Watal**, **tall conical hat**).
4. **Life-cycle and social**: **Domkach** (Bihar, women, **marriage rituals**); **Jat-Jatin** (Bihar, **Mithila**, **love and quarrel of a married couple**); **Biraha/Bidesia** (Bihar, **women's pain when partners are away**, **practised solely by males**); **Lavani** (Mah., from ***Lavanya*** = beauty, **Peshwa era, eighteenth century**, ***dholki***, **nine-yard *Nauvari* saree**); **Dadra** (UP, **semi-classical**, **courtesans of the Lucknow region**); **Bhangra**/**Giddha** (Punjab, female counterpart); **Jhora** (joy, unity, community bonding); **Tarangamel** (Goa, youthfulness); **Gotipua** (Odisha, **'single boy'**, **sixteenth century**, **acrobatic and yogic**, **precursor to classical Odissi**).
5. **Community carriers**: Bodo, Muria, Yadav, Rathwa, Watal, Nalike, Chakyar, Bedia, Bhil, Kalbelia, Kamar, Banjara, Tripuri, Keipeng, the Tulu people, the Vaishnava community of Ganjam, tribal Sikhs, and the tribes of the Chota Nagpur Plateau.
6. Anti-hierarchy argument: **administrative boundary + comparable technical demand + transmission on both sides + two-way historical traffic + an international instrument that ignores the hierarchy**.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Folk dance in India should be analysed as a system of occasions rather than as a catalogue of states." Discuss. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

Occasion predicts form; the state does not.

Occasion determines content. The Tripuri cycle runs from an **April sowing festival**, through a monsoon dance whose bamboo clappers mimic catching insects that foretell the harvest, to a post-harvest dance originating in the weaving process. Kali worship at Kerala's Bhagavati temples produces a masked martial dance with *kolams*; Tulu cobra worship produces two distinct rituals; Navratri in Gujarat sets women circling a lamp-lit *Garbha deep*.

Occasion also fixes performer and property: a Malwa wedding produces a solo women's dance with pots balanced on the head, a Bihar marriage a women's group dance, a Naga war memory mock war formations.

The state predicts little: Chhau has three styles in three states, one without masks, and Kerala alone yields a harvest dance, a temple martial dance and a solo snake-costumed form.

Qualification: the state remains useful for retrieval, but is not an analytical category.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the proposition converted into a testable claim 1 + a complete agrarian cycle used as primary evidence 3 + occasion determining performer and property across at least three regions 3 + the state label falsified with the three-state mask tradition and one state's internal variety 2 + a qualification that preserves the state's retrieval use 1.**

### Concept check

**Question:** A Bihar folk dance whose subject is women's pain when partners are away is practised solely by males, who also play the female characters. Name two other instances in this session in which the performer's identity does not match the subject or the aspect, and state the rule the three instances together establish.

**Model answer:** The two other instances are, first, **Sattriya**, which is recorded as combining both *Lasya* - the aspect described as symbolic of the feminine features of the art form - and *Tandava*, and which is generally performed in a group by **male monks called *Bhokots***; and second, the **gotipua** tradition of Odisha, in which **young boys were recruited and dressed as females** to continue an art form previously practised by the *maharis*, acquiring their own name, their own acrobatic and yogic idiom and, eventually, the status of a precursor to classical Odissi. A fourth instance could be added from the classical field: Bharatanatyam's *Ekcharya lasyam*, in which a single dancer plays multiple roles, necessarily across genders. The rule the instances establish is that in Indian dance the **performer's identity, the subject represented and the expressive register used are three independent variables**. A male troupe may carry a feminine-coded aspect; a male performer may voice a woman's experience; boys may sustain a women's repertoire. It follows that an examiner's statement of the form "this dance is performed by women because it expresses women's feelings" is unsafe, and that the gender history of Indian dance has to be reconstructed from carrier records, patronage and reform, not inferred from subject matter.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating cross-gender performance as an exception or an oddity.
