Lesson 23's attribution discipline adds the three cautions this item family requires: **do not use a proponent as a discriminator when the name appears in two lists**, **do not multiply persons out of a spelling variant**, and **do not lean on an attribution that only one source carries**.

## Earlier Civil Services Main Examination questions on Indian dance

Fourteen are on record, spanning 1980 to 2013. Main Examination questions carry no response choices, so each appears in full with its directive, what it is asking for, and a concise route to the answer. **No solved model answer is given for any of them.**

| # | Year | The question | Directive | What it is asking for | How to answer it, and where the material sits |
|---:|---:|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **2013** | "Discuss the 'Tandava' dance as recorded in early Indian inscriptions." | **Discuss** | The *aspect* named Tandava and its attestation in **inscriptional** evidence - a source-type demand, not a description of a dance form | Open with the aspect as defined in Lesson 3 - symbolic of the male features, with greater emphasis on rhythm and movement - then separate the **aspect** from the **named dances** that carry the word, as Lesson 3 section 5 requires. ⚠️ This session records **sculptural** attestation for a named warrior dance at a Kakatiya temple and does **not** hold inscriptional evidence; say so rather than inventing an inscription, and build the answer from the recorded posture, iconographic and sculptural material of Lesson 21 with the evidential limit attached |
| 2 | **2012** | "Describe the Bagurumba folk dance." | **Describe** | A single named folk dance: region, community, character and instruments | Lesson 19 supplies the complete record - a **nature-themed** folk dance of **Assam's Bodo tribe**, performed with instruments like ***sifung, kham* and *serja***. Use Lesson 19's four-question frame to structure a short answer: occasion and theme, community, instruments, and the caution against describing folk dance as simple |
| 3 | **2011** | "List the classical dance forms of India as per Sangeet Natak Akademi?" | **List** | The exact eight-form list of a named body | Lesson 8 supplies the eight names and, decisively, the **eight-versus-nine** distinction. A list demand attached to a **named body** is an invitation to add the sentence that the Ministry of Culture recognises nine including Chhau - which answers the question asked and demonstrates that the candidate knows why the body was named in the stem |
| 4 | **2009** | "List the salient features of the important folk dances of central India or North Eastern India." | **List** | Regionally grouped folk dances, with features rather than names alone | Lessons 19 and 20 supply both regions. For central India: the Bundelkhand and Malwa material - the peacock dance of the Bedia women, the harvest dance with a basket of jawar balanced on the head, the pot-balancing wedding dance with its two variants - together with the Bastar bison dance and the Diwali dance of the Yadav community. For the north-east: the Bodo nature dance, the Assamese group dance, the Manipur sword-and-spear dance, the Naga war dance, the three-stage Tripuri agricultural cycle and the Tripura hunting dance. Structure by **occasion, community, property and formation**, not by state list |
| 5 | **2008** | "Write about Perini Sivathandavam." | **Write about** | A single named dance with a Tandava-bearing title | Lesson 19 supplies the complete record for the Telangana warrior dance: performed in honour of **Lord Shiva**, depicted in the sculptures near the **Garbha Gudi of the Ramappa Temple at Warangal**, called the **'Dance of Warriors'**, performed by warriors, and reaching its pinnacle during the rule of the **Kakatiya** dynasty. Lesson 3 supplies the caution that a named dance carrying the word is not the same as the aspect, and Lesson 21 supplies the one case in this topic of a direct named sculptural attestation |
| 6 | **1994** | "Write about the Indian ballet." | **Write about** | A composite term not used by the sources | ⚠️ This session records **no** tradition under the name "ballet" and does not supply one. The defensible approach is to treat the demand as being about **narrative dance-drama** and to answer from what is owned: the dance-dramas of this topic - the Kerala form where dance and drama cannot be clearly separated, the Andhra form that becomes a dance-drama when the dancer takes the singer's role, and the Assamese *Ankia Naat* and *Bhaona* - together with Lesson 21's classification rule. State the terminological caution explicitly rather than inventing a tradition to fit the word |
| 7 | **1992** | "Write a short note on Kathakali." | **Write a short note** | Compressed identification of one form | Lesson 12 in compressed order: two parent dance-dramas under feudal-lord patronage; the *Katha* + *Kali* etymology; the all-male troupe; minimal props with elaborate make-up and headgear; the five *vesham* types and the colour code; *Attakkatha* in *Manipravalam*; rasas through eye and eyebrow movement with the *Hastalakshana Deepika* gesture base; open-air staging with a brass lamp; *chenda* and *maddala* framing dusk to dawn; the feudal-breakdown decline and the 1930s revival |

Do not spend the two features on region and a shared quality. Add the eight-versus-nine sentence from Lesson 8 |

## Questions that touch this topic without belonging to it

| Year and paper | Question | Why it is worth knowing about |
|---|---|---|

It is dance-adjacent because the painting's name carries a dance term and one of its described subjects is a kind of dance. **No response choices are printed and no answer is given**; this session teaches nothing about that painting |
| **2024, Mains GS-I** | Chola achievements in art and architecture | A temple-architecture question. Dance material strengthens such an answer through the shared posture vocabulary of Lesson 21, but the question is not a dance question |
| **2025, Mains GS-I** | the Chandella artform, its resilient vigour and breadth of life | A temple-architecture question. Lesson 21's posture-and-gesture vocabulary - *tribhanga*, *Tandava* and *Lasya*, the *mudra* system - is the right tool for reading dynamic figural sculpture, and the evidential limit stated there must travel with it |

## The honest nil, stated plainly

Across **Mains General Studies Paper I from 2018 to 2026 inclusive**, and across the **2026 Essay**, **there is no question on this topic**. Across **Preliminary General Studies Paper I from 2018 to 2026 inclusive**, there is **one**, the 2024 question printed above. Everything else in this section is either an older question or a question belonging to a neighbouring topic.

⚠️ That nil is itself examinable information, and it should change how the topic is prepared rather than how much. The Prelims record shows a **sustained** pattern of identification, pair-matching, posture-definition and discrimination items drawn from this material across at least 2011, 2012, 2013, 2014 and 2018, with a status-and-date item in 2024. The Mains record shows fourteen questions between 1980 and 2013 and none since 2018. The preparation implication is direct: **this topic is currently a Prelims identification-and-status owner and a Mains support owner**, and the highest-yield material is therefore the discriminator set of Lesson 23, the recognition material of Lesson 8, and the cross-topic posture vocabulary of Lesson 21.

# CUMULATIVE CONCEPT CHECKS

These checks cross lesson boundaries. Each one requires material from at least three lessons, which is what distinguishes them from the lesson-local checks already completed.

## Cumulative check 1 - after the foundation block, Lessons 1 to 5

**Question:** Using only the foundation material, explain why Indian classical dance developed such a fine-grained technical vocabulary, and name the three treatises that supply it.

**Model answer:** The chain runs from purpose to precision. The root conception treats performance as a composite art formed from four contributions - words, gestures, music and emotions - and among those four the emotional contribution is not a by-product but a constituent: the performance exists in order to produce a named emotional effect. Nine such effects are named, and several of them are close neighbours that a coarse vocabulary could not separate - sorrow and compassion are given as one rasa, horror and wonder as two, peace and tranquility as one. To produce a specified effect from that set reliably, across performers and generations, the tradition needs a vocabulary fine enough to distinguish them, which is why the apparatus is codified at three scales - the gesture, the movement unit and the whole-body movement - and why a further three-way distinction separates pure rhythmic movement from narrative and from expression. The three treatises are the ***Natyashastra***, conventionally attributed to Bharata, which supplies the composite-art conception, the two aspects and the whole-body movement units; the ***Abhinaya Darpana*** of **Nandikeshvara**, which supplies the three-element distinction and the *Nayaka-Nayika* formulation; and the ***Hastalakshana Deepika***, the treatise on the language of hand gestures on which one Kerala form's *mudras* are based.

**Misconception to avoid:** Explaining the codification as a consequence of the tradition's age. Age does not produce precision; a stated purpose plus a requirement of reliable transmission does. The proof is that the same codification is what later made reconstruction possible when a carrier community was displaced.

## Cumulative check 2 - after the recognition and transmission block, Lessons 6 to 9

**Question:** A candidate claims that the number of classical dance forms in India, the transmission of those forms, and their antiquity are three aspects of one thing. Separate them.

**Model answer:** They are three independent variables and the record separates them cleanly. **Number** is an institutional output: a national academy identifies eight forms and the Ministry of Culture identifies nine, the difference being a mask-and-martial tradition of three states - and since two organs of the same government answer differently, the number cannot be a property of the dances. **Transmission** is a social arrangement, and the record shows at least six different ones operating under a single stated principle: a hereditary temple-attached performer, a travelling troupe, an all-male troupe, a household lineage-school, a monastic order and a named community - all governed by the rule that knowledge transfers through a guru who carries a *sampradaya*. **Antiquity** is a historical question that the other two do not answer: a form may be named in an old treatise, disrupted in the medieval period, substituted in the sixteenth century, reconstructed in the nineteenth or twentieth, and recognised in the twentieth or later. The clinching evidence that the three are independent is a Bengal form with claimed treatise roots, a named historical reviver, temple sculptural evidence and a named modern reconstruction - which holds **no** recognition at all.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that because all three are official-sounding, they must be measuring the same underlying reality. They are answers to three different questions - how many does a body count, who holds the skill, and how old is the practice - and the only reason they are confused is that the word "classical" is used as though it settled all three at once.

## Cumulative check 3 - after the four southern and western forms, Lessons 10 to 13

**Question:** Four classical forms from three states have been taught. Build the shortest chain of facts that separates all four, using no feature that any two of them share.

**Model answer:** Four facts, each belonging to exactly one form. **Bharatanatyam:** the seven-item recital order defined in the early nineteenth century by four named Thanjavur teachers - *Alarippu, Jatiswaram, Shabdam, Varnam, Padam, Jawali, Thillana* - together with the *Ekcharya lasyam* convention in which one dancer plays multiple roles. **Kuchipudi:** the *daaru*, a small composition of dance and song choreographed for the revelation of each character, together with *Tarangam* on the edges of a brass plate. **Kathakali:** the song-text called *Attakkatha* and the five *vesham* make-up types with their colour code, in an all-male troupe performing from dusk to dawn by the light of a brass lamp. **Mohiniyattam:** the forty *Atavakul* movements and the white or off-white *Kasavu* saree with gold brocade and no elaborate facial make-up. Now note what has been deliberately **excluded** because it is shared: the *mridangam* (Bharatanatyam and Kuchipudi), *Manipravalam* (both Kerala forms), the Benarasi female costume (Bharatanatyam and Kuchipudi), devotional theme (all four), *Lasya* and *Tandava* in some balance (all four), and the proponent Yamini Krishnamurthy (two of them).

**Misconception to avoid:** Building the separation from region. Three states carry four forms, and one state carries two of them, so region halves the problem at best and never finishes it.

## Cumulative check 4 - after the four northern and eastern forms, Lessons 14 to 17

**Question:** Each of these four forms has a recorded relationship with an institution or a lineage. Compare the four relationships and say what the comparison establishes.

**Model answer:** **Kathak** is carried by **household lineage-schools**: four of them, each attached to a named patron or founder and each optimising a different technical axis - expression and grace, fluency and speed with long rhythmic patterns, percussion, and floor use with symmetry. The structure is explicitly attributed to the form's basis in the Hindustani musical style. **Odissi** is carried by a **succession of substituted carriers**: a temple-attached community patronised by a Jain king, whose system became defunct; boys dressed as females who continued the art and developed an acrobatic and yogic idiom; and a court variant running in parallel. **Manipuri** is carried by **royal and then educational institutions**: an eighteenth-century ruler attempted its revival, and a Bengali poet brought it into the limelight by introducing it at a school in another state. **Sattriya** is carried by a **monastic order**: monks in Vaishnava monasteries perform it as part of daily ritual, using the founder's own compositions under strictly laid-down rules. What the comparison establishes is that there is **no single institutional model** for the survival of an Indian classical dance form, and that the models differ in exactly the way that matters - in whether the carrier reproduces itself by inheritance, by recruitment, by patronage or by substitution. Resilience follows from that, and not from the age or the prestige of the form.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the household lineage-school as the general model for Indian classical dance because it is the most familiar term. It is recorded here as a **Kathak** structure, explained by that form's musical basis; the other three forms in this block are organised in three entirely different ways.

## Cumulative check 5 - after the classification frontier and the living field, Lessons 18 to 20

**Question:** State the case against a folk-classical hierarchy in five evidenced points, and then state the strongest objection to your own case and answer it.

**Model answer:** **The case.** First, the **boundary is administrative and contested**: one official list contains nine forms and another eight, and a form with treatise claims, a named historical reviver, temple sculptures and a modern reconstruction holds no classical status at all. Second, **technical demand does not divide the two**: the folk field includes solo dancing while balancing several earthen pots, group dancing that balances pitchers while moving only the lower half of the body, dancing between tapped bamboo poles, and seated dancing with cymbals tied to different parts of the body. Third, **transmission exists on both sides**: guru-and-*sampradaya* on one, named communities on the other. Fourth, **the historical traffic runs both ways**: a boys' substitution tradition is recorded as a precursor to a classical form, a folk-drama style shaped a classical form's formalisation, two temple dance-dramas became a classical form, and a classical form is an amalgamation of two earlier regional traditions. Fifth, the **international safeguarding instrument ignores the hierarchy**, its Indian dance-related records here being a mask tradition, a community folk tradition and a mass festival dance. **The objection.** The sources themselves describe folk dances as spontaneous, simple and performed without formal training, and describe the classical forms as governed by the rules of a treatise - so a real difference is being recorded, not invented. **The answer.** The difference is real but it is a difference of **mode**, not of **level**: codified-and-examinable against occasion-bound-and-community-carried. The evaluative words in the description - *simple*, *without formal training* - are comparative and should be read as describing transmission and reach rather than skill, which is exactly what the technical evidence in the second point requires.

**Misconception to avoid:** Over-correcting into the claim that there is no difference at all. Codification is real, the recital orders are real, and the strictly laid-down rules of one tradition are recorded. The argument is about what the difference is, not about whether there is one.

## Cumulative check 6 - across the whole session

**Question:** A single examination stem asks: "Trace the relationship between institutional recognition and the living practice of dance in India." Build the complete answer skeleton from the whole session, naming the lesson that supplies each move.

**Model answer:** **Move one - define recognition as an act, not a description** (Lesson 8): three bodies, three questions, three answers, and a rule that a form may hold one, both or neither. **Move two - evidence the institutional character from disagreement** (Lesson 18): the eight-versus-nine discrepancy within government, with the differing item named. **Move three - evidence it from a negative case** (Lesson 18): a form with every evidential credential and no recognition. **Move four - show that the practice predates and outruns the recognition** (Lessons 10 to 17): named patrons and carriers from a Jain king through Vijayanagara and Golconda rulers, Travancore rulers, Mughal-era courts, feudal lords and Vaishnava monasteries, none of which required any modern list. **Move five - show what recognition changed** (Lesson 22): reconstruction from hereditary repertoire, fixed recital orders, institutional training visible in performers' names, a school in another region carrying a form to national visibility, and international circuits evidenced in the proponent record. **Move six - state the cost** (Lesson 22): the knowledge that made reconstruction possible was held by communities whose position was being dismantled, so revival saved the repertoire and relocated its practitioners. **Move seven - show that the recognition frame keeps moving** (Lesson 8): a dated record on an international list, where the latest element is not a dance at all, which proves the instrument is tracking living heritage and not a canon. **Move eight - close with the verdict** (Lesson 23): the forms as we know them are the outcome of twentieth-century reconstruction and institutional recognition acting on much older regional practices; continuity is real, and so is the break.

**Misconception to avoid:** Writing the skeleton as a chronology from ancient practice to modern recognition. The relationship is not a sequence but a **layering**: the practice continues under its own carriers while recognition is superimposed, which is why a non-recognised form can be fully alive and why an inscribed folk dance can hold no classical status. A chronological framing quietly reintroduces the ladder the whole session has refused.

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

These eight questions are original to this session and sit on top of the 23 lesson-local models already completed - **31 original Mains models in all, with 31 distinct scoring notes**. None reproduces or paraphrases any of the twenty older official questions recorded in the previous section, and no two of the 31 repeat one another's demand. Word ceilings are stated and independently counted, and each model carries its own scoring note.

## Ten marks

### Original Mains 1

**"The Natya Shastra's decisive contribution was not a technique but a method of analysis." Comment. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

The claim holds; the evidence is the tradition's own vocabulary.

The conception derives performance from four contributions - words, gestures, music and emotions - and calls the resulting art a complete art uniting music, sculpture, poetry and drama. That states how performance should be **analysed**, not how to move.

Three consequences follow. Indian dance is described through categories rather than steps: pure rhythmic movement, dramatic representation and expression; two aspects, grace and rhythm; nine named emotional targets. The categories transfer across arts, which is why the same posture and gesture vocabulary reads temple sculpture. And they transfer across forms: eight regionally distinct traditions are intelligible through one set of terms because they inherit the same analytical scheme rather than the same choreography.

Qualification: the text also describes techniques, postures and stagecraft, so the contrast is not absolute.

Verdict: technique varies by region; the method of analysis is shared.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the four-contribution derivation used as evidence for a method claim 2 + three named categorial sets 3 + transfer across arts 2 + transfer across forms 2 + the qualification that the text also records technique 1.**

### Original Mains 2

**Explain why the carrier of a performing tradition is a better predictor of its survival than its age. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

Age is not a mechanism; a carrier is.

Compare three carriers. A **monastic order** recruits rather than inherits, performs as part of daily ritual, uses its founder's own compositions and works under strictly laid-down rules - it reproduces itself without an external patron, and no decline is recorded for it. A **patron class** supports an art without reproducing it: when a feudal set-up broke down, the dance-drama declined, and recovery needed a new patron and a named agent. A **hereditary community** holds both the skill and the repertoire: when such a system declined, the form nearly vanished, and what returned was a reconstruction built from the knowledge of the displaced.

A fourth case shows survival without rescue: when a temple-attached system became defunct, boys dressed as females continued the art with an idiom of their own.

Qualification: carriers are socially embedded, so the claim is about resilience, not permanence.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the mechanism-versus-attribute distinction stated at the outset 1 + three carrier types each with its recorded outcome 6 + the substitution case as survival without rescue 2 + a qualification limiting the claim to resilience 1.**

### Original Mains 3

**Examine the proposition that an instrument is often the most reliable way to identify an Indian classical dance form. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

The proposition is largely sound, for a structural reason.

Accompaniment is form-specific because it was assembled regionally alongside the dance. Hence a named drum usually settles the identification: the *pung* is an intricate element of one north-eastern recital; *chenda* and *maddala* frame a Kerala dance-drama from dusk to dawn; the *khol* accompanies an Assamese monastic tradition; *pakhawaj* with *manjira*, sitar and flute accompanies an Odishan form set to Hindustani music; and a tabla player enters a north Indian recital as a competitive partner.

The proposition has two limits. Some instruments are shared - the *mridangam* belongs to two southern forms - so an unnamed or shared instrument decides nothing. And the accompanying **system** can be counter-intuitive: an eastern form uses Hindustani music, which defeats the assumption that non-northern forms are Carnatic.

Verdict: a **named, unshared** instrument is among the strongest identifiers available; a generic one is among the weakest.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the structural reason for form-specific accompaniment 2 + five named instrument-to-form attributions 4 + the shared-instrument limit with a named instance 2 + the counter-intuitive system point 1 + a verdict that distinguishes named from generic 1.**

## Fifteen marks

### Original Mains 4

**"Vaishnavism reorganised who was permitted to dance in India, and it did so differently in different regions." Discuss. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The proposition is accurate, and three separate regional records support it while disagreeing with one another.

In **Odisha**, the advent of Vaishnavism is recorded as the point at which the *mahari* system - the temple-attached female practitioners patronised in earlier times by the Jain king Kharavela - became defunct. The consequence was substitution, not extinction: boys dressed as females continued the art as *gotipuas*, while a further variant continued at the royal courts. The substitute developed an acrobatic and yogic idiom of its own and is recorded as a precursor to the classical form now performed.

In **Andhra**, the same movement produced restriction of a different kind. The form became a monopoly of male Brahmins and began to be performed at temples; its performers were called *Bhagavathalus*, its repertoire focused on Purana narratives, and it flourished under Vijayanagara and Golconda rulers.

In **Manipur**, the effect was neither displacement nor monopoly but **elevation**: the form gained prominence with the advent of Vaishnavism in the fifteenth century, and Krishna became its central theme.

A fourth case completes the picture. In **Assam**, a Vaishnava saint introduced a dance form in its modern form within monasteries, where male monks perform it daily using his own compositions.

Verdict: the movement was a consistent force acting on access, and its outcomes were region-specific - displacement, monopoly, elevation and institutional creation. Any generalised statement about Vaishnavism and Indian dance will be false in at least two of the four cases.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the proposition stated as a testable regional claim 1 + the displacement case with patron, cause, substitution and its idiom 4 + the monopoly case with performers, repertoire and patrons 3 + the elevation case with century and theme 2 + the institutional-creation case 2 + an explicit statement of what a generalisation would get wrong 2 + verdict 1.**

### Original Mains 5

**Assess the proposition that Indian classical dance forms are best understood as mutually permeable rather than as sealed regional traditions. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The proposition is well supported, provided permeability is not mistaken for derivation.

Four kinds of evidence support it. **Qualities travel.** One Kerala form is recorded as combining the grace and elegance of a Tamil Nadu form with the vigour of the other Kerala form - an explicit statement of borrowing across a state boundary. **Expertise travels.** One of the four Thanjavur teachers who defined a Tamil Nadu form's recital order in the early nineteenth century is the same individual recorded as having further developed the Kerala solo form in that century. **Repertoire travels.** A single poet's verse supplies the lyrics of an Odishan form and is used extensively in a Manipur form, while a second poet is used in the latter alone. **Reconstructors travel.** One Malayali poet is credited with reviving both Kerala classical forms, one Bengali poet with carrying a Manipur form into national visibility through a school in his own region, and a foreign critic with one Odishan form's international acclaim.

A fifth point: the shared analytical vocabulary. Eight regionally distinct forms are described through one set of categories inherited from a common treatise tradition.

None of this establishes descent. Borrowing a quality is not being derived from a source, and each form retains its own origin, patronage history, movement inventory, costume and repertoire.

Verdict: the map of Indian classical dance is a field of exchange with distinct centres, not a set of sealed regional units - and not a family tree either.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = four channels of permeability, each with a named instance 8 + the shared analytical vocabulary as a fifth channel 2 + the explicit refusal of a descent claim 3 + a verdict that rejects both the sealed-unit and the family-tree models 2.**

## Twenty marks

### Original Mains 6

**"In Indian dance, the category 'classical' explains less than it appears to." Critically examine. (20 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The proposition is largely defensible. Test it by asking what the category predicts.

**What it does not predict.** It does not predict antiquity: a fifteenth-century foundation sits beside a recital order fixed in the early nineteenth century and a style revived in the twentieth. It does not predict continuity: two of the eight have recorded carrier displacements, and one nearly vanished. It does not predict technical demand: folk dancers balance several pots solo, move only the lower body while balancing pitchers, and dance seated with cymbals tied to the body. It does not predict performer demography: one form moved from solo female to men and groups, another acquired a solo format and female dancers, and a third is danced by male monks. It does not predict membership: one official list counts eight, another nine.

**What it does predict.** Codification: named recital orders, treatise-derived gesture systems, guru-transmitted *sampradayas* and, in one case, strictly laid-down rules for hand gesture and footwork - a genuine difference in mode of organisation.

**The decisive case.** A form with claimed treatise roots, a named reviver, temple sculptures and a named modern reconstruction holds no recognition; the category cannot be read off the evidence.

Verdict: "classical" names a mode of organisation certified by a dated decision - precise for that, and empty as a claim about age, continuity, difficulty or worth.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = the proposition converted into a predictive test 2 + five things the category fails to predict, each evidenced 10 + the one thing it does predict, stated as a real difference in organisation 4 + the non-recognised case as decisive evidence 2 + a verdict that specifies the category's precise and improper uses 2.**

### Original Mains 7

**Evaluate the use of temple sculpture as evidence for the history of Indian dance. (20 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

Sculpture is indispensable for one question here and inadmissible for another; an evaluation must separate them.

**The licence.** The root conception unites dance with sculpture, poetry, music and drama, so reading carved figures through dance categories is authorised by the tradition, not by modern criticism. The record is substantial: dancer sculptures at Khajuraho, Konark, Ellora and Chidambaram depict poses from named classical forms; the three-bend posture innate to one form was used extensively by the Amaravati sculptors; an Odishan cave site provides a form's earliest examples; a Telangana warrior dance is carved near a Kakatiya temple's sanctum; and a Bengal form appears on many temple walls there.

**What it establishes.** That a movement idiom existed, was valued and was worth representing in specified periods and regions, and that dance vocabulary describes those figures correctly.

**What it cannot establish.** That a named form as now performed was danced then. Geographically, the shared three-bend posture appears far from the region of the form to which it is innate, so it evidences a widespread idiom. Historically, the best-anchored form has a recorded carrier discontinuity - a defunct practitioner system, a substituted carrier with its own acrobatic and yogic idiom, and international currency only in the mid-twentieth century.

**The strongest counter-case** is that Telangana dance, tied to a named temple, a named location within it and a named dynasty. Even there the record says the dance is depicted, not that present performance reproduces it.

Verdict: sculpture dates an idiom, not a repertoire.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = the two-question separation stated before the evidence 2 + five named sculptural records 6 + a precise statement of what is established 3 + the geographical and carrier arguments for the limit 5 + the strongest counter-case admitted and bounded 3 + a one-line verdict that distinguishes idiom from repertoire 1.**

### Original Mains 8

**"Folk dance in India is a record of how communities organise their year, their work and their conflicts." Critically examine. (20 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The proposition is strongly supported - better than the familiar claim that folk dance expresses regional identity.

**The year.** One tribal community's cycle runs through three named dances: an April festival marking the commencement of sowing; a monsoon dance whose bamboo clappers mimic catching insects that foretell the harvest; and a post-harvest dance originating in the weaving process, when cotton is collected. Petition, divination and the turn to the next task: three functions, one calendar. Elsewhere a Himachal farmers' dance falls at the February-March harvest, a Bundelkhand dance balances a basket of grain on the head, and a Banjara dance depicts sowing through women's hand gestures.

**Work.** Besides the weaving origin, a Saurashtra women's dance beats the floor with two long wooden sticks joined by a block, and a Tripura dance enacts hunting sequences through gesture and posture.

**Conflict.** Martial dances preserve combat as performance: a Manipur dance named sword and spear, an Odishan dance named battle danced with wooden spears and shields in infantry-like formations, a Naga war dance of mock formations, and a Kerala and Lakshadweep dance with sticks and shields building to a fast climax.

**The objection.** Much of the field is devotional rather than economic - deity embodiment, cobra worship, masked temple dances, and festival dances at Navratri, Onam, Diwali and Chaitra Sankranti.

**The reply.** Those occasions are calendrical and communal, and several are agricultural - an Onam dance celebrates the rich harvest.

Verdict: it holds, if "organise" includes ritual as well as work time.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = the proposition preferred over the identity claim with a reason 2 + a complete agricultural cycle with all three functions 5 + additional agrarian instances across three regions 3 + the work dimension with two named instances 3 + four martial instances with their properties and formations 4 + the devotional objection stated fairly 2 + a reply that absorbs it rather than dismissing it 1.**

# REMEDIATION

## The thirteen errors that cost most, and the correction for each
| # | Error | Why it is made | Correction |
|---:|---|---|---|
| 1 | "Bharatanatyam passed unchanged from ancient temple to modern stage" | the temple-to-stage narrative is the most available story | Name the chain: *Sadir* of the devadasis -> decline of the system -> the form **nearly vanished** -> revival by a named individual -> global recognition by another -> changed performer demography. **Continuity and break together** |
| 2 | "108 basic mudras" | the number attaches itself to the nearest noun | 108 standardly attaches to the ***karanas***; hand-gesture counts are **treatise-specific**. Record the disagreement rather than resolving it silently |
| 3 | "Nagabandha is a Mohiniyattam posture" | both forms are lyrical, female-performed and grace-dominated | **Nagabandha is Manipuri.** Separate the two by veil and *pung* against *Kasavu* and absence of thumping |
| 4 | "There are eight classical dance forms in India" | one list is better known than the other | The **academy** recognises eight; the **Ministry of Culture** recognises **nine, including Chhau**. Name both bodies |
| 5 | "Garba is UNESCO-recognised, hence classical" | recognition is imagined as a single ladder | Two systems, different criteria and different bodies; a form may hold **one, both or neither**. Garba is a **folk** dance with a 2023 inscription |
| 6 | "Chhau is a mask dance" full stop | the label is learned without its exception | **Mayurbhanj Chhau artists do not wear masks.** State the label, then the exception |
| 7 | Naming revivalists without functions | names are easier to memorise than roles | Five functions are distinguished: **formalising, defining a recital, patronising, reviving, bringing recognition**. Assign each name its own |
| 8 | "Sattriya derives from the devadasi tradition" | a shared word is resolved to the more familiar referent | The absorbed tradition is an **Assamese** one of that name, not the Tamil Nadu system. **Check the region before resolving a term** |
| 9 | "Folk dances are simple and untrained" | the source's own comparative wording is read as a verdict | The words describe **transmission and reach**. Answer with the technical evidence: pot-balancing solos, pitcher-balancing with only lower-body movement, bamboo-stepping, seated cymbal dancing |
| 10 | Undated status claims | "latest" feels like a stable fact | Every "latest", "total" or "how many so far" claim is **time-stamped**. State the record with its date and say the answer changes |
| 11 | "The devadasi system declined" with no cause named | the outcome is easier to recall than the mechanism | Name the **anti-nautch** campaign, organised in the Madras Presidency from the **early 1890s**, and run the chain: occasion -> respectability -> dedication -> statute (**1934 Bombay**, **1947 Madras**) -> contraction of transmission |
| 12 | "Siddhendra Yogi, 11th century" | a printed century looks authoritative | Treat it as an **outlier**; standard accounts place him substantially later, most commonly the **17th century**. Write the function, not a date |
| 13 | "Kuchipudi was revived by Balasaraswati" | she appears in a printed Kuchipudi list | The name is canonically **Bharatanatyam**; the credit is **doubtful**. Rest the paragraph on **Lakshminarayan Sastri**, the father of the solo format |

## Drill set A - one-line discriminators

Cover the right-hand column and reconstruct it. Each answer must be a feature that belongs to **exactly one** form.

| Prompt | The discriminating answer |
|---|---|
| The form whose song-text has its own name | **Kathakali** - *Attakkatha*, in *Manipravalam* |
| The form whose repertoire is its founder's own compositions | **Sattriya** - *Borgeets* of Sankardev |
| The form with a competitive item against the drummer | **Kathak** - *Jugalbandi* with the tabla player |
| The form in which the dancer recites the rhythmic syllables aloud | **Kathak** - *Padhant* |
| The form with a narrative item performed without music or chanting | **Kathak** - *Gat Bhav* |
| The form with a brass-plate item | **Kuchipudi** - *Tarangam* |
| The form in which pictures are drawn on the floor with the toes | **Kuchipudi** - *Jala Chitra Nrityam* |
| The form with a character-introduction composition | **Kuchipudi** - *daaru* |
| The form where one dancer plays multiple roles by convention | **Bharatanatyam** - *Ekcharya lasyam* |
| The form defined by an absence of thumping | **Mohiniyattam** |
| The form with forty named basic movements | **Mohiniyattam** - *Atavakul / Atavus* |
| The form with a veiled face and facial expression of lesser importance | **Manipuri** |
| The form whose body posture is a figure of eight | **Manipuri** - *Nagabandha* |
| The form with two recorded ways of concluding | **Odissi** - *Moksha* or *Trikhanda majura* |
| The form called mobile sculpture | **Odissi** |
| The form whose recital order was fixed by four named teachers | **Bharatanatyam** - the Tanjore Quartet |
| The form performed by monks as part of daily ritual | **Sattriya** - *Bhokots* in *sattras* |
| The form that is an all-male troupe performance | **Kathakali** |

## Drill set B - region and carrier

| Prompt | Answer |
|---|---|
| Two classical forms in one state | **Kathakali and Mohiniyattam**, Kerala |
| One tradition, three styles, three states | **Chhau** - Saraikela (Jharkhand), Mayurbhanj (Odisha), Purulia (West Bengal) |
| A named region spanning two states | **Tulu Nadu** - southern Karnataka and northern Kerala; Buta Kola, Nagaradhane, Aati Kalenja |
| A dance shared by a state and a union territory | **Kolkali-Parichakali** - Kerala and Lakshadweep |
| A harvest dance across three states | **Jhumair** - West Bengal, Jharkhand, Odisha |
| The community that performs the Kashmir dance with a tall conical hat | **Watal** - Dumhal |
| The community that performs the July-August Tulu nature-spirit art | **Nalike** - Aati Kalenja |
| The tribe of the Bastar bison dance | **Muria** - Gaur Muria |
| The tribe of the Bundelkhand peacock dance | **Bedia** - Rai Nritya |
| The tribe of the seated Rajasthani cymbal dance | **Kamar** - Tera Tali |
| The community of the Tripura post-harvest weaving dance | **Keipeng** - Meladom |

## Drill set C - chronology, stated safely

| Prompt | The safe formulation |
|---|---|
| Date of the *Natyashastra* | a **layered** text conventionally attributed to Bharata, broadly dated across the late centuries BCE and early centuries CE; commonly printed as 200 BC - 200 AD |
| Date of the *Abhinaya Darpana* | attributed to **Nandikeshvara**; printed as the 5th-4th century BC; quote the range, do not build a chronology on it |
| Age of a classical form | give the **recorded chain** - origin association, disruption, substitution or revival, recognition - never a single number of years |
| When the classical dances were revived | across the **nineteenth century**, the **1930s** and the **mid-twentieth century**, with an **eighteenth-century** royal attempt; not "after independence" |
| Year of Sattriya's recognition | **reported** as 2000; prefer the unchallengeable point that the list has been added to within living memory |
| Latest international inclusion | a **date-stamped** question; state the record with its date and note that the answer changes |

## Drill set D - the "do not supply" list

Say, aloud, that this session records **none** of the following, and that none may be supplied in an answer:

1. An award, title, decoration or honour for any dancer, guru, revivalist, composer or patron named in this topic.2. A symbolic meaning for any costume colour or material, except the Kathakali make-up colour code, which is recorded in full.
3. A symbolic meaning for any gesture except the three joined fingers of *Katakamukha Hasta*.
4. An elemental association for Kathak, Manipuri or Sattriya; five are recorded and three are not.
5. A mapping of the five *vesham* names onto the six recorded colour meanings.
6. A fifth Kathak gharana, a fourth Chhau style, a third Sattriya modern stream or a sixth elemental association.
7. Any *bani* or stylistic-school name for Bharatanatyam or any other form.
8. A founding date, location or governance record for any training institution.
9. A living-status, biography or dates for any proponent, except where this session records them.
10. Any total, ranking or count of Indian elements on the international safeguarding list beyond the single dated record stated in Lesson 8.

## Map 2 - The causal chain from treatise to canon

```text
   COMPOSITE-ART CONCEPTION
   four contributions: words + gestures + music + emotions
   dance called a complete art uniting music, sculpture, poetry, drama
                              |
                              v
   A STATED PURPOSE: produce one of NINE named emotional effects, reliably
                              |
                              v

   (sorrow/compassion; horror; wonder)
                              |
                              v
   CODIFICATION AT THREE SCALES
   gesture (hasta / mudra) -> movement unit (adavu / atavu) ->
   whole-body unit (karana) -> named signature postures
                              |
                              v
   A TEACHABLE ART -> guru-shishya parampara carrying plural sampradayas
                              |
        +---------------------+---------------------+
        |                                           |
   REGIONAL DIFFERENTIATION                  A RECONSTRUCTIBLE ART
   eight forms, one analytical               (this becomes decisive later)
   vocabulary, different emphases
        |                                           |
        v                                           |
   PATRONAGE HISTORIES                              |
   temple | court | feudal lords | monastery |      |
   village troupe | Vaishnava reorganisation        |
        |                                           |
        v                                           |
   THREE DECLINE MECHANISMS                         |
   patron class collapse | carrier community        |
   dismantled | access rule changed                 |
        |                                           |
        +---------------------+---------------------+
                              |
                              v
   RECONSTRUCTION: method from codification, CONTENT from the displaced
                              |
                              v
   INSTITUTIONS: schools, a national academy, a ministry, an international
   safeguarding instrument
                              |
                              v
   A CANON - eight on one list, nine on another, and one form with every
   credential and no recognition at all
```

## Map 3 - The argument about recognition, as a tree

```text
   CLAIM: "Classical" is an institutional decision, not a property of a dance
        |
        +-- SUPPORT 1: two organs of the same government count differently
        |      academy 8 | ministry 9, the difference being a mask-and-
        |      martial tradition of three states
        |
        +-- SUPPORT 2: a form with the full evidential profile holds no status
        |      claimed treatise roots + a named historical reviver +
        |      temple sculptures + a named modern reconstruction + NO
        |      recognition
        |
        +-- SUPPORT 3: the list has been added to within living memory
        |      (one form's admission is reported for the year 2000)
        |
        +-- SUPPORT 4: a differently constituted international instrument
        |      produces an entirely different set, whose Indian dance-related
        |      records are a mask tradition (2010), a community folk
        |      tradition (2010) and a mass festival dance (2023) - and whose
        |      latest Indian element (10 Dec 2025) is not a dance at all
        |
        +-- OBJECTION: the recognised forms really are differently organised
        |      named recital orders, treatise-derived gesture systems,
        |      guru-transmitted sampradayas, strictly laid-down rules
        |
        +-- REPLY: granted, and this is a difference of MODE, not of LEVEL.
        |      The decision certifies the mode; it does not create it, and it
        |      does not track antiquity, continuity, difficulty or worth
        |
        v
   QUALIFIED VERDICT: "classical" names a mode of organisation certified by a
   dated institutional decision - precise when used for that, and empty when
   used as a claim about age, continuity, difficulty or worth
```

# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

## Treatise foundation and the composite-art conception

- **Natya Veda** created as the **fifth Veda** by combining: **Paathya** (words) from **Rig**, **Abhinaya** (gestures) from **Yajur**, **Geet** (music) from **Sama**, **Rasa** (emotions) from **Atharva**. **Natya** = an amalgamation of dance, drama and music.
- The **first formal mention of dance** is in Bharata's ***Natya Shastra***; it describes **techniques, postures, emotions, stagecraft and audiences** and calls dance a **"complete art" uniting music, sculpture, poetry and drama**.
- Dance in the Indian tradition is **both sacred and social**.
- **Mohenjo-daro bronze figure, c. 2500 BCE** - indicates **early familiarity with dance gestures and body movements**: an idiom, not a form.
- Dating: *Natya Shastra* printed as **200 BC - 200 AD**, treated as **layered**; *Abhinaya Darpana* attributed to **Nandikeshvara**, printed as the **5th-4th century BC**. Quote ranges; never a single year.
- Three treatises, never merged: ***Natya Shastra*** (conception, aspects, whole-body units); ***Abhinaya Darpana*** (the three elements, *Nayaka-Nayika bhava*); ***Hastalakshana Deepika*** (Kathakali's hand-gesture language).

## Aspects, elements and the emotional apparatus

- **Lasya** - grace; denotes **bhava, rasa, abhinaya**; symbolic of the **feminine** features. **Tandava** - symbolic of the **male** aspects; emphasis on **rhythm and movement**. Qualities in varying emphasis, **not** a gender-exclusive performance rule.
- Balances: Bharatanatyam **equal**; Kuchipudi **both important**; Mohiniyattam **Lasya dominant**; Manipuri **both, Lasya emphasised**; Sattriya **both combined**.
- **Nritta** = rhythmic steps **devoid of expression or mood**. **Natya** = **dramatic representation**; the story elaborated through the recital. **Nritya** = **sentiment and emotions**, including **mime** and methods of expression, of which *mudras* are principal.
- **Nayaka-Nayika Bhav**: deity = **Nayaka** (hero); devotee-dancer = **Nayika** (heroine).
- **Nine rasas**: **Shringara** love, **Roudra** anger, **Bibhatsa** disgust, **Veera** heroism, **Shanta** peace and tranquility, **Haasya** laughter and comedy, **Karuna** sorrow and compassion, **Bhayanaka** horror, **Adbhuta** wonder.
- Chain: **bhava -> abhinaya -> rasa**. *Abhinaya* runs through separable **channels** - hand gesture, facial expression, eye and eyebrow movement, posture - and a form is partly defined by which channels it opens and closes.

## Body vocabulary and the disputed number

- **Mudra** = **a combination of hand gestures and body postures**; combinations depict a particular emotion. **Hasta** = the codified hand gesture.
- **Adavu** (steps) | **Hastamudra** | **Bhavabhinaya** - Bharatanatyam's named triad. **Atavakul / Atavus = 40 basic movements** - Mohiniyattam.
- **Karana** = coordinated unit of **whole-body** movement. **108 standardly attaches to karanas**; some preparation material prints "108 fundamental mudras". **Record the disagreement.** The *Abhinaya Darpana* total of 28 single + 23 combined hand gestures is **reported, not verified here**.
- **Katakamukha Hasta** - three fingers joined, symbolising 'Om' (Bharatanatyam).
- Signature postures: **tribhanga** (three-bend, innate to Odissi, used extensively by the **Amaravati sculptors**); **Chowk** (hands spread out, masculinity, Odissi); **Nagabandha** (figure of eight, **Manipuri**); the Bharatanatyam stance of **bent knees with weight evenly distributed**.

## Performance architecture

- Shared shape: **stage preparation -> invocation -> interleaved elements -> conclusion**.
- **Bharatanatyam**: Alarippu (respect to God, teacher, audience; seeking blessings) -> Jatiswaram (Nritta) -> Shabdam (dramatic, expressed words) -> Varnam (Nritya, the main part; tala + raag) -> Padam (abhinaya mastery) -> Jawali (short love-lyrics, faster tempo) -> Thillana (pure Nritta, exuberant, intricate rhythm). Opens with worship of **Ganapati and Nataraja**.
- **Odissi**: Mangalacharan (flower offered to Mother Earth) -> Batu nritya (Tribhanga and Chowk) -> Pallavi (facial expression, representation of the song) -> Tharijham (pure dance) -> **Moksha** (liberation) **or Trikhanda majura** (leave taken from the gods, the audience and the stage).
- **Kathak**: Ananda -> Thaat -> Todas and Tukdas -> **Jugalbandi** (competitive play with the tabla player) -> **Padhant** (reciting *bols* and demonstrating them) -> Tarana -> Kramalaya; plus **Gat Bhav** (dance without music or chanting).
- **Kathakali**: **Kelikottu** (calling the audience's attention) -> **Todayam** (invoking the blessings of the Gods) -> the drama; **dawn with continuous *chenda* and *maddala* marks both beginning and end**.
- Devices: **daaru** (Kuchipudi character introduction); **Ekcharya lasyam** (one dancer, many roles); **Tarangam** and **Jala Chitra Nrityam** (Kuchipudi solo items); **Manduka Shabdam** (frog story).
- Staging: Kathakali - **minimal props**, elaborate make-up and headgear, **open-air theatres with coarse mats** or temple premises, **brass lamp**.
- Organisation is **historical**: Bharatanatyam solo female -> also men and groups; Kuchipudi team -> also solo with female dancers; Kathakali all-male troupe; Sattriya monastic group; Mohiniyattam essentially solo.

## Transmission and the carriers

- Principle: **true transfer of knowledge only through a guru**, who passes on **sampradayas**; **guru-shishya parampara** is the core.
- **Gharana** is a **Kathak** structure here, arising from its Hindustani musical basis: **Lucknow** (peak under **Wajid Ali Shah**; expression and grace); **Jaipur** (initiated by **Bhanuji**; fluency, speed, long rhythmic patterns); **Raigarh** (**Raja Chakradhar Singh**; percussion); **Banaras** (**Janakiprasad**; floor use and symmetry).
- **No *bani* or school names are recorded in this session's sources.** Do not supply them.
- **Sattras** = Vaishnava monasteries; **Bhokots** = the male monks; **Borgeets** = Sankardev's compositions. The only carrier in the topic that **recruits** rather than inherits.
- **Substitution**: *maharis* (patronised by the Jain king **Kharavela**) -> defunct with Vaishnavism -> **gotipuas** ('single boy', from the Mahari tradition **c. 16th century**, acrobatic and yogic, **precursor to classical Odissi**) -> **Nartala** in parallel at the royal courts.
- **Community carriers** in the living field: Bodo, Muria, Yadav, Rathwa, Watal, Nalike, Chakyar, Bedia, Bhil, Kalbelia, Kamar, Banjara, Tripuri, Keipeng, the Tulu people, tribal Sikhs, the Vaishnava community of Ganjam, the tribes of the Chota Nagpur Plateau.

## Recognition, status and safeguarding

- **Sangeet Natak Akademi: eight** - Bharatanatyam, Kuchipudi, Kathakali, Mohiniyattam, Odissi, Manipuri, Kathak, Sattriya. **Ministry of Culture: nine, including Chhau.** Do not merge.
- **Sattriya's recognition year is reported as 2000**; prefer the point that the list has been added to within living memory.
- Dance-related Indian ICH records owned here: **Chhau 2010**; **Kalbelia folk songs and dances 2010**; **Garba of Gujarat 2023** (eighteenth session, **Kasane, Botswana**).
- 📰 **Deepavali inscribed 10 December 2025**, twentieth session, **held in New Delhi**; India's **sixteenth** element; domain **social practices, rituals and festive events**; dossier prepared by the **Sangeet Natak Akademi** under the **Ministry of Culture**. The instrument is **not** a dance canon.
- **Gaudiya Nritya**: name from **Gaur**; *believed* to have **Natya Shastra** roots; *said* to have been revived by **Chaitanya Mahaprabhu**; sculptures *noted* on Bengal temple walls; **reconstructed by Mahua Mukherjee**; **not recognised** by the academy.
- A form may hold **one, both or neither** recognition; neither is evidence about antiquity.

## The eight forms - compressed identification

- **Bharatanatyam** (TN): from *Sadir* / *Dashiattam*; **Tanjore Quartet** (Chinnaiah, Ponniah, Vadivelu, Sivanandam) fixed the recital, **early 19th c.**; also **Tanjore Natyam**; **'fire dance'**; Adavu, Hastamudra, Bhavabhinaya; Katakamukha Hasta; *Ekcharya lasyam*; Kanchipuram and Benarasi silk; mridangam, veena, flute; revived by **E. Krishna Iyer (1897-1968)**, global recognition by **Rukmini Devi Arundale**.
- **Kuchipudi** (AP): village **Kuchelapuram**; **Kusselavas**; **Siddhendra Yogi** credited with formalisation and *Bhama Kalapam* (⚠️ century **contested** - printed "11th c." is an outlier against accounts placing him substantially later, most commonly the **17th**), Yakshagana-style shaping; Vaishnavism -> **male Brahmin monopoly**, **Bhagavathalus**, Bhagavata Purana themes with a **secular theme** and **Shringara predominance**; **Vijayanagara and Golconda** patronage; village-bound till the 20th c.; **Lakshminarayan Sastri** introduced the solo format and female dancers (⚠️ printed revival credit to **Balasaraswati** is **doubtful** - canonically a Bharatanatyam figure; **Ragini Devi** hedged); *daaru*; Sollakath/Patakshara (Nritta) and Kavutvams (Nritya, acrobatics); *Manduka Shabdam*, *Tarangam*, *Jala Chitra Nrityam*; **earth**; Carnatic with violin and mridangam; **Telugu**; *Bagalbandi* and Benarasi.
- **Kathakali** (KL): from **Ramanattam and Krishnattam** under **feudal lords**; ***Katha*** + ***Kali***; related to **Koodiyattam** and **martial art performance**; **all-male troupe**; five *vesham* - **Pacha, Kathi, Thadi, Kari, Minukku**; colours: **green** nobility/divinity/virtue, **red patches beside the nose** royalty, **black** evil and wickedness, **yellow** saints and women, **completely red face** evil, **white beard** higher consciousness and divinity; **'Ballad of the East'**; *Attakkatha* in **Manipravalam**; **Navarasas** through eye and eyebrow; **Hastalakshana Deepika**; open-air, coarse mats, **brass lamp**; *chenda* and *maddala* at dawn; **sky/ether**; feudal breakdown -> **1930s revival under Mukunda Raja**.
- **Mohiniyattam** (KL): **Mohini** + **attam**; solo female; narrates the **feminine dance of Vishnu**; **Vadivelu**, 19th c.; **Travancore**, **Swathi Thirunal**; obscurity -> revived by **V. N. Menon with Kalyani Amma**; **grace of Bharatanatyam + vigour of Kathakali**; **marked absence of thumping**; **Lasya dominant**; **40 Atavakul/Atavus**; **Manipravalam**; white/off-white **Kasavu** with gold brocade, **no elaborate facial make-up**, **ghungroo**; cymbals, veena, drums, flute; **air**.
- **Kathak** (UP): from the ***Ras Leela* of Brajbhoomi**; name from ***Kathika*** storytellers; Mughal era - **court branch** and **Persian** costume and movement influence, described in the source with an explicit moral verdict that must be separated from the record; revived by **Lady Leela Sokhey**; four gharanas; **intricate footwork and pirouettes**; eight recital elements; **dhrupad** with taranas, thumris and ghazals from the Mughal period; **Birju Maharaj, Lacchu Maharaj, Sitara Devi, Damayanti Joshi**.
- **Odissi** (OD): **Udayagiri-Khandagiri**; ***Odra nritya*** in the *Natya Shastra*; **maharis** under **Kharavela**; Vaishnavism -> defunct -> **gotipuas**; **Nartala** at courts; **Charles Fabri and Indrani Rahman**, mid-20th c. international acclaim; **tribhanga** and **Chowk**; static lower body, moving torso; **'mobile sculpture'**; five-part recital with **two conclusions**; **Hindustani** music with manjira, pakhawaj, sitar, flute; ***Gita Govinda*** of **Jayadeva**; **Bomkai and Sambalpuri** sarees, silver jewellery; **water**; proponents include **Sharon Lowen (USA)** and **Anandini Dasi (Argentina)**.
- **Manipuri** (MN): **Shiva and Parvati** with local **Gandharvas**; **Vaishnavism, 15th c.**, Krishna central; **Raja Bhag Chandra**, 18th c.; **Tagore** at **Santiniketan**; **devotion, not sensuality**; **thin veil**, facial expression of **lesser importance**; **Nagabandha**; **Ras Leela**; ***pung***, flute, **khartals**, dhols; **Jayadeva and Chandidas**; **Thang-ta and Sankirtana** influenced by it; **Jhaveri sisters (Nayana, Suverna, Ranjana, Darshana)**, **Guru Bipin Singha**, **N Madhabi Devi**.
- **Sattriya** (AS): **Sankardev**, **15th c. CE**, **in modern form**; **sattras**; mentioned in the *Natya Shastra*; **Bhakti** inspired; amalgamation of **Ojapali** and an Assamese **Devadasi** tradition; Vishnu narratives; all three elements; **Bhokots**, **daily rituals**; **khol, manjira, flute**; **Borgeets**; **strictly laid down rules**; Dhoti and **Paguri**, **Ghuri** and **Chador** in **Pat silk**, waist cloth for both; two streams - **Gayan-Bhayanar Nach** and **Kharmanar Nach**; ***Ankia Naat*** in ***Brajavali*** (Assamese + Maithili) and ***Bhaona***.

## The classification frontier

- **Chhau**: from ***Chhaya***, shadow; **mask dance**, **vigorous martial movements**, mythological stories; natural themes ***Sarpa Nritya*** and ***Mayura Nritya***; **Saraikela (JH), Mayurbhanj (OD - no masks), Purulia (WB)**; **UNESCO 2010**; the **ninth** form on the ministry's list.
- **Gaudiya Nritya**: everything except the decision - see the recognition notes above.

## The living field, by function

- **Ritual embodiment**: Theyyam (KL, KA - performers **embody deities and spirits**); Buta Kola, Nagaradhane (*Aashleshabali*, *Nagamandala*), Aati Kalenja (**Nalike**, month of **Aati**, the spirit **Kalenja**) - all Tulu Nadu; Padayani (KL, Bhagavati temples, **Kali**, masks called ***kolams***, Bhairavi/Kalan/Yakshi/Pakshi); Gambhira (WB, **Malda**, **Chaitra Sankranti**, **neem and fig** masks, shakti and shaiva); Ghanta Mrudangam (OD, **Ganjam**, Radha-Krishna-Jagannath); Danda Jatra (OD, **oldest folk arts**, Shiva, theme **social harmony and brotherhood**); Chakyar Koothu (KL, **Chakyar**, snake costume, Malayalam prose and poetry); Dalkhai (OD, Dussehra, girlfriend-word at the start and end of each stanza); Garadi (PY, **Vanaras**, **10 Anjalis** on each leg); Singhi Chham (SK, **snow lion**, **Kanchenjunga**); Raut Nacha (CG, **Yadav**, Diwali); Alkap (JH-WB, **Gajan**); Karma Naach (**Karma** festival, Chota Nagpur); Tarangamel (GA, Dussehra and Holi, flags and streamers); Charba (HP, Dussehra); Rathwa ni Gher (GJ, **Rathwa**, **Kavant**); Pata Kunitha (KA, ribboned **pata** poles, **attracts all religions**, variant **Puja Kunitha**); Hulivesha (KA, tiger, **Durga**, Navaratri); Hojagiri (TR, **Lakshmi Puja**).
- **Martial and hunting**: **Thang-Ta** (MN, sword + spear); **Paika** (JH-OD, = **battle**, spears and shields, **infantry-like formations**); **Rangma** (NL, war dance); **Kolkali-Parichakali** (KL-LD, sticks and shields, slow to fast climax); **Perini** (TG, **Shiva**, **Ramappa Temple, Warangal**, **'Dance of Warriors'**, **Kakatiya**); **Mosak Sumani** (TR, hunting); **Padayani** (KL); **Chhau**.
- **Agrarian and seasonal**: **Garia** (TR, **sowing**, **April**) -> **Lebang Boomani** (TR, **monsoon**, bamboo clappers, ***lebang*** insects foretelling the harvest) -> **Meladom** (TR, **post-harvest**, **weaving**, **Keipeng**); **Phag** (HP, **Feb-March**); **Jhumair** (WB-JH-OD, **Janani** and **Mardana**, **Bhimbetka** resemblance); **Jhoomar** (PB, harvest, circle, **Baluchistan** traders); **Jawara** (MP, **jawar** basket); **Lambadi** (AP, **Banjara**, sowing and planting gestures); **Kaikottikali** (KL, **Onam**, with *Airukali* and *Tattamakali*); **Bagurumba** (AS, **Bodo**, *sifung, kham, serja*); **Bihu** (AS).
- **Property-led**: **Kolattam** (AP, **8-40**); **Dandiya Raas** (GJ, Navratri, **Raas Garba**); **Cheraw** (MZ, bamboo, **likely foreign origin**); **Pata Kunitha** (KA); **Tippani** (GJ, **Saurashtra**, **exclusively women**, **two sticks joined by a block**); **Matki** (MP, **Malwa**, weddings, solo, pots; **Aada**, **Khada Nach**); **Hojagiri** (TR, **4-6**, **lower half only**, pitchers); **Jawara** (MP); **Tera Tali** (RJ, **Kamar**, seated, **cymbals tied to the body**); **Garba** (**Garbha deep**).
- **Formation-led**: **Garba**; **Ghoomar / Gangore** (RJ, **Bhil**, pirouetting, **Ghaghra**); **Fugdi** (Konkan, circles or rows, many sub-types); **Kummi** (TN-KL, **clapping instead of instruments**, **Pongal**, variants **Kolattam** and **Pinnal Kolattam**); **Jhoomar**; **Jhora** (UK, Kumaoni, joy, unity, community bonding); **Karma Naach**; **Bihu**; **Rangma**; **Paika**.
- **Impersonation-led**: **Hulivesha** (tiger); **Gaur Muria** (CG, **Muria**, **Bastar**, bison); **Mayilattam** (TN-KL, peacock; with **Kaalai** bull, **Karadi** bear, **Aali** demon, **Paampu** snake **Attam**); **Rai Nritya** (MP, **Bedia**, peacock dance of the women); **Singhi Chham**; **Kalbelia** (RJ, serpent movement, ***Been***, **UNESCO 2010**); **Chakyar Koothu**; **Butta Bommalu** (AP, **basket toys**, **West Godavari**, **non-verbal music**); **Dumhal** (JK, **Watal**, tall conical hat).
- **Life-cycle and social**: **Domkach** (BR, marriage rituals); **Jat-Jatin** (BR, **Mithila**, love and quarrel of a married couple); **Biraha / Bidesia** (BR, women's pain at separation, **practised solely by males**); **Lavani** (MH, from ***Lavanya***, **Peshwa era, 18th c.**, ***dholki***, **nine-yard Nauvari** saree); **Dadra** (UP, **semi-classical**, **Lucknow courtesans**); **Bhangra / Giddha** (PB); **Jhora**; **Tarangamel**; **Gotipua** (OD).

## Cross-art evidence and boundaries

- **Sculpture**: dancers at **Khajuraho, Konark, Ellora and Chidambaram** depict poses from named classical forms; **tribhanga** shared with the **Amaravati sculptors**; **Udayagiri-Khandagiri** for Odissi; **Perini** at the **Ramappa Temple** sanctum; **Gaudiya Nritya** on Bengal temple walls. **Limit: idiom, not choreography** - argued from geography and from carrier discontinuity.
- **Music**: instruments and systems identify forms; raga and tala theory, the two systems as systems, the musical lineage-schools and *Sankirtana* as a practice are routed out.
- **Theatre and puppetry**: classify by **dominant grammar**. Owned: Kathakali, Kuchipudi, *Ankia Naat*, *Bhaona*, Chhau. Named and routed out: **Koodiyattam** (Sanskrit drama) and **Yakshagana** (folk-drama style). Puppetry test: **performer inside the figure = dance**.
- **Society**: the reform debate and the devadasi system as social history are routed out; the community is named and the consequence for the art is recorded.
- **Heritage policy**: award administration, institutional governance and changing totals are routed out; exactly one dated current record is stated.

## Reform, revival and the verdict

- Three decline mechanisms: **patron class collapse** (feudal breakdown); **carrier community dismantled** (devadasi decline; **nearly vanished**); **access rule changed by a religious movement** (Vaishnavism - **defunct** in Odisha, **monopoly** in Andhra, **prominence** in Manipur, **institutional creation** in Assam).
- Functions to distinguish: **formalising, defining a recital, patronising, reviving, bringing recognition, reconstructing**.
- Reconstruction: **codification supplied the method; hereditary practitioners supplied the content.**
- **Verdict:** twentieth-century revival **saved the repertoire and relocated its practitioners**.
- **Thesis:** the eight classical forms as we know them are the outcome of **twentieth-century reconstruction and institutional recognition acting on much older regional practices** - continuity is real, but so is the break.

## Answer-writing spine

- **Identify**: discriminating feature -> region and origin -> repertoire item or posture -> costume or instrument -> nearest rival excluded.
- **Compare**: shared grammar in one line -> origin and carrier -> repertoire -> technique -> accompaniment and language -> costume -> the one unshareable feature.
- **Revival**: three mechanisms -> named actors with functions -> what reconstruction rested on -> the institutional layer -> the two-part verdict.
- **Folk and classical**: administrative boundary -> technical parity -> transmission on both sides -> two-way traffic -> the instrument that ignores the hierarchy -> organisation, not worth.
- **Sculpture and dance**: licence -> five records -> what is established -> geography and carrier limits -> the strongest counter-case admitted -> idiom, not repertoire.
- **Recognition**: who is speaking -> what is being classified -> when -> the eight-versus-nine disagreement -> the non-recognised case -> the dated record -> revisability.

## Bounded-research declaration

This session was generated under the bounded-research rule. The substantive base is the complete core owner, the complete enrichment owner and one core OCR art and performance source. External work was confined to three declared purposes.

**(a) PYQ verification and routing.** The routing ledgers and the locally held official papers named in the manifest above.

**(b) One bounded material-fact pass.** The list of items was fixed before searching and is reproduced here in full, with the outcome of each.

| Item | Why it was on the list | Outcome |
|---|---|---|
| The inscription year and session for Garba of Gujarat | the core source records a nomination year; a general search returned a conflicting year, and the topic's recognition control depends on the date being right | **Resolved.** Inscribed in **2023**, at the **eighteenth session** of the Intergovernmental Committee, held at **Kasane, Botswana**; the decision reference for that inscription is recorded in the committee's published decisions |
| Whether the number 108 attaches to *karanas* or to *mudras* | the core owner and the core art source disagree, and the number is among the most frequently tested in the topic | **Resolved as a disagreement, not as a fact.** Standard treatment of the *Natyashastra* attaches 108 to the ***karanas***, codified in the treatise's chapter on the characteristics of Tandava; hand-gesture counts are treatise-specific. The session records both formulations and the safe wording |
| The hand-gesture total in the *Abhinaya Darpana* | it is widely quoted and would have filled an obvious gap | **Not resolved to a verifiable standard.** The commonly given total of 28 single and 23 combined gestures was not confirmed against a primary source within the bounded pass. It is recorded in the session as **reported, not verified**, and no claim in the session depends on it |
| The year in which Sattriya was added to the national academy's list of classical dance forms | its omission would leave a real gap in the recognition lesson, and the year is commonly examined | **Not resolved to a verifiable standard.** Widely reported as **2000**; the bounded pass did not reach a primary academy record. Recorded in the session as **reported**, with the analytically safer alternative formulation supplied, and no claim depends on it |
| Whether any Indian **dance** element was inscribed on the international list after 2023 | the topic's status control requires the current position | **Resolved in the negative.** The element inscribed at the most recent session is **not** a dance form. This result is what makes the current linkage genuinely useful to the topic rather than decorative |
| The century attributed to **Siddhendra Yogi** | the core art source prints "11th century", which is a strong and frequently repeated claim, and the topic's chronology discipline depends on it | **Resolved as an outlier.** Standard accounts of Kuchipudi place the systematisation attributed to him **substantially later**, most commonly in the **seventeenth century**, with a minority placing him in the **fourteenth**; the eleventh-century attribution is not supported in critical treatments, and every account describes the historical record for the figure as thin. The session demotes the printed century, states the standard placement as a range, and removes the entry from the fixed points of the date spine |
| Whether **Balasaraswati** can be taught as a reviver of **Kuchipudi** | the core art source names her in that role, and the name is canonically associated with a different form | **Resolved as doubtful.** The name is canonically associated with **Bharatanatyam**; no independent corroboration of a Kuchipudi revival role was reached within the bounded pass. The session marks the attribution as contested wherever it appears, removes it from the teaching, and rests the twentieth-century paragraph on the securely attributed father of the solo format. **Ragini Devi** is retained only as a hedged attribution |
| The **anti-nautch** campaign: term, period and the statutory endpoint | the second decline mechanism was stated as an outcome with no named cause, and three places in the session referred to a campaign the session never taught | **Resolved to a bounded finite set.** The campaign is recorded as taking organised form in the **Madras Presidency from the early 1890s**; **Muthulakshmi Reddy**, first woman member of the **Madras Legislative Council (1926)**, moved an anti-dedication resolution in **1927**; dedication was prohibited by the **Bombay Devadasis Protection Act, 1934** and the **Madras Devadasis (Prevention of Dedication) Act, 1947**. Lesson 22 teaches the term and a five-step mechanism within the dance boundary and routes the reform debate itself to society and modern history. Nothing beyond this finite set is asserted |
| Whether any Indian intangible-heritage element on the international list is **dance-inclusive** without being a dance form | the session's declared gap had been worded as though the remaining Indian elements were simply "non-dance" | **Resolved.** ***Sankirtana*, ritual singing, drumming and dancing of Manipur, 2013** and ***Mudiyettu*, ritual theatre and dance drama of Kerala, 2010** both name danced performance; **Ramman, 2009**, is masked ritual performance. The session corrects the wording in Lesson 8 and in the declared gaps, routes *Sankirtana* to its existing appearance in Lesson 16 with the musical treatment left to the music topic, and **discloses the absence of *Mudiyettu* as a genuine gap** |

**(c) One bounded current linkage.** Exactly one is claimed, file-wide, in Lesson 8, and it is the Deepavali inscription record above. Every other pre-teach checklist in this session records the absence of a genuine linkage truthfully, and no current claim appears anywhere else in the file.

## Declared gaps - material a candidate may expect that this session does not supply

1. **No *bani* or stylistic-school names** for Bharatanatyam or any other form. The sources used do not record them; the structural point about plural *sampradayas* is supplied instead. Stated openly in Lesson 9.
2. **No elemental association** for Kathak, Manipuri or Sattriya. Five are recorded and three are not.
3. **No mapping of the five *vesham* names onto the six recorded colour meanings.** Both lists are given; the mapping is not in the source and is not invented.
4. **No founding record** - date, location or governance - for any training institution, including the one whose name appears as a prefix in recorded performer names. Routed to the heritage and institutions owner.
5. **No awards, honours or living-status information** for any named dancer, guru, revivalist, composer or patron.
6. **Inscription years for Indian intangible-heritage elements outside this topic's dance boundary are not supplied, and no running total is given.** ⚠️ This gap must not be stated as "non-dance elements", because the Indian list contains elements in which **dance is constitutive rather than absent**. Two are directly relevant. ***Sankirtana*, ritual singing, drumming and dancing of Manipur, inscribed in 2013** - dance is named in the element's own title, and this session already routes the practice: Lesson 16 records *Sankirtana* among the traditions influenced by Manipuri dance, while its treatment as a musical and devotional practice belongs to the music topic. ***Mudiyettu*, ritual theatre and dance drama of Kerala, inscribed in 2010** - **this session teaches nothing about it, and that is a genuine gap**; it is a Kerala ritual dance-drama that a complete treatment of this topic's classification frontier would have to place beside Theyyam, Padayani and Kathakali's parent traditions. A third, **Ramman, the religious festival and ritual theatre of the Garhwal Himalayas, inscribed in 2009**, is masked ritual performance and is similarly untreated here. The correct statement of the gap is therefore: **this session carries the three dance-related records it owns - Chhau 2010, Kalbelia 2010 and Garba 2023 - plus the one current record of Lesson 8; it does not carry the dance-inclusive ritual-performance elements just named, nor any element total.** Full status coverage belongs to the heritage, conservation and institutions topic, with its own verification date.
7. **No treatment of the 2026 Preliminary item on a Bagh Caves painting.** This session holds no record of that painting and supplies nothing towards the item.
8. **No inscriptional evidence for the Tandava aspect.** The 2013 Main Examination question asks about inscriptions; this session holds sculptural evidence and states the limit rather than supplying an inscription.
9. **Three of the five names in a 1985 Main Examination question** have no entry in the sources used, and no regional attribution is supplied for them.
10. **Two of the five traditions in a 1983 Main Examination question** are musical and devotional traditions belonging to the music topic, and are routed rather than improvised.
11. **No treatment of *Mudiyettu*, the Kerala ritual theatre and dance drama inscribed in 2010.** It is named in gap 6 above and in Lesson 8 so that the learner knows it exists and that this session does not cover it; it belongs beside Theyyam and Padayani in a complete treatment of Kerala ritual performance and is not taught here.
12. **No treatment of *Ramman*, the Garhwal masked ritual performance inscribed in 2009.** Named only, for the same reason.
13. **No secure century for Siddhendra Yogi.** The printed eleventh-century attribution is demoted as an outlier and the standard later placement is given only as a range; no single date is supplied.
14. **No usable revival attribution for Kuchipudi beyond the father of the solo format.** The printed Balasaraswati credit is marked doubtful and Ragini Devi is hedged, so the twentieth-century Kuchipudi paragraph rests on one securely attributed name.

## Source-use statement

Canonical Markdown supplied the topic's ownership boundaries, the trap set, the answer architecture, the directive and demand map, the identification discriminators and the factual-risk controls. The enrichment owner supplied the recognition-versus-practice analytical frame, the revival-and-reconfiguration argument and the shared-root-text licence for cross-art reading. The core OCR art and performance source supplied the full paragraph-level record for all eight classical forms, the Chhau and Gaudiya Nritya boxes, the complete folk table and the two reproduced question lists, and it discharged the enrichment owner's own open caution about six of the eight forms. The PYQ ledgers and locally held official papers supplied ownership, exact wording and key status. The single current linkage supplied one dated status record.

No artifact under the permanently excluded package directory was read, searched, cited or relied upon at any stage of this generation, and no learner-v2 artifact was used. Qdrant was not required and did not block or delay this session.
